Generalized Bessel-Dunkl diffusions
Jacek Małecki

Abstract
. We develop a general theory of Bessel-Dunkl type diffusions in Weyl chambers associated with classical root systems. The class considered here allows time-dependent and configuration-dependent diffusion and drift coefficients, as well as state-dependent singular repulsion coefficients which may vanish on the walls of the chamber. This includes, in a unified framework, Dyson-type logarithmic particle systems, radial Dunkl processes, squared Bessel and Wishart particle systems, and non-colliding diffusion models. Using the geometry of the underlying root system and a symmetric-polynomial approach, we establish weak existence in both the strictly positive repulsion and the degenerate regimes. In the strictly positive regime, we prove that positive repulsion prevents positive-time multiple collisions. In the degenerate case, we identify geometric conditions which exclude boundary sticking and allow one to recover the genuine singular equation from its interior form. We also prove non-explosion under a radial linear-growth condition, obtain pathwise uniqueness and strong existence in the non-sticky class under natural Yamada-Watanabe type or locally Lipschitz assumptions, and establish a mean-field convergence theorem for systems of types A N − 1 A_{N-1} , B N B_{N} , and D N D_{N} . Together, these results build the structural foundations for a systematic theory of generalized Bessel-Dunkl diffusions with non-constant and possibly degenerate coefficients.



2020 Mathematics Subject Classification
Primary 60H10; Secondary 60J60, 60K35, 60B20, 33C52

1.
Introduction

We consider the Euclidean space
\mathbb{R}^{N}
, where
N\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
, equipped with
the usual inner product and the corresponding Euclidean norm

\left<x,y\right>=\sum_{k=1}^{N}x_{k}y_{k},\qquad|x|^{2}=\left<x,x\right>,\qquad x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{N}.

Let
R
denote the irreducible finite root system in
\mathbb{R}^{N}
, with chosen subsystem of positive roots
R_{+}
, positive Weyl chamber
C_{+}
, and closed Weyl chamber
\overline{C_{+}}
. The paper is devoted to the study of existence, uniqueness, boundary behaviour, non-explosion, and mean-field limits for chamber-valued solutions
X=(X_{1},\ldots,X_{N})
of the singular stochastic differential system

(1.1)

dX_{k}(t)=\sigma_{k}(t,X(t))\,dB_{k}(t)+b_{k}(t,X(t))\,dt+\sum_{\alpha\in R_{+}}\frac{k_{\alpha}(t,X(t))\alpha_{k}}{\langle X(t),\alpha\rangle}\,dt,\qquad k=1,\ldots,N,

such that
X(t)\in\overline{C_{+}}
,
0\leq t<\zeta^{X}
. Here
\zeta^{X}
denotes the lifetime of the process, which may be a fixed deterministic time horizon or the explosion time. The driving process
B=(B_{1},\ldots,B_{N})
is an
N
-dimensional Brownian motion with independent one-dimensional components. The diffusion matrix is diagonal,
\sigma(t,x)=\operatorname{diag}\bigl(\sigma_{1}(t,x),\ldots,\sigma_{N}(t,x)\bigr)
, the regular drift is
b=(b_{1},\ldots,b_{N})
, and the repulsion coefficients
k_{\alpha}
are non-negative functions on
[0,\infty)\times\overline{C_{+}}
. Equivalently, (
1.1
) can be written in vector form as

(1.2)

dX(t)=\sigma(t,X(t))\,dB(t)+b(t,X(t))\,dt+\sum_{\alpha\in R_{+}}\frac{k_{\alpha}(t,X(t))\,\alpha}{\langle X(t),\alpha\rangle}\,dt.

The singular drift in (
1.2
) acts in the normal directions to the root hyperplanes
\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{N}:\langle x,\alpha\rangle=0\}
,
\alpha\in R_{+}
. Thus the boundary of the Weyl chamber plays the role of a collision boundary. In type
A_{N-1}
, this boundary is formed by the hyperplanes
x_{i}=x_{j}
. In types
B_{N}
and
D_{N}
, one additionally encounters walls corresponding to collisions with mirror images and, in type
B_{N}
, the wall
x_{i}=0
. The term
{k_{\alpha}(t,X(t))\,\alpha}/{\langle X(t),\alpha\rangle}
is therefore a Bessel-type repulsion from the corresponding wall. The rank-one case makes this interpretation explicit. Indeed, if
N=1
and
R_{+}=\{1\}
, then
C_{+}=(0,\infty)
and
\overline{C_{+}}=[0,\infty)
, and (
1.2
) reduces to

dX(t)=\sigma(t,X(t))\,dB(t)+b(t,X(t))\,dt+\frac{k(t,X(t))}{X(t)}\,dt.

In the special case
\sigma\equiv 1
,
b\equiv 0
, and
k(t,x)\equiv k_{0}>0
, this is the stochastic differential equation for a
Bessel process of dimension
2k_{0}+1
or, equivalently, of index
\nu=k_{0}-\frac{1}{2}
(see
[
39
]
). Thus the singular terms in (
1.2
) are Bessel-type repulsions from the walls of the chamber. The multidimensional root-system case replaces the single boundary point
0
by the family of root hyperplanes
\{\langle x,\alpha\rangle=0\}
,
\alpha\in R_{+}
.

We shall call solutions of (
1.2
) generalized Bessel-Dunkl diffusions. The word “Bessel” reflects the rank-one reduction above, while “Dunkl” reflects the root-system geometry of the singular drift. In the classical specialization
\sigma\equiv I
,
b\equiv 0
,
k_{\alpha}(t,x)\equiv k(\alpha)
, where
k
is a Weyl-invariant multiplicity function, one recovers the usual radial Dunkl processes, also known as multivariate Bessel processes.

To the best of our knowledge, equation (
1.2
) has not been studied before at this level of generality. The diffusion coefficients may depend on time and on the whole configuration, the regular drift may be time-inhomogeneous and configuration-dependent, and the singular coefficients
k_{\alpha}
are not assumed to be constant root multiplicities. Moreover, the coefficients
k_{\alpha}
may vanish on the corresponding wall
\{\langle x,\alpha\rangle=0\}
. This last feature is essential for degenerate models, including squared Bessel and Wishart-type particle systems, but it also creates the main analytic difficulty: the singular drift may no longer prevent the process from interacting nontrivially with the boundary.

In this sense, equation (
1.2
) provides a natural starting point for a systematic theory of generalized Bessel-Dunkl diffusions. The classical radial Dunkl processes correspond to the special case
\sigma\equiv I
,
b\equiv 0
, and
k_{\alpha}(t,x)\equiv k(\alpha)
, where
k
is a Weyl-invariant multiplicity function. The present framework allows one to move beyond that setting by treating time-dependent, state-dependent, and possibly degenerate singular coefficients in the same root-geometric formulation.

A key feature of our approach is that it uses the geometry of the underlying root system in an essential way. The chamber
\overline{C_{+}}
is not treated merely as an ordered state space with singular pairwise interactions, but as a fundamental domain for the action of the corresponding reflection group. The boundary faces, their normal directions, and the singular drift are all encoded by the root configuration. This geometric structure allows us to choose adapted invariant coordinates, to desingularise the equation, and to analyse boundary faces simultaneously rather than one collision hyperplane at a time. This is one of the reasons why the results can be obtained in the present generality with time-dependent and configuration-dependent coefficients and with repulsion coefficients that may vanish on the walls. For background on reflection groups, Weyl chambers and invariant polynomial coordinates, see
[
24
,
10
]
.

The main problem is to construct a chamber-valued process satisfying the singular equation and to understand what happens on the boundary.
When
k_{\alpha}
is strictly positive on the wall
\{\langle x,\alpha\rangle=0\}
, the repulsion is Bessel-type and forces the corresponding singular drift to be integrable along the trajectory. In particular, the process cannot spend positive Lebesgue time on that wall. Moreover, in the strictly positive regime, the geometry of the root system prevents positive-time multiple collisions, that is, simultaneous hits of non-orthogonal walls. When
k_{\alpha}
is allowed to vanish at the wall, this automatic non-sticking property may fail. We therefore distinguish between a strictly positive repulsion regime and a degenerate regime. In the degenerate case we first construct solutions of the natural interior equation, and then give a geometric criterion which excludes hidden boundary measures and allows us to recover the genuine singular equation.

The method is based on invariant coordinates associated with the root system. The singular equation in the Weyl chamber is transformed into a non-singular system for a suitable family of invariant polynomials. After solving this regularized system, we return to the closed Weyl chamber through the inverse invariant map and prove that the obtained process satisfies the original singular SDE. In the degenerate case, the boundary analysis is carried out by means of face detectors, which measure the distance to boundary faces in admissible normal directions. These detectors allow us to control local times and to decide whether additional finite-variation terms can be supported on the boundary.

The main results of the paper are the following. First, we prove weak existence in the strictly positive repulsion regime and show that positive repulsion prevents positive-time multiple collisions. Second, we prove weak existence in the degenerate regime, initially for an interior equation in which the singular terms are switched off on the corresponding walls. Third, under a facewise non-sticking condition, we show that the constructed solution spends zero Lebesgue time on the relevant boundary faces and therefore solves the genuine singular equation (
1.2
). Fourth, we establish a radial linear-growth condition which excludes explosion. Fifth, we prove pathwise uniqueness and strong existence in the non-sticky class, under either Yamada-Watanabe type assumptions or locally Lipschitz Euclidean assumptions. Finally, we prove a mean-field convergence theorem for systems of types
A_{N-1}
,
B_{N}
, and
D_{N}
. This last result connects the present work with the literature on mean-field limits and large-dimensional eigenvalue dynamics, including
[
40
,
3
,
6
,
33
,
43
,
44
]
.

We now recall the main sources of motivation for such systems. The first classical example is Dyson’s Brownian motion
[
18
]
. Dyson showed that the eigenvalues of Brownian motion on spaces of random matrices evolve as interacting one-dimensional particles with logarithmic electrostatic repulsion. For real symmetric, complex Hermitian, and quaternionic self-dual matrix Brownian motions one obtains the classical parameters
\beta=1,2,4
. The same equation, however, is meaningful for arbitrary positive values of the repulsion parameter. This model is fundamental in random matrix theory and statistical physics, where it appears as the dynamical version of the logarithmic Coulomb gas or log-gas; see
[
34
,
1
,
19
]
. For general beta ensembles and related diffusion limits, see also
[
16
,
38
]
. The connection between eigenvalue diffusions and limiting spectral laws was also studied in
[
9
,
40
]
. The same inverse-square and logarithmic interaction structures are closely related to Calogero-Moser-Sutherland systems; see
[
7
,
45
,
46
,
35
,
36
,
37
]
.

After Dyson, singularly interacting Brownian particles were studied from several complementary perspectives. Rogers and Shi
[
40
]
analysed interacting Brownian particles and their connection with the Wigner law. Cépa and Lépingle
[
8
]
developed a general framework for diffusing particles with electrostatic repulsion, using stochastic equations with multivalued drift. Their work is one of the foundational references for one-dimensional ordered particle systems in which the singular repulsion is understood as a boundary mechanism preventing or controlling collisions. The method of multivalued stochastic differential equations is very effective in that setting, but it does not seem to extend in a direct way to the present root-system framework, where the singularities are organized by all positive roots, the coefficients may depend on the full configuration, and the repulsion may degenerate on boundary faces. In contrast, the approach of the present paper is intrinsically geometric: it uses the Weyl chamber, reflection symmetries, invariant polynomials, and face structure associated with the root system. Later developments include, among others, the work of Graczyk and Małecki
[
22
,
23
]
, where strong solutions and squared Bessel particle systems were studied under assumptions allowing weak or degenerate repulsion.

A second important origin of these processes comes from conditioning particles not to collide. The Karlin-McGregor formula
[
27
]
gives determinantal transition densities for one-dimensional Markov processes killed at the first collision time. Applying a Doob
h
-transform
[
15
]
with the Vandermonde determinant produces non-colliding Brownian motion and recovers the same logarithmic drift as in Dyson’s model. Grabiner
[
20
]
extended this viewpoint to Brownian motion in Weyl chambers and related it to random matrices. This conditioning interpretation is one of the main reasons why Weyl chambers and root systems are natural state spaces for such diffusions. Related constructions and path-transform interpretations appear in
[
4
,
47
,
28
]
.

Root systems also enter through Dunkl theory. Dunkl operators were introduced in
[
17
]
; see also the survey

[
42
]
. The associated Markov processes were studied by
Rösler and Voit
[
41
]
; for further developments on
Dunkl processes and their radial parts, see

[
11
,
12
]
. The radial parts of Dunkl processes are diffusions in Weyl chambers with drift of the form

\sum_{\alpha\in R_{+}}k(\alpha)\,\frac{\alpha}{\langle x,\alpha\rangle},

where the multiplicity function
k
is constant on Weyl-group orbits. Radial Dunkl processes and their boundary hitting properties were further investigated, for example, by Demni
[
13
]
. These processes form the classical non-degenerate Bessel-Dunkl class. The present paper keeps the same geometric singular structure, but allows the coefficients to be time-dependent, configuration-dependent, and possibly degenerate.

Matrix-valued stochastic processes provide another major family of examples. Bru introduced Wishart processes
[
5
]
, which are matrix-valued analogues of squared Bessel processes. The eigenvalues of such processes satisfy singular SDEs of Laguerre or Wishart type. In the complex case, König and O’Connell
[
30
]
identified the eigenvalues of the Laguerre process with non-colliding squared Bessel processes. More recent
\beta
-extensions, such as beta-Wishart particle systems
[
26
]
, further illustrate the need for a theory that does not rely on a fixed matrix representation. Further developments on Wishart processes and their eigenvalues include
[
14
,
21
,
43
]
.

The importance of studying (
1.2
) in this generality is therefore twofold. On the one hand, it gives a unified stochastic framework for models coming from random matrix theory, statistical physics, Dunkl theory, and conditioned non-colliding particle systems. On the other hand, it isolates the analytic mechanisms responsible for existence, uniqueness, non-explosion, and boundary non-sticking in singular multidimensional diffusions. This is especially relevant for models in which the repulsion is weak or degenerate, because in such cases the behaviour at the boundary cannot be inferred from the classical non-colliding theory.

The paper is organized as follows. Section
2
states the assumptions and the main results. Section
3
recalls the necessary notation concerning root systems, Weyl chambers, symmetric polynomials, and collision faces. The next sections develop the invariant-polynomial construction, analyse collisions and integrability of the singular drift, and prove existence in the non-degenerate and degenerate regimes. The uniqueness results are proved in Section
9
. Section
10
contains the proof of the mean-field convergence theorem. The final section discusses examples, including Dyson-Cépa-Lépingle logarithmic systems, generalized particle systems introduced in
[
22
]
, squared Bessel and Wishart particle systems, and non-colliding Brownian bridge models.

2.
Assumptions and Results

This section is devoted to presenting the main results of the paper together with an explanation of the relevant assumptions. The proofs, as well as a more detailed description of the notation, are postponed to later sections, and here we introduce only the objects necessary to state the results. The assumptions are presented in groups, and this division is closely related to the theorem or proposition to which they apply.

Throughout this section, as mentioned in the Introduction,
R
denotes one of the classical root systems considered in the paper,
R_{+}
is the chosen set of positive roots,
C_{+}
is the open Weyl chamber, and
\overline{C_{+}}
is its closure. The boundary is denoted by
\partial C_{+}
. The coefficients in (
1.2
) are always evaluated on
[0,\infty)\times\overline{C_{+}}
. See Section
3
for a more detailed introduction to and description of these objects.

We begin with two structural assumptions that will be used throughout the paper. The first concerns continuity of the non-singular coefficients and the repulsion coefficients, while the second concerns compatibility of the diffusion coefficient with the boundary geometry of the Weyl chamber.

(C1)

For every
i=1,\ldots,N
and every
\alpha\in R_{+}
, the functions

(2.1)

\displaystyle\sigma_{i}

\displaystyle:[0,\infty)\times\overline{C_{+}}\longrightarrow\mathbb{R},

\displaystyle b_{i}

\displaystyle:[0,\infty)\times\overline{C_{+}}\longrightarrow\mathbb{R},

\displaystyle k_{\alpha}

\displaystyle:[0,\infty)\times\overline{C_{+}}\longrightarrow[0,\infty)

are continuous.

(C2)

For every
t\geq 0
and every
\alpha\in R_{+}
such that
|\alpha|^{2}=2
, write

\displaystyle\operatorname{supp}(\alpha)

\displaystyle=\{r\in\{1,\ldots,N\}:\alpha_{r}\neq 0\}

\displaystyle=\{i,j\}.

We assume that

(2.2)

\displaystyle\sigma_{i}^{2}(t,x)-\sigma_{j}^{2}(t,x)

\displaystyle\longrightarrow 0\qquad\text{as }x\in\overline{C_{+}},\ \langle x,\alpha\rangle\downarrow 0.

Assumption
(C1)
is standard. It is imposed on the non-singular coefficients
\sigma_{i}
,
b_{i}
, and
k_{\alpha}
, and the same type of continuity hypothesis already appears in the work of Graczyk and Małecki
[
22
]
. In their setting, the diffusion coefficient is scalar and depends on a single space variable, so continuity of
\sigma:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
is sufficient.

Assumption
(C2)
is specific to, and natural in, the present level of generality. Here the diffusion coefficient is diagonal with components
\sigma=(\sigma_{1},\ldots,\sigma_{N})
and depends on the whole configuration
x\in\overline{C_{+}}
, so one needs an additional continuity-type compatibility condition at long-root walls. For
\alpha=e_{i}-e_{j}
or
\alpha=e_{i}+e_{j}
, it requires that
\sigma_{i}^{2}(t,x)
and
\sigma_{j}^{2}(t,x)
become asymptotically equal as
\langle x,\alpha\rangle\downarrow 0
within
\overline{C_{+}}
. In particular, it is not an ellipticity assumption. In type
A_{N-1}
it reduces to the usual requirement that colliding particles have the same variance. In type
B_{N}
it is imposed only on long roots, so it does not force
\sigma_{i}
to vanish at the short-root wall
x_{i}=0
. This condition is precisely what prevents the Itô correction terms in the invariant-coordinate equations from creating singular boundary contributions.

2.1.
Strictly positive repulsion

The first existence regime we consider is the one in which each repulsion coefficient is strictly positive on its corresponding wall. This is the natural analogue of the one-dimensional Bessel-type situation: the singular drift is strong enough to prevent the process from spending positive Lebesgue time on the boundary, although instantaneous visits to the boundary may still occur.

(A1)

For every
\alpha\in R_{+}
, every
t\geq 0
, and every
x\in\overline{C_{+}}
satisfying

\langle x,\alpha\rangle=0
, we assume that

(2.3)

\displaystyle k_{\alpha}(t,x)

\displaystyle>0.

Assumption
(A1)
is local to the wall associated with the root
\alpha
. Away from this wall, the quotient
k_{\alpha}(t,x)/\langle x,\alpha\rangle
is regular, so no lower bound on
k_{\alpha}
is needed there.

Theorem 1

(Existence in the strictly positive regime)
.

Let
x_{0}\in\overline{C_{+}}
. Assume
(C1)
,
(C2)
, and
(A1)
. Then there exists a weak solution
X=(X_{1},\ldots,X_{N})
of (
1.2
), started from
x_{0}
, which takes values in
\overline{C_{+}}
up to its lifetime
\zeta^{X}>0
. More precisely, there exist a filtered probability space carrying an
N
-dimensional Brownian motion
B
and a continuous
\overline{C_{+}}
-valued semimartingale
X=(X_{1},\ldots,X_{N})
such that

(2.4)

\displaystyle X(t)

\displaystyle=x_{0}+\int_{0}^{t}\sigma(s,X(s))\,dB(s)+\int_{0}^{t}b(s,X(s))\,ds+\sum_{\alpha\in R_{+}}\alpha\int_{0}^{t}\frac{k_{\alpha}(s,X(s))}{\langle X(s),\alpha\rangle}\,ds

for every
t\in[0,\zeta^{X})
. Moreover, the singular integrals in (
2.4
) are finite on compact subintervals of
[0,\zeta^{X})
, with the usual improper interpretation at the initial time if
x_{0}\in\partial C_{+}
.

By continuity of
k_{\alpha}
and Assumption
(A1)
, the quotient
k_{\alpha}(t,x)/\langle x,\alpha\rangle
extends continuously to the wall
\{\langle x,\alpha\rangle=0\}
as the value
+\infty
in the extended real sense. Consequently, the finiteness of the singular drift integral in Theorem
1
implies that the process cannot spend positive Lebesgue time on that wall.

The proof of Theorem
1
is based on the
invariant-coordinate map. One first transforms the singular equation in the
chamber into a non-singular system for the basic invariant polynomials. After
solving that system, one returns to the closed chamber via the inverse
invariant map and verifies that the recovered process satisfies
(
1.2
). The zero-occupation property discussed above then
follows from the wall positivity of
k_{\alpha}
and the finiteness of the
corresponding singular drift integrals.

The proof also yields an additional geometric conclusion. The crucial point
is that, in the strictly positive regime, the process cannot hit multiple
collision strata after time zero. This property is used in the identification
of the singular drift, and, once Theorem
1
is proved, it
becomes a property of the constructed solution. Due to its importance, we
state it separately.

Theorem 2

(Absence of positive-time multiple collisions)
.

Let
x_{0}\in\overline{C_{+}}
. Assume
(C1)
,
(C2)
, and
(A1)
. Then the weak solution
X
constructed in Theorem
1
has no multiple collisions at positive times. More precisely, for every two different roots
\alpha,\beta\in R_{+}
such that
\sum_{i=1}^{N}\alpha_{i}^{2}\beta_{i}^{2}>0
we have

\left<X(t),\alpha\right>+\left<X(t),\beta\right>>0,\qquad 0<t<\zeta^{X}\qquad\textrm{a.s.}

Since
X(t)\in\overline{C_{+}}
, all quantities
\left<X(t),\alpha\right>
are non-negative. The condition
\sum_{i=1}^{N}\alpha_{i}^{2}\beta_{i}^{2}>0

means that the two roots
\alpha
and
\beta
involve at least one common coordinate. Thus the conclusion says that two such walls cannot be hit simultaneously after time zero. This includes, in particular, all pairs of non-orthogonal positive roots. In type
A_{N-1}
, it implies that three or more particles cannot collide at a positive time. In types
B_{N}
and
D_{N}
, it also includes the orthogonal zero-pair
\alpha=e_{i}-e_{j}
,
\beta=e_{i}+e_{j}
and therefore excludes the simultaneous vanishing of
X_{i}-X_{j}
and
X_{i}+X_{j}

at a positive time.

This should be distinguished from simultaneous collisions of genuinely orthogonal type with disjoint coordinate supports. It may still be possible that two distinct roots
\alpha,\beta\in R_{+}
with
\sum_{i=1}^{N}\alpha_{i}^{2}\beta_{i}^{2}=0
, satisfy

\left<X(t),\alpha\right>=\left<X(t),\beta\right>=0

at the same time. Related results excluding such simultaneous collisions for a different class of
interacting particle systems were obtained in

[
2
]
.

2.2.
Degenerate repulsion

We next allow the singular repulsion to vanish on the walls. This generalization lets us treat a substantially wider class of particle systems, including squared Bessel particle systems and related Wishart-type examples, in which the repulsion coefficients may vanish at specific boundary points.

We begin by introducing two assumptions that replace Assumption
(A1)
. Both are natural: their role is to ensure that there is still enough repulsion at the boundary even when the coefficient of the singular term vanishes. The formulation of the second assumption requires the introduction of the collision faces and the associated detectors. More precisely, for
y\in\partial C_{+}
, set

\displaystyle S(y)

\displaystyle:=\{\alpha\in R_{+}:\langle y,\alpha\rangle=0\}.

Let
\mathfrak{S}
be the family of all non-empty sets of the form
S(y)
, and for
S\in\mathfrak{S}
define

(2.5)

\displaystyle F_{S}

\displaystyle:=\{x\in\overline{C_{+}}:\langle x,\alpha\rangle=0\text{ for }\alpha\in S,\ \langle x,\beta\rangle>0\text{ for }\beta\in R_{+}\setminus S\}.

Thus
F_{S}
is the relatively open part of the boundary on which exactly the roots in
S
vanish. A vector
u\in\operatorname{span}S
is called an admissible detector for
F_{S}
if
\tau_{u}(x):=\langle u,x\rangle
satisfies

\displaystyle\tau_{u}(x)

\displaystyle\geq 0,

\displaystyle x\in\overline{C_{+}},

\displaystyle\tau_{u}(x)

\displaystyle=0,

\displaystyle x\in F_{S},

\displaystyle\langle u,\alpha\rangle

\displaystyle\geq 0,

\displaystyle\alpha\in S.

The roots of the face crossed by
u
are collected in

\displaystyle\Gamma(S,u)

\displaystyle:=\{\alpha\in S:\langle u,\alpha\rangle>0\}.

For every
S\in\mathfrak{S}
, we fix a finite family
\mathfrak{D}(S)
of admissible detectors. We choose this family so that it contains the canonical detector
u_{S}:=\sum_{\beta\in S}\beta
, and such that each
\alpha\in S
belongs to
\Gamma(S,u)
for at least one
u\in\mathfrak{D}(S)
. For an explicit example, consider type
A_{N-1}
with
C_{+}=\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{N}:x_{1}>\cdots>x_{N}\}
, and the face

\displaystyle F_{S}=\{x\in\overline{C_{+}}:x_{1}>x_{2}=x_{3}=x_{4}>x_{5}>\cdots>x_{N}\}

corresponding to

\displaystyle S=\{e_{2}-e_{3},\ e_{2}-e_{4},\ e_{3}-e_{4}\}.

An admissible detector for this face is
\tau_{u}(x)=x_{2}-x_{4}=\langle e_{2}-e_{4},x\rangle
: it is nonnegative on
\overline{C_{+}}
, vanishes on
F_{S}
, and is strictly sensitive to each root in
S
. One may therefore take
\mathfrak{D}(S)=\{e_{2}-e_{4}\}
, because then
\Gamma(S,e_{2}-e_{4})=S
. Finally, for
u\in\mathfrak{D}(S)
, define the regular detector drift by

(2.6)

\displaystyle B_{S,u}(t,x)

\displaystyle:=\langle u,b(t,x)\rangle+\sum_{\beta\in R_{+}\setminus S}\frac{k_{\beta}(t,x)\langle u,\beta\rangle}{\langle x,\beta\rangle}.

Only roots not vanishing on the face appear in
B_{S,u}
. Hence, after localizing away from neighboring faces, the denominators in (
2.6
) stay bounded away from zero, and
B_{S,u}
admits a continuous trace on
F_{S}
. Intuitively,
B_{S,u}
is the regular part of the drift seen in the detector direction.

(D1)

Normal dominance near the wall.

For every
T,R>0
there exist constants

\varepsilon=\varepsilon(T,R)>0
and
\gamma=\gamma(T,R)>0
such that, for every
t\in[0,T]
, every
x\in\overline{C_{+}}
with
|x|\leq R
, and every
\alpha\in R_{+}
,

(2.7)

\displaystyle 0\leq\langle x,\alpha\rangle\leq\varepsilon

\displaystyle\Longrightarrow k_{\alpha}(t,x)\geq\gamma\sum_{i=1}^{N}\alpha_{i}^{2}\sigma_{i}^{2}(t,x).

(D2)

Face sign condition.
For every
S\in\mathfrak{S}
and every
u\in\mathfrak{D}(S)
, the continuous trace of the regular detector drift satisfies

(2.8)

\displaystyle B_{S,u}(t,x)

\displaystyle\geq 0,\qquad t\geq 0,\qquad x\in F_{S}.

Since
\sum_{i=1}^{N}\alpha_{i}^{2}\sigma_{i}^{2}(t,x)
is the quadratic variation density of the martingale part in the normal direction
\alpha
, Assumption
(D1)
says that when
x
approaches the wall
\{\langle x,\alpha\rangle=0\}
, the repulsion coefficient
k_{\alpha}(t,x)
may vanish, but not faster than the corresponding normal martingale variation. In this sense, the possible loss of repulsion is compensated by a comparable loss of noise in the same direction. This is the Bessel-type balance that is later used to show that the relevant detector local times vanish. It also makes the framework flexible enough to cover, in particular,
\beta
-versions of known particle systems also those with vanishing coefficients.

Assumption
(D2)
is a weak inward-pointing condition on the regular part of the detector drift. It is used to eliminate finite-variation measures which could otherwise be supported on the boundary. Together, these two assumptions yield the general degenerate existence result below. By themselves, however, they do not force the process to spend zero Lebesgue time on the boundary.

Theorem 3

(Existence in the degenerate case)
.

Let
x_{0}\in\overline{C_{+}}
. Assume
(C1)
,
(C2)
,
(D1)
, and
(D2)
. Then there exists a filtered probability space carrying an
N
-dimensional Brownian motion
B
and a continuous
\overline{C_{+}}
-valued semimartingale
X=(X_{1},\ldots,X_{N})
, defined up to its lifetime
\zeta^{X}>0
, such that

(2.9)

\displaystyle X(t)

\displaystyle=x_{0}+\int_{0}^{t}\sigma(s,X(s))\,dB(s)+\int_{0}^{t}b(s,X(s))\,ds+\sum_{\alpha\in R_{+}}\alpha\int_{0}^{t}\frac{k_{\alpha}(s,X(s))}{\langle X(s),\alpha\rangle}\mathbf{1}_{\{\langle X(s),\alpha\rangle>0\}}\,ds\/,

for every
t\in[0,\zeta^{X})
. In particular, for every
\alpha\in R_{+}
the singular integrals in (
2.9
) are finite on compact subintervals of
[0,\zeta^{X})
.

Theorem
3
is the basic existence statement in the degenerate regime. In contrast to the strictly positive case, it does not exclude the possibility that the process spends a positive amount of Lebesgue time on the boundary. In the present framework, such behavior may occur only at genuinely degenerate boundary points, that is, at points where the corresponding coefficient
k_{\alpha}
vanishes. By Assumption
(D1)
, this then forces the normal quadratic variation coefficient
a_{\alpha}(t,x)
to vanish as well. Thus any possible sticking can only occur at boundary points where both the singular repulsion and the normal martingale variation degenerate simultaneously.

The indicator in (
2.9
) means that the singular drift is interpreted through its interior density. This is natural in the degenerate case, because when both
k_{\alpha}(t,x)
and
\langle x,\alpha\rangle
vanish, the ratio
k_{\alpha}(t,x)/\langle x,\alpha\rangle
need not have a canonical boundary value and, in general, need not admit a continuous extension to the wall. For this reason, the interior formulation with an indicator is the natural object to consider. A formulation of this type already appears in
[
23
]
, where squared Bessel particle systems are considered.

Finally, any genuine solution of the equation without the indicator is automatically a solution of (
2.9
) whenever it spends zero Lebesgue time on every root wall, because the two drift densities then differ only on a Lebesgue-null set of times. The converse need not hold in general: the indicator formulation may admit sticky behavior at degenerate walls. This is why removing the indicator requires an additional non-sticking input, which we isolate in the next subsection.

2.3.
A non-sticking criterion for degenerate walls

Theorem
3
gives a solution of the degenerate equation in its natural interior form. To pass to the formulation without the indicator, one has to exclude sticking on the boundary. We impose a structural face-by-face escape condition expressed in terms of admissible detectors and the quadratic-variation densities of the corresponding detector martingales. This is the form that seems most useful in applications.

(D3)

Facewise escape at fully degenerate points.

For every collision face
F_{S}
, every
t\geq 0
, and every
x\in F_{S}
,

(2.10)

\displaystyle\max_{u\in\mathfrak{D}(S)}\sum_{i=1}^{N}u_{i}^{2}\sigma_{i}^{2}(t,x)=0

\displaystyle\Longrightarrow\max_{u\in\mathfrak{D}(S)}B_{S,u}(t,x)>0.

Assumption
(D3)
concerns only the genuinely degenerate situation in which all admissible detectors have zero quadratic variation at the face point. If for some admissible detector
u
the quantity

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{N}u_{i}^{2}\sigma_{i}^{2}(t,x)

is positive, then that detector already provides the stochastic escape mechanism needed in the local argument. Assumption
(D3)
says that in the complementary case, when all detector martingale variations vanish, at least one detector must still have strictly positive regular drift.

This condition is closely related in spirit to the non-degeneration condition used by Graczyk and Małecki
[
22
]
. In their one-dimensional ordered-particle setting, when the pairwise martingale part and the pairwise repulsion vanish at a degenerate collision point, one assumes that the remaining drift of the colliding block does not vanish. Here the same idea is formulated geometrically on each collision face: if every admissible detector loses its martingale variation, then some detector must still point strictly away from the face. In the Wishart-type examples, this role is played by a block-center detector on a zero block.

Theorem 4

(Degenerate existence with non-sticking boundary behavior)
.

Let
x_{0}\in\overline{C_{+}}
. Assume
(C1)
,
(C2)
,
(D1)
,
(D2)
, and
(D3)
. Then there exists a filtered probability space carrying an
N
-dimensional Brownian motion
B
and a continuous
\overline{C_{+}}
-valued semimartingale
X=(X_{1},\ldots,X_{N})
, defined up to its lifetime
\zeta^{X}>0
, such that
X(0)=x_{0}
and, for every
t\in[0,\zeta^{X})
,

(2.11)

\displaystyle X(t)

\displaystyle=x_{0}+\int_{0}^{t}\sigma(s,X(s))\,dB(s)+\int_{0}^{t}b(s,X(s))\,ds+\sum_{\alpha\in R_{+}}\alpha\int_{0}^{t}\frac{k_{\alpha}(s,X(s))}{\langle X(s),\alpha\rangle}\,ds.

The singular integrals in (
2.11
) are finite on compact subintervals of
[0,\zeta^{X})
, with the usual improper interpretation at the initial time if
x_{0}\in\partial C_{+}
.

By the discussion above, at each collision face there is an escape mechanism: either some admissible detector retains non-trivial quadratic variation, or, in the fully degenerate case, Assumption
(D3)
provides a strictly positive detector drift. Consequently, the solution from Theorem
3
spends zero Lebesgue time on every collision face, hence also on every root wall
\{x\in\overline{C_{+}}:\langle x,\alpha\rangle=0\}
,
\alpha\in R_{+}
and the indicator in (
2.9
) becomes irrelevant.

The indicator formulation in Theorem
3
is nevertheless the natural starting point in the general degenerate setting. Indeed, when both
k_{\alpha}(t,x)
and
\langle x,\alpha\rangle
vanish, the ratio
{k_{\alpha}(t,x)}/{\langle x,\alpha\rangle}
need not have a canonical boundary value and, in general, need not admit a continuous extension to the wall. Theorem
4
shows that under the additional facewise escape condition this ambiguity is harmless, because the boundary is then visited only on a Lebesgue-null set of times.

2.4.
Non-explosion under radial linear growth

The lifetime in the preceding results is included because, up to this point, no growth condition has been imposed on the coefficients. A standard radial linear-growth assumption removes the possibility of an explosion.

(G1)

For every
T>0
there exists a constant
C_{T}<\infty
such that, for every

t\in[0,T]
and every
x\in\overline{C_{+}}
,

(2.12)

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{N}x_{i}b_{i}(t,x)+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\sigma_{i}^{2}(t,x)+\sum_{\alpha\in R_{+}}k_{\alpha}(t,x)

\displaystyle\leq C_{T}\bigl(1+|x|^{2}\bigr).

Assumption
(G1)
is the usual radial linear-growth condition for the Lyapunov function
|x|^{2}
. It controls at once the contribution of the drift, the Itô correction coming from the diffusion part, and the radial contribution of the singular repulsion. This is the analogue of the non-explosion condition used by Graczyk and Małecki
[
22
]
. We use here the radial bound in the form involving
|x|^{2}
; in the formulation of condition (C2) in
[
22
]
, the upper bound for the interaction function
H
is written with a term of the form
|xy|
, but the estimate needed in the non-explosion argument is the radial one, namely a bound by a constant times
1+|x|^{2}+|y|^{2}
. These two bounds are not equivalent in the required direction.

Theorem 5

(Non-explosion)
.

Assume
(G1)
. Let
X
be any
\overline{C_{+}}
-valued weak solution, defined up to its explosion lifetime, satisfying either the singular equation (
2.4
) or the degenerate interior equation (
2.9
). Then

(2.13)

\displaystyle\zeta^{X}

\displaystyle=\infty,\qquad\text{a.s.}

Consequently, under Assumption
(G1)
, the weak and strong solutions provided by Theorems
1
,
3
,
4
, and
7
are global in time.

2.5.
Uniqueness

The assumptions used in the existence theorems are not sufficient to imply uniqueness. We therefore introduce a separate set of hypotheses tailored to pathwise uniqueness. These assumptions are natural from two complementary viewpoints. On the one hand, they extend the standard one-dimensional uniqueness assumptions from the literature. On the other hand, they are adapted to the root-system geometry underlying the particle representation of the Bessel-Dunkl diffusions considered here.

(U1)

For every
T,r>0
, at least one of the following two alternatives holds:

(a)

there exists an increasing function

\rho=\rho_{T,r}:\mathbb{R}_{+}\to\mathbb{R}_{+}
such that

(2.14)

\displaystyle\rho(0)

\displaystyle=0,

\displaystyle\rho(u)

\displaystyle>0\quad\text{for }u>0,

\displaystyle\int_{0+}\frac{du}{\rho(u)}

\displaystyle=\infty,

and, for every
i=1,\ldots,N
,

(2.15)

\displaystyle|\sigma_{i}(t,x)-\sigma_{i}(t,y)|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\rho(|x_{i}-y_{i}|)

whenever
t\in[0,T]
,
x,y\in\overline{C_{+}}
, and
|x|\vee|y|\leq r
;

(b)

there exists a constant
L_{T,r}<\infty
such that, for every
i=1,\ldots,N
,

(2.16)

\displaystyle|\sigma_{i}(t,x)-\sigma_{i}(t,y)|

\displaystyle\leq L_{T,r}|x-y|

whenever
t\in[0,T]
,
x,y\in\overline{C_{+}}
, and
|x|\vee|y|\leq r
.

(U2)

For every
T,r>0
there exists a constant
L_{T,r}<\infty
such that

(2.17)

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{N}\operatorname{sgn}(x_{i}-y_{i})\bigl(b_{i}(t,x)-b_{i}(t,y)\bigr)

\displaystyle\leq L_{T,r}\sum_{i=1}^{N}|x_{i}-y_{i}|

whenever
t\in[0,T]
,
x,y\in\overline{C_{+}}
, and
|x|\vee|y|\leq r
.

(U3)

For
x\in C_{+}
, define the singular force

(2.18)

G(t,x):=\sum_{\alpha\in R_{+}}\alpha\,\frac{k_{\alpha}(t,x)}{\langle x,\alpha\rangle}.

For every
T,r>0
, every
t\in[0,T]
, and all
x,y\in C_{+}
with

|x|\vee|y|\leq r
,

(2.19)

\sum_{i=1}^{N}\operatorname{sgn}(x_{i}-y_{i})\bigl(G_{i}(t,x)-G_{i}(t,y)\bigr)\leq 0.

We shall also use the following Euclidean alternatives in the locally Lipschitz
diffusion regime.

(U2’)

For every
T,r>0
there exists a constant
L_{T,r}<\infty
such that

(2.20)

\langle x-y,b(t,x)-b(t,y)\rangle\leq L_{T,r}|x-y|^{2}

whenever
t\in[0,T]
,
x,y\in\overline{C_{+}}
, and
|x|\vee|y|\leq r
.

(U3’)

For every
T,r>0
, every
t\in[0,T]
, and all
x,y\in C_{+}
with

|x|\vee|y|\leq r
,

(2.21)

\langle x-y,G(t,x)-G(t,y)\rangle\leq 0.

The assumptions above split the uniqueness theory into two regimes. Assumption
(U1)
(a) is the coordinatewise Yamada-Watanabe modulus condition and, in one dimension, reduces to the classical criterion allowing less than Lipschitz regularity in the martingale part. It is the natural framework for square-root-type examples. In this regime the proof is based on Tanaka’s formula in the
\ell^{1}
-geometry. Therefore it is paired with Assumption
(U2)
, the corresponding one-sided Lipschitz condition on the regular drift, and with the rootwise monotonicity condition Assumption
(U3)
.

Assumption
(U1)
(b) is the standard local Lipschitz condition. In this regime the proof is based on Itô’s formula for
|X-\widetilde{X}|^{2}
. Therefore the appropriate drift condition is the Euclidean one-sided Lipschitz condition Assumption
(U2’)
. The singular part may then be controlled either by the rootwise condition Assumption
(U3)
, or more generally by the global dissipativity condition Assumption
(U3’)
.

In the ordered-particle setting of Graczyk and Małecki
[
22
]
, alternative (a) in Assumption
(U1)
together with Assumption
(U2)
plays the role of their condition (C1), while Assumption
(U3)
is the analogue of their condition (A1).

Remark 1

(Logarithmic potentials)
.

A useful way to verify Assumption
(U3’)
is through a logarithmic potential. Suppose that, for each
t
, there exists a positive function
h_{t}
on
C_{+}
such that
\log h_{t}
is concave and

G(t,x)=\nabla\log h_{t}(x),\qquad x\in C_{+}.

Then Assumption
(U3’)
holds, because the gradient of a concave function is dissipative:

\langle x-y,\nabla\log h_{t}(x)-\nabla\log h_{t}(y)\rangle\leq 0.

Equivalently, if
G(t,x)=-\nabla\Phi_{t}(x)
for a convex function
\Phi_{t}
, then Assumption
(U3’)
holds. In particular, the constant-multiplicity root-barrier force is obtained from

h_{t}(x)=\prod_{\alpha\in R_{+}}\langle x,\alpha\rangle^{m_{\alpha}(t)},\qquad m_{\alpha}(t)>0.

Then

\nabla\log h_{t}(x)=\sum_{\alpha\in R_{+}}m_{\alpha}(t)\frac{\alpha}{\langle x,\alpha\rangle},

which corresponds to
k_{\alpha}(t,x)=m_{\alpha}(t)
. This is the root-system analogue of the logarithmic electrostatic repulsion appearing in the Cépa-Lépingle framework.

As already observed in
[
23
]
, equations with an indicator in the singular term may admit solutions of two genuinely different types when degenerate boundary points are present: sticky solutions, which may spend positive Lebesgue time on the boundary, and non-sticky solutions, for which the time spent on the boundary has Lebesgue measure zero. One therefore cannot expect pathwise uniqueness in the full class of solutions of (
2.9
). For this reason, we restrict attention to non-sticky solutions, that is, solutions satisfying

(2.22)

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}\mathbf{1}_{\{\langle X(s),\alpha\rangle=0\}}\,ds

\displaystyle=0,\qquad\alpha\in R_{+},\qquad t<\zeta^{X},\qquad\text{a.s.}

In this class, the indicator is irrelevant, and a non-sticky solution of (
2.9
) is equivalently a solution of (
1.2
).

Theorem 6

(Pathwise uniqueness in the non-sticky class)
.

Assume that one of the following two uniqueness regimes holds.

(i)

The Yamada-Watanabe alternative
(a)
in
Assumption
(U1)
holds, together with
Assumptions
(U2)
and
(U3)
.

(ii)

The locally Lipschitz alternative
(b)
in
Assumption
(U1)
holds, together with

(U2’)
and Assumption
(U3’)
.

Let
(X,B)
and
(\widetilde{X},B)
be two solutions of
(
2.9
), defined on the same filtered
probability space, driven by the same Brownian motion, and with the same
initial condition. Assume moreover that (
2.22
) holds for both

X
and
\widetilde{X}
. Then

(2.23)

\displaystyle X(t)

\displaystyle=\widetilde{X}(t),\qquad 0\leq t<\zeta^{X}\wedge\zeta^{\widetilde{X}},\qquad\text{a.s.}

In particular,

\displaystyle\zeta^{X}

\displaystyle=\zeta^{\widetilde{X}},\qquad\text{a.s.}

Under alternative (a) in Assumption
(U1)
, the proof follows the classical Tanaka-Gronwall strategy: the coordinatewise local times vanish, Assumption
(U2)
controls the contribution of the regular drift, and Assumption
(U3)
makes the singular term non-positive in the
\ell^{1}
estimate. The treatment of alternative (b) is different and will be handled separately.

Again, every solution of (
1.2
) satisfying (
2.22
) is also a solution of (
2.9
). Therefore, combining the weak existence results with Theorem
6
yields strong existence and uniqueness by the Yamada-Watanabe principle, after localization by the explosion time. In the degenerate regime this is done only after Theorem
4
, because only then do we have a weak solution of (
1.2
) in the non-sticky class.

Theorem 7

(Strong existence and uniqueness)
.

Let
\nu
be a probability measure on
\overline{C_{+}}
. Assume
(C1)
and

(C2)
. Assume moreover that one of the two uniqueness regimes in
Theorem
6
holds. Suppose also that
one of the following alternatives holds:

(S1)

Assumption
(A1)
holds.

(S2)

Assumptions
(D1)
,
(D2)
, and

(D3)
hold.

If
\xi
is a
\overline{C_{+}}
-valued random variable with law
\nu
, independent of an

N
-dimensional Brownian motion
B
, then (
1.2
) has a strong
solution
X
with
X(0)=\xi
, adapted to the completed filtration generated by

\xi
and
B
, up to its lifetime. This solution is pathwise unique in the
class of solutions satisfying (
2.22
), and uniqueness in law holds
in the same class.

2.6.
Mean-field convergence

One of the most significant results in the theory of particle systems concerns the convergence of systems in the case where the number of particles tends to infinity. The starting point for studying phenomena of this type is always provided by theorems concerning the existence and uniqueness of solutions to stochastic differential equations. Therefore, the previously presented results, as well as their generality, make it possible to explore this part of the theory in a broad context.

Below we present one such result concerning mean-field convergence. In this case, however, we restrict the class of particles under consideration to those for which the martingale and regular drift coefficients depend only on one coordinate, while the repulsion coefficient attached to a pair root depends only on the two particles involved in this interaction. This is precisely the structure which makes the limiting integro-differential equation for the normalized empirical measures closed. Nevertheless, we retain the dependence of all coefficients on the time variable, and the model still covers most examples considered in the literature.

Fix a type
\mathfrak{r}\in\{A,B,D\}
and set
E_{A}=\mathbb{R}
,
E_{B}=E_{D}=[0,\infty)
together with
\eta_{A}^{0}=\eta_{D}^{0}=0
,
\eta_{B}^{0}=1
. We will drop the subscript related to the root type in the notation below and write simply
E
in all cases. In the
D_{N}
case, under our chamber convention, only the last coordinate may be negative. We assume that the martingale and drift coefficients are functions of the form

\sigma_{i}(t,x)=\sigma(t,x_{i})\/,\qquad b_{i}(t,x)=b(t,x_{i})

with a fairly clear abuse of notation, and
\sigma,b:[0,\infty)\times E\to\mathbb{R}
. The coefficient describing the singular behavior is of the form

(2.24)

k_{\alpha}(t,x)=k(t,x_{i},x_{j})\/,\quad\textrm{for }\alpha=e_{i}\pm e_{j}\/,\qquad k_{\alpha}(t,x)=k(t,x_{i})\/,\quad\textrm{for }\alpha=e_{i}\/.

Thus, whenever we write
k_{\alpha}
below we understand it to be in the form given in (
2.24
), with the simplified convention that whenever
k
has two spatial arguments, then it corresponds to the pair roots, and with only one spatial argument, whenever we are dealing with short roots. It is clear that
k(t,x,y)=k(t,y,x)
in this setting. In the
D_{N}
case, if the last coordinate is involved in the evaluation of a coefficient, we use its absolute value in the argument of
\sigma
,
b
, and
k
.

Additionally, we consider the set of test functions
D=C_{b}^{2}(\mathbb{R})
, whenever we work in the
A_{N-1}
case and we get
D=\{f\in C_{b}^{2}([0,\infty)):\ f^{\prime}(0)=0\}
in the cases
B_{N}
and
D_{N}
. Note that although for
D_{N}
one of the particle might be negative, since we consider the limit with number of particles growing to infinity, it is irrelevant and the limiting measures have supports on non-negative half-line. Finally, we define

\displaystyle D_{f}^{-}(x,y)

\displaystyle:=\begin{cases}\dfrac{f^{\prime}(x)-f^{\prime}(y)}{x-y},&x\neq y,\\[5.16663pt]
f^{\prime\prime}(x),&x=y,\end{cases}

and additionally for
x,y\geq 0
we set

\displaystyle D_{f}^{+}(x,y)

\displaystyle:=\begin{cases}\dfrac{f^{\prime}(x)+f^{\prime}(y)}{x+y},&x+y>0,\\[5.16663pt]
f^{\prime\prime}(0),&x=y=0.\end{cases}

Using those two we can cover all three considered case by writing
D_{f}(x,y)=D_{f}^{-}(x,y)
in
A_{N-1}
case and
D_{f}(x,y)=D_{f}^{-}(x,y)+D_{f}^{+}(x,y)
in the remaining two cases.

For each
N
, let
X^{(N)}=(X_{1}^{(N)},\ldots,X_{N}^{(N)})
be a continuous solution, taking values in the appropriate closed chamber, of the system

(2.25)

\displaystyle dX_{i}^{(N)}(t)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}\sigma\bigl(t,X_{i}^{(N)}(t)\bigr)\,dB_{i}(t)+b\bigl(t,X_{i}^{(N)}(t)\bigr)\,dt+\sum_{\alpha\in R_{+}}\alpha_{i}\frac{k_{\alpha}^{(N)}\bigl(t,X^{(N)}(t)\bigr)}{\langle X^{(N)}(t),\alpha\rangle}\,dt,

for
i=1,\ldots,N

where
k_{e_{i}\pm e_{j}}^{(N)}(t,x)=\frac{1}{N}k(t,x_{i},x_{j})
and
k_{e_{i}}^{(N)}(t,x)=k(t,x_{i})
, so the additional scaling is not present when we are considering the short roots. The corresponding empirical measure is defined as

\displaystyle\mu_{t}^{(N)}

\displaystyle:=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\delta_{X_{i}^{(N)}(t)}

in the
A_{N-1}
and
B_{N}
cases. In the
D_{N}
case, since only the last coordinate may be negative, we define instead

\displaystyle\mu_{t}^{(N)}

\displaystyle:=\frac{1}{N-1}\sum_{i=1}^{N-1}\delta_{X_{i}^{(N)}(t)}.

This modification has no effect on the limiting measure as
N\to\infty
, and it makes
\mu_{t}^{(N)}
supported on
E_{D}=[0,\infty)
.

Since in our framework the coefficients
\sigma
and
k
are not tied together by any specific matrix structure, one may also study different scalings. For instance, one may replace
N^{-1/2}
by
\sqrt{\varepsilon_{N}}
in the martingale part and
N^{-1}
by
q_{N}/N
in the pair interaction, where
\varepsilon_{N}\geq 0
and
q_{N}\geq 0
are given convergent sequences. The classical regime considered above corresponds to
\varepsilon_{N}=1/N
and
q_{N}\equiv 1
. More general choices lead to different limiting equations and can be treated in exactly the same way.

We impose the following assumptions on every finite time interval.

(MF1)

For every
N
, the system (
2.25
) has a continuous solution on compact time intervals in the appropriate closed chamber, and all singular drift terms in (
2.25
) are integrable on compact time intervals.

(MF2)

The functions
\sigma
,
b
, and the one- and two-variable versions of
k
are continuous on their respective domains. Moreover, the two-variable function
k
is symmetric, non-negative, and for every
T>0
there exists
C_{T}<\infty
such that, for all
t\in[0,T]
and all admissible
x,y
,

(2.26)

\displaystyle|\sigma(t,x)|^{2}+|b(t,x)|^{2}+k(t,x)

\displaystyle\leq C_{T}(1+|x|^{2}),\qquad x\in E\/,

(2.27)

\displaystyle k(t,x,y)

\displaystyle\leq C_{T}(1+|x|)(1+|y|)\/,\qquad x,y\in E\/.

The one-variable function
k(t,x)
is present only in the
B_{N}
case.

(MF3)

The initial empirical measures converge in probability to a deterministic probability measure
\mu_{0}\in\mathcal{P}(E)
, and

\displaystyle\sup_{N\geq 1}\mathbf{E}\int_{E}|x|^{8}\mu_{0}^{(N)}(dx)<\infty.

Theorem 8

(Mean-field convergence)
.

Assume
(MF1)
-
(MF3)
. Then, for every
T>0
, the laws of
(\mu_{t}^{(N)})_{0\leq t\leq T}
are tight in
C([0,T],\mathcal{P}(E_{\mathfrak{r}}))
. Moreover, every weak limit point
(\mu_{t})_{0\leq t\leq T}
satisfies, for every
f\in D
and every
t\in[0,T]
,

\displaystyle\left\langle\mu_{t},f\right\rangle

\displaystyle=\left\langle\mu_{0},f\right\rangle+\int_{0}^{t}\int_{E}b(s,x)f^{\prime}(x)\mu_{s}(dx)\,ds+\eta_{\mathfrak{r}}^{0}\int_{0}^{t}\int_{E}\frac{k(s,x)f^{\prime}(x)}{x}\mu_{s}(dx)\,ds

(2.28)

\displaystyle\qquad+\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{t}\int_{E^{2}}D_{f}(x,y)\,k(s,x,y)\,\mu_{s}(dx)\mu_{s}(dy)\,ds

The quotient
k(s,x)f^{\prime}(x)/x
is understood at
x=0
as
k(s,0)f^{\prime\prime}(0)
. In particular, every subsequential limit solves (
2.28
). If (
2.28
) has a unique solution in
C([0,T],\mathcal{P}(E))
, then

\displaystyle\mu^{(N)}\Longrightarrow\mu\qquad\text{in }C([0,T],\mathcal{P}(E)).

The same argument extends immediately to the case in which the one-particle and two-particle coefficients depend on
N
, provided they satisfy the same growth bounds uniformly in
N
and converge locally uniformly on compact sets. This is exactly the pattern of the general convergence theorem in Małecki and Pérez
[
33
]
. Since no new idea is needed here, we omit the proof and leave it to the reader.

Corollary 1

(Mean-field convergence with converging coefficients)
.

Let the finite systems be given by (
2.25
), with the same
1/N
mean-field scaling, but with
\sigma,b,k
replaced by coefficients
\sigma_{N},b_{N},k_{N}
. Assume
(MF1)
and
(MF3)
. Assume also that
k_{N}
is symmetric, that all coefficients satisfy the growth bounds (
2.26
)-(
2.27
) uniformly in
N
, and that, for every
T,R>0
,

(2.29)

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}0\leq t\leq T\\
|x|\leq R\end{subarray}}\left|\sigma_{N}^{2}(t,x)-\sigma^{2}(t,x)\right|

\displaystyle=0,

(2.30)

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}0\leq t\leq T\\
|x|\leq R\end{subarray}}|b_{N}(t,x)-b(t,x)|

\displaystyle=0,

(2.31)

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}0\leq t\leq T\\
|x|\leq R,\ |y|\leq R\end{subarray}}|k_{N}(t,x,y)-k(t,x,y)|

\displaystyle=0,

(2.32)

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}0\leq t\leq T\\
0\leq x\leq R\end{subarray}}|k_{N}(t,x)-k(t,x)|

\displaystyle=0.

Then the conclusions of Theorem
8
remain valid, with the same limiting equation (
2.28
).

3.
Preliminaries and notations

3.1.
Root systems

Let
E
be the Euclidean state space. We shall use finite reduced root systems, possibly in a non-essential ambient realization. More precisely, for a finite set
R\subset E\setminus\{0\}
we put
E_{R}:=\operatorname{span}R
.We assume that
R
, viewed as a subset of the Euclidean space
E_{R}
, is a reduced root system, that is, it satisfies the following properties:

(1)

R
is finite and spans
E_{R}
.

(2)

If
\alpha\in R
, then the only multiples of
\alpha
in
R
are
\alpha
and
-\alpha
.

(3)

For every
\alpha\in R
, the reflection
s_{\alpha}
preserves
R
.

(4)

For any two roots
\alpha,\beta\in R
, the quantity
\frac{2\left<\beta,\alpha\right>}{\left<\alpha,\alpha\right>}
is an integer.

The case
E_{R}=E
is the essential realization. If
E_{R}\neq E
, the roots are regarded as vectors in the larger state space
E
, and the orthogonal complement
E_{R}^{\perp}
is left unconstrained by the root system. The reflections are then understood as the corresponding orthogonal reflections in
E
, acting trivially on
E_{R}^{\perp}
.

The reflection
s_{\alpha}
across the hyperplane perpendicular to a root
\alpha\in R
is given by the formula:

(3.1)

s_{\alpha}(x)=x-\frac{2\left<x,\alpha\right>}{|\alpha|^{2}}\alpha\/,\quad x\in E\/.

We denote by
R_{+}
the collection of positive roots. We specify those roots in the following with regard to the individual root systems we are considering. In general, the set of roots is divided into positive and negative roots in such a way that for given root
\alpha\in R
it is either positive or negative, and then
-\alpha
is then negative or positive, respectively. However, when fixed
\alpha,\beta\in R
there exists a unique choice of sign such that
\pm s_{\alpha}(\beta)
is in
R_{+}
. We denote the resulting root (
\alpha
reflected in
\beta
then a chosen sign) as
s^{+}_{\alpha}(\beta)
. Then we immediately obtain

\langle s^{+}_{\beta}(\alpha),\beta\rangle=\mp\left<\alpha,\beta\right>\/,\quad\langle x,s^{+}_{\beta}(\alpha)\rangle=\pm\left(\left<x,\alpha\right>-\frac{2\left<\alpha,\beta\right>\left<x,\beta\right>}{|\beta|^{2}}\right)\/,

and consequently, we arrive at one of the most crucial relations, which we will exploit very often in the sequel

(3.2)

\left<\alpha,\beta\right>\left<x,\beta\right>+\langle s^{+}_{\beta}(\alpha),\beta\rangle\langle x,s^{+}_{\beta}(\alpha)\rangle=\frac{2\left<\alpha,\beta\right>^{2}\left<x,\beta\right>}{|\beta|^{2}}\/.

The
positive Weyl chamber
, denoted
C_{+}
, is the region of
E
where vectors have a positive inner product with all simple roots. It is given by:

C_{+}=\{x\in E:\left<x,\alpha\right>>0\text{ for all }\alpha\in R_{+}\}\/.

A root system is said to be
irreducible
if it cannot be partitioned into two nonempty, mutually orthogonal subsets. We will consider the finite classical root systems
A_{N-1}
,
B_{N}
, and
D_{N}
. Given the general form of the root systems we are considering, types
B_{N}
and
C_{N}
lead to exactly the same systems of stochastic differential equations (
1.1
), and therefore we do not consider type
C_{N}
separately, since it is fully covered by the
B_{N}
case.

Throughout the paper the stochastic process is
N
-dimensional, and we realize all three systems in the ambient space
E=\mathbb{R}^{N}
, with standard basis
e_{1},\ldots,e_{N}
. In types
B_{N}
and
D_{N}
the roots span all of
\mathbb{R}^{N}
. In type
A_{N-1}
, however, the roots
\pm(e_{i}-e_{j})
span only the hyperplane
H_{N}:=\left\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{N}:\sum_{i=1}^{N}x_{i}=0\right\}
. Thus type
A_{N-1}
is used in its standard non-essential realization in
\mathbb{R}^{N}
: the root system acts on the difference coordinates, while the direction
\mathbb{R}(1,\ldots,1)
is the unconstrained center-of-mass direction. Accordingly, the type
A_{N-1}
chamber below is the inverse image in
\mathbb{R}^{N}
of the essential
A_{N-1}
Weyl chamber in
H_{N}
.

The specified positive root systems and positive Weyl chambers in the considered
three types are given below.

1.

Root system of type
A_{N-1}
:

Roots:

\displaystyle R=\{\pm(e_{i}-e_{j}):1\leq i<j\leq N\}

Positive Roots:

\displaystyle R_{+}=\{e_{i}-e_{j}:1\leq i<j\leq N\}

Positive Weyl Chamber:

\displaystyle C_{+}=\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{N}:x_{1}>x_{2}>\dots>x_{N}\}

2.

Root system of type
B_{N}
:

Roots:

\displaystyle R=\{\pm e_{i}:1\leq i\leq N\}\cup\{\pm(e_{i}-e_{j}),\pm(e_{i}+e_{j}):1\leq i<j\leq N\}

Positive Roots:

\displaystyle R_{+}=\{e_{i}:1\leq i\leq N\}\cup\{e_{i}\pm e_{j}:1\leq i<j\leq N\}

Positive Weyl Chamber:

\displaystyle C_{+}=\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{N}:x_{1}>x_{2}>\dots>x_{N}>0\}

3.

Root system of type
D_{N}
:

Roots:

\displaystyle R=\{\pm(e_{i}-e_{j}),\pm(e_{i}+e_{j}):1\leq i<j\leq N\}

Positive Roots:

\displaystyle R_{+}=\{e_{i}\pm e_{j}:1\leq i<j\leq N\}

Positive Weyl Chamber:

\displaystyle C_{+}=\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{N}:x_{1}>x_{2}>\dots>x_{N-1}>|x_{N}|\}

For every fixed
A\subseteq R_{+}
and every fixed
\alpha\in R_{+}
we define the following sets of positive roots

(3.3)

\displaystyle N_{\alpha}^{(1)}(A)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\{\beta\in A:\beta\neq\alpha,\left<\alpha,\beta\right>\neq 0\}\/,

(3.4)

\displaystyle N_{\alpha}^{(2)}(A)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\{(\beta,\gamma):\beta,\gamma\in A:\beta\neq\gamma,\left<\alpha,\beta\right>\neq 0,s^{+}_{\gamma}(\beta)=\alpha\}

3.2.
Symmetric polynomials

For a given set
A=\{a_{1},\ldots,a_{p}\}
,
p\in\mathbb{N}
, we denote by
e_{n}(A)
,
n=1,\ldots,p
the basic symmetric polynomials in variables
a_{1},\ldots,a_{p}
, i.e.

e_{n}(A)=\sum_{i_{1}<\ldots<i_{n}}a_{i_{1}}\cdot\ldots\cdot a_{i_{n}}\/,\quad n=1,\ldots,p\/.

We also define the corresponding power sums
p_{k}(A)
,
k=1,2,3,\ldots
by

p_{k}(A)=\frac{1}{k}\sum_{i=1}^{p}a_{i}^{k}\/.

We introduce the normalizing factor
1/k
in the definition to make some of the calculations appearing in the next sections shorter and more transparent.

3.3.
Clusters and collision points

We begin the section with an introduction of clusters, which is a concept helping to deal with collisions of the process with the Weyl chamber walls.

Definition 1
.

A subset
\varphi
of
R_{+}
is called
a cluster
, if there exists
k\in\{1,\ldots,N\}
such that
\alpha_{k}\neq 0
for every
\alpha\in\varphi
.

Based on the above definition, the clusters in
A_{N-1}

are

\varphi_{1}=\{e_{2}-e_{3},e_{3}-e_{4}\}\qquad\varphi_{2}=\{e_{1}-e_{2},e_{1}-e_{6},e_{1}-e_{8}\}

as well as the following are the clusters in
B_{N}

\varphi_{3}=\{e_{1},e_{1}-e_{3}\}\qquad\varphi_{4}=\{e_{2},e_{2}+e_{3},e_{2}-e_{4}\}

We will denote by
\Phi
a family of all clusters and
|\varphi|
the length of a cluster, which is just the number of elements in the cluster. A cluster with length
1
will be called
singular cluster
and
multiple cluster
is a cluster with length greater than
1
. Finally, for each
m\in\mathbb{N}
we introduce the set of clusters with fixed length

\Phi^{(m)}=\{\varphi\in\Phi:|\varphi|=m\}\/.

For a given cluster
\varphi\in\Phi
we denote by
R_{+}(\varphi)
the set of all positive roots, which can be obtained as a reflection of the cluster elements in the cluster elements (with suitable change of sign if necessary).

R_{+}(\varphi)=\varphi\cup\{s^{+}_{\beta}(\alpha)\in R_{+}:\alpha,\beta\in\varphi\}\/,

where we recall that
s^{+}_{\beta}(\alpha)=\pm s_{\alpha}(\beta)
and the sign is chosen in a way to ensure that we obtain a positive root. For example we get

\displaystyle R_{+}(\varphi_{1})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\{e_{2}-e_{3},e_{3}-e_{4},e_{2}-e_{4}\}

\displaystyle R_{+}(\varphi_{2})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\{e_{1}-e_{2},e_{1}-e_{6},e_{1}-e_{8},e_{2}-e_{6},e_{2}-e_{8},e_{6}-e_{8}\}

\displaystyle R_{+}(\varphi_{4})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\{e_{2},e_{3},e_{4},e_{2}\pm e_{3},e_{2}\pm e_{4},e_{3}\pm e_{4}\}

For every boundary point
y\in\partial C_{+}
, we define clusters related to a given boundary point as

\Phi(y)=\{\varphi\in\Phi:\left<y,\alpha\right>=0\textrm{ for all }\alpha\in\varphi\}.

Therefore, we can decompose the boundary of the Weyl chamber according to the size of the longest cluster in
\Phi(y)
. For every
m=1,\ldots,s
, we define

\partial C_{+}^{(m)}=\{y\in\partial C_{+}:\max_{\varphi\in\Phi(y)}|\varphi|=m\}\/.

Then the boundary
\partial C_{+}
is a disjoint union of the above-given sets.

\partial C_{+}=\bigcup_{m=1}^{s}\partial C_{+}^{(m)}\/.

The size of the longest possible cluster
s
depends on
N
and the kind of a root system we are dealing with, but its specific value is not important for our consideration. We call the boundary points in
\partial C_{+}^{(m)}

a multiple collision point of order
m
, whenever
m\geq 2
. If
y\in\partial C_{+}^{(1)}
, then it is called
a single collision point
. Recall that at a single collision point we can see many
\alpha\in R_{+}
such that
\left<y,\alpha\right>=0
, but none of them form a cluster of length greater than or equal to
2
. In other words, there might be simultaneous singular collisions, but not multiple ones.

3.4.
Notation

For a given diffusion
Z=(Z(t))
we define its life-time by

\zeta^{Z}=\inf\{t:|Z(t)|=\infty\}=\lim_{R\to\infty}\inf\{t:|Z(t)|\geq R\}\/.

If additionally
Z
is a continuous semi-martingale, then we denote by
M^{Z}
its local martingale part and
A^{Z}
will stand for the finite variation part and consequently

Z(t)=Z(0)+M^{Z}(t)+A^{Z}(t)

for every
t<\zeta^{Z}
. Moreover, we denote the first hitting time of a set
D\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{N}
by
Z
as

\tau_{D}^{Z}=\inf\{t:Z(t)\in D\}\/.

Whenever we consider hitting a single point
z
, we will simplify the notation by writing
\tau_{z}^{Z}
.

4.
Invariant polynomials and construction of a candidate solution

The core idea of our construction is to leverage the similarities between multivariate Bessel-Dunkl diffusions on
\overline{C_{+}}
and Bessel processes on the half-line
[0,\infty)
. Recall that the classical definition of Bessel processes involves starting with a stochastic differential equation (SDE) for squared Bessel processes, where the drift term is non-singular. Subsequently, by applying the inverse function
x\to\sqrt{x}
, Bessel processes are defined, and it is then shown that they satisfy a corresponding SDE. We adopt a similar approach, beginning with an SDE for invariant polynomials, which serve as the multidimensional analogue of the square function used in the classical Bessel process derivation.

4.1.
Stochastic description of symmetric polynomials

Recall that the idea of constructing a solution based on invariant polynomials was introduced in
[
22
]
for the root system
A_{N-1}
. Since we want to cover also remaining root systems, we have to expand this approach and choose family of polynomials suitable for every considered root system. However, to unify the notation, we define

\displaystyle F_{\alpha}^{(k)}(x)=\begin{cases}\sum\limits_{j=0}^{k-2}x_{n}^{j}x_{m}^{k-2-j},&\text{if $\alpha=r_{n}-r_{m}$ and $k=1,2,\ldots$}\/,\\[8.1805pt]
\sum\limits_{j=0}^{k-2}x_{n}^{j}(-x_{m})^{k-2-j},&\text{if $\alpha=r_{n}+r_{m}$ and $k=2,4,6,\ldots$}\/,\\[8.1805pt]
x_{n}^{k-2},&\text{if $\alpha=r_{n}$ and $k=2,4,6,\ldots$}\/,\end{cases}

which is obviously continuous in
x
for every
\alpha
and
k
.

Going back to the stochastic description of the invariant polynomials, we take the starting point
x(0)
in the interior of the Weyl chamber
C_{+}
and we consider
X(t)=(X_{1}(t),\ldots,X_{N}(t))
as a solution to (
1.2
) up to the first hitting time
T_{C}=\inf\{t:X(t)\in\partial C\}
. Consequently, using the Itô formula, we can write SDEs for the related polynomials
p_{k}(x)
for
t<T_{C}
as follows

\displaystyle dp_{k}\left(X\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{N}X_{i}^{k-1}dX_{i}+\frac{k-1}{2}\sum_{i=1}^{N}X_{i}^{k-2}dX_{i}dX_{i}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{N}X_{i}^{k-1}\sigma_{i}\left(t,X\right)dB_{i}+\frac{k-1}{2}X_{i}^{k-2}\sigma_{i}^{2}\left(t,X\right)dt

\displaystyle\quad+\sum_{i=1}^{N}\left[X_{i}^{k-1}b_{i}\left(t,X\right)+X_{i}^{k-1}\sum_{\alpha\in R_{+}}\dfrac{k_{\alpha}(t,X)\alpha_{i}}{\left<X,\alpha\right>}\right]dt.

The crucial observation is that the singular expression
k_{\alpha}(t,x)/\left<x,\alpha\right>
disappear for the carefully chosen
k
. Indeed, for every
\alpha=r_{n}-r_{m}
and every
k=1,2,\ldots
we have

\sum_{i=1}^{N}x_{i}^{k-1}\alpha_{i}=x_{n}^{k-1}-x_{m}^{k-1}=(x_{n}-x_{m})\sum_{j=0}^{k-2}x_{n}^{j}x_{m}^{k-2-j}=\left<x,\alpha\right>F_{\alpha}^{(k)}(x)\/.

Moreover, for even
k
and
\alpha=r_{n}+r_{m}
we obtain

\sum_{i=1}^{N}x_{i}^{k-1}\alpha_{i}=x_{n}^{k-1}+x_{m}^{k-1}=x_{n}^{k-1}-(-x_{m})^{k-1}=(x_{n}+x_{m})\sum_{j=0}^{k-2}x_{n}^{j}(-x_{m})^{k-2-j}=\left<x,\alpha\right>F_{\alpha}^{(k)}(x)\/.

Finally, for
\alpha=r_{n}
and
k\geq 2
we simply get

\sum_{i=1}^{N}x_{i}^{k-1}\alpha_{i}=x_{n}^{k-1}=x_{n}\cdot x_{n}^{k-2}=\left<x,\alpha\right>F_{\alpha}^{(k)}(x)\/.

Collecting all together we arrive at

\displaystyle dp_{k}\left(X\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{N}X_{i}^{k-1}\sigma_{i}\left(t,X\right)dB_{i}+\sum_{i=1}^{N}\left(X_{i}^{k-1}b_{i}\left(t,X\right)+\frac{k-1}{2}X_{i}^{k-2}\sigma_{i}^{2}\left(t,X\right)\right)dt

(4.1)

\displaystyle\quad+\sum_{\alpha\in R_{+}}F_{\alpha,k}\left(X\right)k_{\alpha}(t,X)dt\/,

where all the functions appearing above are continuous.

Although the symmetric polynomials
p_{k}(x)
are sufficient to define a solution in
A_{N-1}
and
B_{N}
cases, we need to consider
e_{N}(x)
, which is the product
x_{1}\cdot,\ldots\cdot x_{N}
, in the
D_{N}
case. Proceeding as previously we apply the Itô formula to arrive at the following SDEs for
e_{N}(x)
assuming the start from the interior of the Weyl chamber and working up to the first hitting time of its boundary

de_{N}\left(X\right)=\sum_{i=1}^{N}e_{N-1}^{\overline{x_{i}}}(X)dX_{i}=\sum_{i=1}^{N}e_{N-1}^{\overline{x_{i}}}\left(X\right)\left(\sigma_{i}\left(t,X\right)dB_{i}+b_{i}\left(t,X\right)dt+\sum_{\alpha\in R_{+}}\frac{\alpha_{i}k_{\alpha}(t,X)}{\left<X,\alpha\right>}dt\right)

Once again the singularity can be removed. For every
\alpha=r_{n}\pm r_{m}
using the relation
e_{N-1}^{\overline{x_{n}}}(x)=x_{m}e_{N-2}^{\overline{x_{n}},\overline{x_{m}}}(x)
we obtain

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{N}\alpha_{i}e_{N-1}^{\overline{x_{i}}}(x)=\alpha_{n}e_{N-1}^{\overline{x_{n}}}(x)+\alpha_{m}e_{N-1}^{\overline{x_{m}}}(x)=(\alpha_{n}x_{n}+\alpha_{m}x_{m})e_{N-2}^{\overline{x_{n}},\overline{x_{m}}}(x)=\left<x,\alpha\right>e_{N-2}^{\overline{x_{n}},\overline{x_{m}}}(x)

and consequently setting
G_{\alpha,N}(x)=e_{N-2}^{\overline{x_{n}},\overline{x_{m}}}(x)
for
\alpha=r_{n}\pm r_{m}
we arrive at

(4.2)

de_{N}\left(X\right)=\sum_{i=1}^{N}e_{N-1}^{\overline{x_{i}}}\left(X\right)\sigma_{i}\left(t,X\right)dB_{i}+\sum_{i=1}^{N}e_{N-1}^{\overline{x_{i}}}\left(X\right)b_{i}\left(t,X\right)dt+\sum_{\alpha\in R_{+}}G_{\alpha,N}\left(X\right)k_{\alpha}(t,X)dt\/.

Remark 2
.

We can also remove the singularity from the SDEs for every symmetric polynomial
e_{n}(x)
,
n=1\ldots,N
in the
A_{N-1}
case. Indeed, the similar computations as above but involving the general formula
e_{n-1}^{\overline{x_{n}}}(x)=x_{m}e_{n-2}^{\overline{x_{n}},\overline{x_{m}}}(x)+e_{n-1}^{\overline{x_{n}},\overline{x_{m}}}(x)
(note that for
n=N
the last expression vanishes) lead to

\sum_{i=1}^{N}\alpha_{i}e_{n-1}^{\overline{x_{i}}}(x)=\left<x,\alpha\right>e_{n-2}^{\overline{x_{n}},\overline{x_{m}}}(x)+e_{n-1}^{\overline{x_{n}},\overline{x_{m}}}(x)\sum_{i=1}^{N}\alpha_{i}=\left<x,\alpha\right>e_{n-2}^{\overline{x_{n}},\overline{x_{m}}}(x)\/,

where the last equations is a consequence of the fact that
\sum_{i=1}^{N}\alpha_{i}=0
for every
\alpha=r_{n}-r_{m}
. Consequently, we have

de_{n}\left(X\right)=\sum_{i=1}^{N}e_{n-1}^{\overline{x_{i}}}\left(X\right)\left(\sigma_{i}\left(t,X\right)dB_{i}+b_{i}\left(t,X\right)dt\right)+\sum_{\alpha\in R_{+}}G_{\alpha,n}\left(X\right)k_{\alpha}(t,x)dt\/,

for every
n=1,\ldots,N
,
G_{\alpha,n}(x)=e_{n-2}^{\overline{x_{n}},\overline{x_{m}}}(x)
for
\alpha=r_{n}-r_{m}\in A_{N-1}^{+}
.

4.2.
Basic invariant polynomials

For every considered root system, we choose the corresponding basic invariant polynomials
w=(w_{1},\ldots,w_{N})
indicated in
[
31
]
. Since for the
A_{N}
root system the basic invariant polynomials are just
e_{1},\ldots,e_{N}
we will take
w_{k}=p_{k}
for
k=1,\ldots,N
since it is more convenient for further calculations to work with the power sums than with
e_{k}
polynomials. The set of basic invariant polynomials in the
B_{N}
case can be chosen as power sums of squares of
x_{1},\ldots,x_{N}
, i.e. we define
w_{k}=p_{2k}
for
k=1,\ldots,N
in this case. Finally, for
D_{N}
root system we set
w_{k}=p_{2k}
for
k=1,\ldots,N-1
as in the
B_{N}
case, but the last polynomial must be defined as
w_{N}=e_{N}
to have a control on the sign of the smallest particle
x_{N}
.

Such choice of
w=(w_{1},\ldots,w_{N})
ensure that there is one-to-one correspondence between
x=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{N})
and
(w_{1},\ldots,w_{N})
whenever we restrict our consideration to the positive Weyl chamber
C
, i.e. the smooth function

w=(w_{1},\ldots,w_{N}):C\longrightarrow w[C]

is one-to-one, where
w[C]
stands for the image of
C
by
w
. To see this note that in the
A_{N-1}
case it just follows from the fact that there is one-to-one relation between
(p_{1},\ldots,p_{N})
and
(e_{1},\ldots,e_{N})
, which (with suitable sign) are coefficients of the characteristic polynomial
P(x)=\prod_{i=1}^{N}(x-x_{i})
, i.e. there is one-to one relation between coefficients of the polynomial and its ordered roots. In the
B_{N}
case the same argument shows that there is one-to-one relation between
(w_{1},\ldots,w_{N})
and
(x_{1}^{2},\ldots,x_{N}^{2})
and since here we are restricted to the positive
x_{i}
’s we get the claim. Finally, in the
D_{N}
case, knowing
(w_{1},\ldots,w_{N})
we can determine
(p_{2},p_{4},\ldots,p_{2N})
. From
p_{2},\ldots,p_{2N-2}
we determine the first
N-1
elementary symmetric polynomials of
y_{i}=x_{i}^{2}
, and from
w_{N}=e_{N}(x)
we determine the last one, namely
\prod_{i}y_{i}=e_{N}(x)^{2}=w_{N}^{2}
. Hence we determine the unordered set
\{x_{1}^{2},\ldots,x_{N}^{2}\}
. It gives
(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3},\ldots,|x_{N}|)
, but using
w_{N}=x_{1}\cdot\ldots\cdot x_{N}
we can determine the sign of
x_{N}
.

Summing these up we can define the inverse diffeomorphism between
C
and
w[C]

f:w[C_{+}]\stackrel{{\scriptstyle 1-1}}{{\longrightarrow}}C_{+}\/,

which is smooth on
w[C_{+}]
can be continuously extended to

f:\overline{w[C_{+}]}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle 1-1}}{{\longrightarrow}}\overline{C_{+}}\/.

Moreover, there exists extension of
f
on
\mathbb{R}^{N}
, which is obviously not
1-1
, but remains continuous

(4.3)

\tilde{f}:\mathbb{R}^{N}\longrightarrow\overline{C_{+}}

and such that
\tilde{f}[(w[C_{+}])^{c}]=\partial C_{+}
. Starting with
A_{N-1}
recall that the function
g:C_{+}^{N}\to C_{+}^{N}
giving the relation between coefficients of a polynomial and its (suitable ordered) complex roots is continuous (see
[
32
]
) and in particular the function

\mathbb{R}^{N}\ni a=(a_{1},\ldots,a_{N})\longrightarrow\tilde{f}(a)=H(\Re(g_{1}(a)),\ldots\Re(g_{N}(a)))\/,

is continuous, where
H(b_{1},\ldots,b_{N})=(b_{\pi(1)},\ldots,b_{\pi(N)})
is the ordering permutation, i.e. we have
b_{\pi(i)}\geq b_{\pi(i+1)}
,
i=1,\ldots,N-1
. Observe also that if
a\in{w[C]}
, then the polynomial with coefficients
a_{1},\ldots,a_{N}
has all real and distinct roots and
\tilde{f}(a)=f(a)
. If
a\in\partial w[C]
, then we still have all real roots of the polynomial, but some of them are multiple roots, i.e.
\tilde{f}(a)\in\partial C
. Finally, if
a\notin\overline{w[C]}
, then we have a polynomial with real coefficients, but some of its roots are complex. However, since the coefficients are real, for every complex root its conjugate is also a root, which means that some of
\Re(g_{i}(a)
are equal, i.e.
\tilde{f}(a)\in\partial C
. Thus we have just constructed the desired extension (
4.3
) for
A_{N-1}
. For
B_{N}
root system we just have to compose this with square root function
x\to\sqrt{\textrm{max}(x,0)}
on every coordinate and
D_{N}
case requires additionally to observe that
x_{N}=\textrm{sgn}(w_{N})|x_{N}|
.

To unify all the considered cases we introduce for
R=A_{N-1}
and every
k=1,\ldots,N
the following notation

a_{i,k}^{R}(t,x)=x_{i}^{k-1}\sigma_{i}(t,x)\/,\quad h_{i,k}^{R}(t,x)=x_{i}^{k-1}b_{i}(t,x)+\frac{k-1}{2}x_{i}^{k-2}\sigma_{i}^{2}(t,x)\/,\quad H_{\alpha,k}^{R}(x)=F_{\alpha,k}(x)\/.

Since for
R=B_{N}
we just have
u_{k}=p_{2k}
, for every
k=1,\ldots,N
and the same polynomials are chosen for
R=D_{N}
for
k=1,\ldots,N-1
, so in those cases we set

a_{i,k}^{R}(t,x)=x_{i}^{2k-1}\sigma_{i}(t,x)\/,\quad h_{i,k}^{R}(t,x)=x_{i}^{2k-1}b_{i}(t,x)+\frac{2k-1}{2}x_{i}^{2k-2}\sigma_{i}^{2}(t,x)\/,\quad H_{\alpha,k}^{R}(x)=F_{\alpha,2k}(x)\/.

Finally, for
R=D_{N}
and
k=N
we have

a_{i,N}^{R}(t,x)=e_{N-1}^{\overline{x_{i}}}(x)\sigma_{i}(t,x)\/,\quad h_{i,N}^{R}(t,x)=e_{N-1}^{\overline{x_{i}}}(x)b_{i}(t,x)\/,\quad H_{\alpha,N}^{R}(x)=G_{\alpha,N}(x)\/.

Now we can define
U=(U_{1},\ldots,U_{N})
as solution to appropriate SDEs of the form (
4.1
) and/or (
4.2
) with
X
replaced by
\tilde{f}(U)
, which, according to the previous considerations, are SDEs with continuous coefficients.

Definition 2
.

For a given collection of independent one-dimensional Brownian motions

(B_{1},\ldots,B_{N})
we define
U=(U_{1},\ldots,U_{N})
as a solution to the system

(4.4)

dU_{k}=\sum_{i=1}^{N}a_{i,k}^{R}(t,\tilde{f}(U))\,dB_{i}+\sum_{i=1}^{N}h_{i,k}^{R}(t,\tilde{f}(U))\,dt+\sum_{\alpha\in R_{+}}H_{\alpha,k}^{R}(\tilde{f}(U))\,k_{\alpha}(t,\tilde{f}(U))\,dt,

for every
k=1,\ldots,N
, with initial condition
U(0)=u_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{N}
.

Remark 3
.

The existence of a solution of (
4.4
) follows from the continuity of
\tilde{f}
on
\mathbb{R}^{N}
and consequently from the continuity of the coefficients. Similar arguments were used in the construction presented in
[
22
]
. However two arguments were missing in the proof presented there, which was pointed out to the author by Prof. Michael Voit. First is the definition of the extension of
f
. Second is showing that in fact we can choose
U
to stay in the set
\overline{w[C_{+}]}
. In the present paper both problems are solved in much more general setting, which keeps all the results from
[
22
]
staying in charge.

Let
\zeta^{U}
denote the lifetime of the solution
U
of (
4.4
). We set

(4.5)

\mathcal{K}:=\overline{w[C_{+}]}\subset\mathbb{R}^{N},\qquad\tau_{\mathcal{K}}:=\inf\{0\leq t<\zeta^{U}:U(t)\notin\mathcal{K}\},

with the convention that the infimum of the empty set is
\zeta^{U}
.

Definition 3
.

Let
x_{0}\in\overline{C_{+}}
and put
u_{0}=w(x_{0})
. Let
U
be a solution of the invariant-coordinate equation from Definition
2
, started from
U(0)=u_{0}
, and let
\zeta^{U}
be its lifetime. If among such solutions there exists one satisfying
\tau_{\mathcal{K}}=\zeta^{U}
a.s., then, in the construction below, we choose such a solution
U
.
We define the candidate chamber-valued process by

(4.6)

X(t)=\tilde{f}(U(t)),\qquad 0\leq t<\zeta^{U}.

We also put
\zeta^{X}:=\zeta^{U}
.

On the stochastic interval
[0,\tau_{\mathcal{K}})
we have

U(t)\in\mathcal{K}
, and therefore

(4.7)

X(t)=\tilde{f}(U(t))=f(U(t)),\qquad U(t)=w(X(t)),\qquad 0\leq t<\tau_{\mathcal{K}}.

Thus, before the no-exit result is proved, all particle computations are understood with the additional stopping at
\tau_{\mathcal{K}}
.

Remark 4
.

Let
p
be a polynomial invariant under the Weyl group corresponding to the root system
R
. By the choice of the basic invariant polynomials
w_{1},\ldots,w_{N}
, there exists a polynomial
\widehat{p}\in\mathbb{R}[u_{1},\ldots,u_{N}]
such that

p(x)=\widehat{p}(w_{1}(x),\ldots,w_{N}(x)),\qquad x\in C_{+}.

Hence, on
[0,\tau_{\mathcal{K}})
,

p(X(t))=\widehat{p}(U(t)).

The rigorous way to compute
p(X)
on this stopped interval is therefore to apply Itô’s formula to the smooth polynomial
\widehat{p}(U)
. In the sequel we shall often use the shorter symbolic notation obtained by applying Itô’s formula formally in the particle variables
x
. This is only a shorthand on
[0,\tau_{\mathcal{K}})
: on the open chamber the map
w:C_{+}\to w[C_{+}]
is a smooth diffeomorphism with inverse
f
, and the identity
p\circ f=\widehat{p}
shows, by the chain rule, that the first- and second-order terms coincide with those obtained from Itô’s formula for
\widehat{p}(U)
. After Proposition
3
below is proved,
\tau_{\mathcal{K}}=\zeta^{X}
and the same shorthand is valid for all
t<\zeta^{X}
.

5.
Collisions and preservation of the invariant chamber image

The main difficulty in showing that the process constructed through the invariant-coordinate equation is a genuine solution of the particle system is the behavior at the boundary of the Weyl chamber. In this section we prove that the invariant-coordinate process does not leave the closed invariant chamber image
\mathcal{K}=\overline{w[C_{+}]}

The proof is organized as follows. We first prove a stopped no-multiple-collision result, valid for interior initial points up to the first possible exit time
\tau_{\mathcal{K}}
. This is then used to prove that
\tau_{\mathcal{K}}=\zeta^{X}
whenever the process starts from the interior
C_{+}
. Boundary initial points are handled by approximation from the interior. Finally, we prove instantaneous diffraction from the boundary and deduce the full no-multiple-collision result after the initial time.

5.1.
Multiple collision points

Recall the definitions related to clusters and multiply collision points presented in Subsection
3.3
. We begin with introducing some additional functions and symmetric polynomials, which enable us to control visits to multiple collision points. First, for given cluster
\varphi\in\Phi
, we define

r^{\varphi}(x)=\sum_{\alpha\in R_{+}(\varphi)}\left<x,\alpha\right>^{2}\/,\quad x\in\overline{C_{+}}\/.

We also introduce the following function, which up to the constant
2
, is the derivative of
r^{\varphi}(x)
with respect to
x_{k}

h^{\varphi}_{k}(x)=\sum_{\alpha\in R_{+}(\varphi)}\alpha_{k}\left<x,\alpha\right>\/,\quad x\in\overline{C_{+}}\/,\quad k=1,\ldots,N\/.

together with

(5.1)

h^{\varphi}_{\delta}(x)=\sum_{k=1}^{N}\delta_{k}h^{\varphi}_{k}(x)=\sum_{\alpha\in R_{+}(\varphi)}\left<\alpha,\delta\right>\left<x,\alpha\right>\/.

Please note that the sum in the definition of
r^{\varphi}(y)
is over all elements generated by the given cluster. In particular, if
x
is an element of the boundary and
\varphi
is a cluster of
\Phi(x)
, then the function
r^{\varphi}(x)
is zero. Conversely, if
r^{\varphi}(x)=0
for some cluster
\varphi\in\Phi
and
x\in\overline{C_{+}}
, then
x\in\partial C_{+}
and
\varphi\in\Phi(x)
. Consequently, defining

(5.2)

q_{m}(x)=\prod_{\varphi\in\Phi^{(m)}}r^{\varphi}(x)\/,\quad m=1,\ldots,s

we obtain a symmetric polynomial, which controls if the point
x
is an element of
\partial C_{+}^{(m)}
. In fact, if
x\in\partial C_{+}^{(m)}
, then there exists a cluster
\varphi
of length
m
in
\Phi(x)
, which implies
r^{\varphi}(x)=0
