and consequently
q_{m}(x)=0
. Conversely, if
q_{m}(x)=0
, then there exists
\varphi\in\Phi^{(m)}
such that
r^{\varphi}(x)=0
, which implies
x\in\partial C_{+}^{(j)}
with
j\geq m
.

In what follows, we focus on the stochastic description and the properties of the symmetric polynomial process defined as

(5.3)

q_{m}(X)=\prod_{\varphi\in\Phi^{(m)}}r^{\varphi}(X)\/.

As we have discussed previously, the process
q_{m}(X)
controls, in some way, the collisions between particles, which makes it natural to study its first hitting time of zero

\tau_{\,0}^{m}:=\tau_{0}^{q_{m}(X)}=\inf\{t\geq 0:q_{m}(X(t))=0\}\/.

We will use the classical approach to study the hitting time considering its logarithm
Q_{m}=-\ln(q_{m}(X))
and its explosion time
\tau_{\infty}^{Q_{m}}
.

Proposition 1
.

Let
q_{m}(X)
be defined in (
5.3
) and assume that
X(0)=x_{0}\in\overline{C_{+}}
is such that
q_{m}(x_{0})>0
. Then, for
t<\tau_{\,0}^{m}\wedge\tau_{\mathcal{K}}
, the process
Q_{m}=-\ln q_{m}(X)
is a continuous semi-martingale with the local martingale part

(5.4)

dM^{Q_{m}}=-2\sum_{\varphi\in\Phi^{(m)}}\frac{1}{r^{\varphi}(x)}\sum_{k=1}^{N}h^{\varphi}_{k}(X)\sigma_{k}(X)dB_{k}

and the drift giving as a sum
dA^{Q_{m}}=h_{1}(X)dt+h_{2}(X)dt+h_{3}(X)dt
, where

(5.5)

\displaystyle h_{1}(x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{\varphi\in\Phi^{(m)}}\frac{1}{(r^{\varphi}(x))^{2}}\sum_{k=1}^{N}\left(2(h^{\varphi}_{k}(x))^{2}-r^{\varphi}(x)\sum_{\alpha\in R_{+}(\varphi)}\alpha_{k}^{2}\right)\sigma_{k}^{2}(x),

(5.6)

\displaystyle h_{2}(x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-2\sum_{\varphi\in\Phi^{(m)}}\frac{1}{r^{\varphi}(x)}\sum_{k=1}^{N}h^{\varphi}_{k}(x)b_{k}(x),

(5.7)

\displaystyle h_{3}(x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-2\sum_{\varphi\in\Phi^{(m)}}\frac{1}{r^{\varphi}(x)}\sum_{\delta\in R_{+}}\frac{k_{\delta}(t,x)h^{\varphi}_{\delta}(x)}{\left<x,\delta\right>}\/,

Proof.

Since we simply have
\ln q_{m}(x)=\sum_{\varphi\in\Phi^{(m)}}\ln r^{\varphi}(x)
, the Itô formula implies

dQ_{m}=-\sum_{\varphi\in\Phi^{(m)}}d\ln r^{\varphi}(x)=-\sum_{\varphi\in\Phi^{(m)}}\left(\frac{1}{r^{\varphi}(x)}dr^{\varphi}(x)-\dfrac{1}{2}\frac{dr^{\varphi}(x)dr^{\varphi}(x)}{(r^{\varphi}(x))^{2}}\right)\/,

which gives the local martingale part
dM^{Q_{m}}
of the form (
5.4
) and the drift part is represented as a sum
dA^{Q_{m}}=h_{1}(X)dt+h_{2}(X)dt+h_{3}(X)dt
, where
h_{1}
and
h_{2}
are given in (
5.5
) and (
5.6
) respectively. For the last part we just have

\displaystyle h_{3}(x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-2\sum_{\varphi\in\Phi^{(m)}}\frac{1}{r^{\varphi}(x)}\sum_{k=1}^{N}h^{\varphi}_{k}(x)\sum_{\delta\in R_{+}}\frac{k_{\delta}(t,x)\delta_{k}}{\left<x,\delta\right>}=-2\sum_{\varphi\in\Phi^{(m)}}\frac{1}{r^{\varphi}(x)}\sum_{\delta\in R_{+}}\frac{k_{\delta}(t,x)h^{\varphi}_{\delta}(x)}{\left<x,\delta\right>}\/.

∎

Note that for fixed cluster
\varphi\in\Phi^{(m)}
, if
\sum_{\alpha\in\varphi}\left<\alpha,\delta\right>^{2}=0
, then
\sum_{\alpha\in R_{+}(\varphi)}\left<\alpha,\delta\right>^{2}=0
and consequently
h^{\varphi}_{\delta}(x)=0
. Thus it is enough to consider those
\delta\in R_{+}
such that
\sum_{\alpha\in\varphi}\left<\alpha,\delta\right>^{2}>0
or conversely, for fixed
\delta\in R_{+}
we can consider only clusters from

\Phi^{(m)}_{\delta}=\{\varphi\in\Phi^{(m)}:\sum_{\alpha\in\varphi}\left<\alpha,\delta\right>^{2}>0\}\/.

Moreover, for fixed
\delta\in R_{+}
and
\varphi\in\Phi^{(m)}_{\delta}
we define

\hat{\varphi}=\{s^{+}_{\delta}(\alpha)\in R_{+}:\alpha\in\varphi\}\/.

It is important to see that if
\delta\in R_{+}(\varphi)
, directly from the definition of
R_{+}(\varphi)
together with
s^{+}_{\delta}(s^{+}_{\delta}(\alpha))=\alpha
we have
R_{+}(\varphi)=R_{+}(\hat{\varphi})
. Then
h^{\varphi}_{\delta}(x)=h^{\hat{\varphi}}_{\delta}(x)
and
r^{\varphi}(x)=r^{\hat{\varphi}}(x)
.

Note that, since at least one of the inner products
\left<\alpha,\delta\right>
is non-zero and obviously
\hat{\varphi}\in\Phi^{(m)}_{\delta}
, as
\left<\alpha,\delta\right>=\pm\left<s^{+}_{\delta}(\alpha),\delta\right>
. We can thus rewrite
h_{3}(x)
as

h_{3}(x)=-\sum_{\delta\in R_{+}}\dfrac{k_{\delta}(t,x)}{\left<x,\delta\right>}\sum_{\varphi\in\Phi^{(m)}_{\delta}}\left(\frac{h^{\varphi}_{\delta}(x)}{r^{\varphi}(x)}+\frac{h^{\hat{\varphi}}_{\delta}(x)}{r^{\hat{\varphi}}(x)}\right)

Recall that

s^{+}_{\delta}(\alpha)=\pm\left(\alpha-\frac{2\left<\alpha,\delta\right>\delta}{|\delta|^{2}}\right)\/,\langle s^{+}_{\delta}(\alpha),\delta\rangle=\mp\left<\alpha,\delta\right>\/,\quad\langle x,s^{+}_{\delta}(\alpha)\rangle=\pm\left(\left<x,\alpha\right>-\frac{2\left<\alpha,\delta\right>\left<x,\delta\right>}{|\delta|^{2}}\right),

and

(5.8)

\left<\alpha,\delta\right>\left<x,\alpha\right>+\langle s^{+}_{\delta}(\alpha),\delta\rangle\langle x,s^{+}_{\delta}(\alpha)\rangle=\frac{2\left<\alpha,\delta\right>^{2}\left<x,\delta\right>}{|\delta|^{2}}\/.

We begin with using (
5.8
) to get

h^{\varphi}_{\delta}(x)+h^{\hat{\varphi}}_{\delta}(x)=\sum_{\alpha\in R_{+}(\varphi)}\left<\alpha,\delta\right>\left<x,\alpha\right>+\langle s^{+}_{\delta}(\alpha),\delta\rangle\langle x,s^{+}_{\delta}(\alpha)\rangle=\frac{2\left<x,\delta\right>}{|\delta|^{2}}\sum_{\alpha\in R_{+}(\varphi)}\left<\alpha,\delta\right>^{2}.

We have

\left<x,\alpha\right>^{2}-\langle x,s^{+}_{\delta}(\alpha)\rangle^{2}=\frac{2\left<x,\delta\right>}{|\delta|^{2}}\left(\left<\alpha,\delta\right>\left<x,\alpha\right>-\langle s^{+}_{\delta}(\alpha),\delta\rangle\langle x,s^{+}_{\delta}(\alpha)\rangle\right).

Consequently,

\displaystyle r^{\varphi}(x)-r^{\hat{\varphi}}(x)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\alpha\in R_{+}(\varphi)}\left(\left<x,\alpha\right>^{2}-\langle x,s^{+}_{\delta}(\alpha)\rangle^{2}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{2\left<x,\delta\right>}{|\delta|^{2}}\sum_{\alpha\in R_{+}(\varphi)}\left(\left<\alpha,\delta\right>\left<x,\alpha\right>-\langle s^{+}_{\delta}(\alpha),\delta\rangle\langle x,s^{+}_{\delta}(\alpha)\rangle\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{2\left<x,\delta\right>}{|\delta|^{2}}\left(h^{\varphi}_{\delta}(x)-h^{\hat{\varphi}}_{\delta}(x)\right).

where we have also used the fact that if
\left<\alpha,\delta\right>=0
then
s^{+}_{\delta}(\alpha)=\alpha
. Combining all together we get

\displaystyle\frac{h^{\varphi}_{\delta}(x)}{r^{\varphi}(x)}+\frac{h^{\hat{\varphi}}_{\delta}(x)}{r^{\hat{\varphi}}(x)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{(r^{\varphi}(x)+r^{\hat{\varphi}}(x))(h^{\varphi}_{\delta}(x)+h^{\hat{\varphi}}_{\delta}(x))}{2r^{\varphi}(x)r^{\hat{\varphi}}(x)}-\frac{(r^{\varphi}(x)-r^{\hat{\varphi}}(x))(h^{\varphi}_{\delta}(x)-h^{\hat{\varphi}}_{\delta}(x))}{2r^{\varphi}(x)r^{\hat{\varphi}}(x)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\left<x,\delta\right>}{|\delta|^{2}}\left[\left(\frac{1}{r^{\varphi}(x)}+\frac{1}{r^{\hat{\varphi}}(x)}\right)\sum_{\alpha\in R_{+}(\varphi)}\left<\alpha,\delta\right>^{2}-\frac{(h^{\varphi}_{\delta}(x)-h^{\hat{\varphi}}_{\delta}(x))^{2}}{r^{\varphi}(x)r^{\hat{\varphi}}(x)}\right]

which allow us to remove the singularity related to
\left<\alpha,\delta\right>^{-1}
in the formula for
h_{3}(x)
and arrive at the following representation of
h_{3}(x)
.

(5.9)

-\sum_{\delta\in R_{+}}\dfrac{k_{\delta}(t,x)}{|\delta|^{2}}\sum_{\varphi\in\Phi^{(m)}_{\delta}}\left[\left(\frac{1}{r^{\varphi}(x)}+\frac{1}{r^{\hat{\varphi}}(x)}\right)\sum_{\alpha\in R_{+}(\varphi)}\left<\alpha,\delta\right>^{2}-\frac{(h^{\varphi}_{\delta}(x)-h^{\hat{\varphi}}_{\delta}(x))^{2}}{r^{\varphi}(x)r^{\hat{\varphi}}(x)}\right]\/.

Examine the behavior of
h_{3}(x)
in the neighborhood of the boundary point
y\in\partial C_{+}^{m}
. Recall that for such a point, the largest clusters in
\Phi(y)
are of length
m
. The function
h_{3}(x)
might explode only if
r^{\varphi}(x)=0
for some
\varphi\in\Phi^{(m)}_{\delta}
and this might only occur in the small neighborhood of
y
if additionally
\varphi\in\Phi(y)
. Fix this
\varphi\in\Phi(y)\cap\Phi^{(m)}_{\delta}
. If
\delta\in R_{+}(\varphi)
, then, as we have previously seen,
R_{+}(\varphi)=R_{+}(\hat{\varphi})
and
h^{\varphi}_{\delta}(x)=h^{\hat{\varphi}}_{\delta}(x)
,
r^{\varphi}(x)=r^{\hat{\varphi}}(x)
, which reduce the corresponding expression in (
5.9
) to

-\dfrac{2k_{\delta}(t,x)}{|\delta|^{2}}\sum_{\varphi\in\Phi^{(m)}_{\delta}}\frac{1}{r^{\varphi}(x)}\sum_{\alpha\in R_{+}(\varphi)}\left<\alpha,\delta\right>^{2}\/.

We use those observations to prove the next proposition, which is one of the most crucial results and shows that whenever the repulsive function
k_{\alpha}(t,x)
does not vanish at the multiple boundary points, then those points are not visited by the process.

Proposition 2
.

Assume that
X(0)=x_{0}\in C_{+}
, and
(A1)
holds. Recall that
\tau_{0}^{m}=\inf\{t>0:X(t)\in\partial C_{+}^{(m)}\}
, for
m\in\{2,\ldots,s\}
.
Then

\mathbf{P}\left(\tau_{0}^{m}\leq\tau_{\mathcal{K}},\ \tau_{0}^{m}<\zeta^{X}\right)=0,\qquad m=2,\ldots,s.

Equivalently, before and at the first possible exit from
\mathcal{K}
, the process cannot reach a multiple collision point.

Proof.

We begin with fixing
m\in\{2,\ldots,s\}
and restricting our consideration to the sets

\{\tau_{0}^{m}<\tau_{0}^{m+1}\}\cap\{\tau_{0}^{m}\leq\tau_{\mathcal{K}}\}\cap\{\tau_{0}^{m}<\zeta^{X}\}\/,

where we have
\tau_{0}^{s+1}=\zeta^{X}
. We consider
Q_{m}=-\ln q_{m}(X)
, with
q_{m}(X)
defined in (
5.3
). By Proposition
1
, we know the form of
Q_{m}(t)
for
t<\tau_{0}^{m}
, which is our starting point for further consideration. We will show that under assumption on
k_{\alpha}
, this process does not explode to
\infty
in finite time and consequently
\tau_{0}^{m}=\tau_{0}^{m+1}
on
\{\tau_{0}^{m}\leq\tau_{\mathcal{K}}\}\cap\{\tau_{0}^{m}<\zeta^{X}\}
. Indeed, the martingale part
M^{Q_{m}}
given in (
5.4
), by the McKean argument, cannot explode in finite time, as it is always a Brownian motion with time changed.

To deal with the drift part, let us denote by
y=X(\tau_{0}^{m})
. Since we are working on
\{\tau_{0}^{m}<\tau_{0}^{m+1}\}
, it follows that
y\in\partial C_{+}^{(m)}
.
Let us see that the blow-up of any of the parts
h_{1}
,
h_{2}
, or
h_{3}
(in the form (
5.9
)) is only possible when
r^{\varphi}(y)
becomes zero, which might happen only if
\varphi\in\Phi(y)\cap\Phi^{(m)}
. From now on we will fix
\varphi
in
\Phi(y)\cap\Phi^{(m)}
and consider the corresponding element from
h_{1}(x)+h_{2}(x)+h_{3}(x)
, i.e. the expression

(5.10)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{(r^{\varphi}(x))^{2}}\sum_{k=1}^{N}\left(2(h^{\varphi}_{k}(x))^{2}-r^{\varphi}(x)\sum_{\alpha\in R_{+}(\varphi)}\alpha_{k}^{2}\right)\sigma_{k}^{2}(t,x)

(5.11)

\displaystyle-\frac{2}{r^{\varphi}(x)}\sum_{k=1}^{N}h^{\varphi}_{k}(x)b_{k}(t,x)

(5.12)

\displaystyle-\frac{2}{r^{\varphi}(x)}\sum_{\delta\in R_{+}(\varphi)}\dfrac{k_{\delta}(t,x)}{|\delta|^{2}}\sum_{\alpha\in R_{+}(\varphi)}\left<\alpha,\delta\right>^{2}

(5.13)

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{r^{\varphi}(x)}\sum_{\delta\notin R_{+}(\varphi)}\dfrac{k_{\delta}(t,x)}{|\delta|^{2}r^{\hat{\varphi}}(x)}\left[\left(r^{\varphi}(x)+r^{\hat{\varphi}}(x)\right)\sum_{\alpha\in R_{+}(\varphi)}\left<\alpha,\delta\right>^{2}-(h^{\varphi}_{\delta}(x)-h^{\hat{\varphi}}_{\delta}(x))^{2}\right]

First we deal with the last part. Note that if
\delta\notin R_{+}(\varphi)
, then
\hat{\varphi}
cannot be a cluster from
\Phi(y)
. Indeed, otherwise all roots from
\hat{\varphi}
would vanish at
y
, and we would obtain a cluster of length larger than
m
, which is impossible since
y\in\partial C_{+}^{m}
. Hence not all roots in
\hat{\varphi}
vanish at
y
, and therefore
r^{\hat{\varphi}}(y)>0
. Consequently, using
\left<\alpha,\delta\right>^{2}=\langle s^{+}_{\delta}(\alpha),\delta\rangle^{2}
, we get

\displaystyle\lim_{x\to y}\hbox to0.0pt{$\displaystyle[(r^{\varphi}(x)+r^{\hat{\varphi}}(x))\sum_{\alpha\in R_{+}(\varphi)}\left<\alpha,\delta\right>^{2}-(h^{\varphi}_{\delta}(x)-h^{\hat{\varphi}}_{\delta}(x))^{2}]=$\hss}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle r^{\hat{\varphi}}(y)\sum_{\alpha\in R_{+}(\varphi)}\langle s^{+}_{\delta}(\alpha),\delta\rangle^{2}-(h^{\hat{\varphi}}_{\delta}(y))^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{\alpha\in R_{+}(\varphi)}\langle y,s^{+}_{\delta}(\alpha)\rangle^{2}\sum_{\alpha\in R_{+}(\varphi)}\langle s^{+}_{\delta}(\alpha),\delta\rangle^{2}-\left(\sum_{\alpha\in R_{+}(\varphi)}\langle s^{+}_{\delta}(\alpha),\delta\rangle\langle y,s^{+}_{\delta}(\alpha)\rangle\right)^{2}\geq 0\/.

Non-negativity of the last expressions follows from Cauchy-Schwarz inequality.
It is also clear that
h^{\varphi}_{k}(x)b_{k}(x)
goes to zero as
x\to y
.
These two observations together with positivity of
k_{\delta}(t,y)
,
r^{\hat{\varphi}}(y)
and
\sum_{\alpha\in R_{+}(\varphi)}\left<\alpha,\delta\right>^{2}
and continuity argument show that there exists a neighborhood
U(y)
of
y
such that for every
x\in U(y)
the sum of (
5.10
), (
5.11
), (
5.12
) and (
5.13
) is bounded from above by

\frac{1}{(r^{\varphi}(x))^{2}}\sum_{k=1}^{N}\left(2(h^{\varphi}_{k}(x))^{2}-r^{\varphi}(x)\sum_{\alpha\in R_{+}(\varphi)}\alpha_{k}^{2}\right)\sigma_{k}^{2}(t,x)-\frac{1}{r^{\varphi}(x)}\sum_{\delta\in R_{+}(\varphi)}\dfrac{k_{\delta}(t,x)}{|\delta|^{2}}\sum_{\alpha\in R_{+}(\varphi)}\left<\alpha,\delta\right>^{2}

Using the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality we get

2(h^{\varphi}_{k}(x))^{2}-r^{\varphi}(x)\sum_{\alpha\in R_{+}(\varphi)}\alpha_{k}^{2}\leq r^{\varphi}(x)\sum_{\alpha\in R_{+}(\varphi)}\alpha_{k}^{2}\/.

Continuity of
\sigma_{k}(t,x)
gives
(\sigma_{k}(t,x)-\sigma_{k}(t,y))\to 0
as
x
approaches
y
and the same argument as above allows us to replace the expression by the following

\frac{1}{(r^{\varphi}(x))^{2}}\sum_{k=1}^{N}\left(2(h^{\varphi}_{k}(x))^{2}-r^{\varphi}(x)\sum_{\alpha\in R_{+}(\varphi)}\alpha_{k}^{2}\right)\sigma_{k}^{2}(t,y)-\frac{1}{2r^{\varphi}(x)}\sum_{\delta\in R_{+}(\varphi)}\dfrac{k_{\delta}(t,x)}{|\delta|^{2}}\sum_{\alpha\in R_{+}(\varphi)}\left<\alpha,\delta\right>^{2}

perhaps by shrinking the neighborhoods of
U(y)
, if needed. Note that the only problematic element of the expression is
2(h^{\varphi}_{k}(x))^{2}
, since the rest is obviously non-positive and thus can not explode to
\infty
. This is way we can restrict the sum over
k
to the set of indices, which appear in roots from
R_{+}(\varphi)
, i.e.

I(\varphi)=\left\{k\in\{1,\ldots,N\}:\sum_{\alpha\in R_{+}(\varphi)}\alpha_{k}^{2}>0\right\}

because only for those
k
we might have
h^{\varphi}_{k}(x)\neq 0
.
By Assumption
(C2)
, the diffusion variances are constant on the coordinate block generated by the long roots in
R_{+}(\varphi)
. Hence there exists
c_{\varphi}(t,y)\geq 0
such that

(5.14)

\displaystyle\sigma_{k}^{2}(t,y)=c_{\varphi}(t,y),\qquad k\in I(\varphi)\/.

These observations enable us to write

\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{N}\left[2(h^{\varphi}_{k}(x))^{2}-r^{\varphi}(x)\sum_{\alpha\in R_{+}(\varphi)}\alpha_{k}^{2}\right]\sigma_{k}^{2}(t,y)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle c_{\varphi}(t,y)\sum_{k\in I(\varphi)}\left[2(h^{\varphi}_{k}(x))^{2}-r^{\varphi}(x)\sum_{\alpha\in R_{+}(\varphi)}\alpha_{k}^{2}\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle c_{\varphi}(t,y)\sum_{k=1}^{N}\left[2(h^{\varphi}_{k}(x))^{2}-r^{\varphi}(x)\sum_{\alpha\in R_{+}(\varphi)}\alpha_{k}^{2}\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle c_{\varphi}(t,y)\left[2\sum_{k=1}^{N}(h^{\varphi}_{k}(x))^{2}-r^{\varphi}(x)\sum_{\alpha\in R_{+}(\varphi)}|\alpha|^{2}\right]\/.

It remains to estimate

2\sum_{k=1}^{N}(h^{\varphi}_{k}(x))^{2}-r^{\varphi}(x)\sum_{\alpha\in R_{+}(\varphi)}|\alpha|^{2}.

Put
q:=|I(\varphi)|
. Since
y\in\partial C_{+}^{(m)}
and
\varphi\in\Phi(y)\cap\Phi^{(m)}
, the cluster
\varphi
is maximal at
y
. Therefore, up to relabelling of the coordinates in
I(\varphi)
, the generated set
R_{+}(\varphi)
has one of the following forms. First, we may have an
A
-type cluster,

R_{+}(\varphi)=\{e_{i}-e_{j}:\ i,j\in I(\varphi),\ i<j\}.

This case may occur in all three classical root systems. Second, in type
D_{N}
we may have a mixed nonzero mirror cluster. In this
case

I(\varphi)=J\cup\{N\},\qquad J\subset\{1,\ldots,N-1\},

and

R_{+}(\varphi)=\{e_{i}-e_{j}:\ i,j\in J,\ i<j\}\cup\{e_{i}+e_{N}:\ i\in J\}.

This corresponds to nonzero collisions of the form
x_{i}=-x_{N}\neq 0
together with ordinary equalities among the coordinates
indexed by
J
. Third, in type
B_{N}
we may have a zero cluster,

R_{+}(\varphi)=\{e_{i}:\ i\in I(\varphi)\}\cup\{e_{i}-e_{j},\ e_{i}+e_{j}:\ i,j\in I(\varphi),\ i<j\}.

Finally, in type
D_{N}
we may have a zero cluster,

R_{+}(\varphi)=\{e_{i}-e_{j},\ e_{i}+e_{j}:\ i,j\in I(\varphi),\ i<j\}.

Notice that in the last case with
q=2
this includes the zero-pair cluster
\{e_{i}-e_{j},\ e_{i}+e_{j}\}
. We now compute the above expression in these cases.

We now compute the above expression in these cases. In the type
A_{N-1}
case, writing

\bar{x}_{I}:=\frac{1}{q}\sum_{i\in I(\varphi)}x_{i},

we have

r^{\varphi}(x)=\sum_{i<j,\ i,j\in I(\varphi)}(x_{i}-x_{j})^{2}=q\sum_{i\in I(\varphi)}(x_{i}-\bar{x}_{I})^{2},

and, for
i\in I(\varphi)
,

h^{\varphi}_{i}(x)=\sum_{j\in I(\varphi),\,j\neq i}(x_{i}-x_{j})=q(x_{i}-\bar{x}_{I}).

Hence

\sum_{k=1}^{N}(h^{\varphi}_{k}(x))^{2}=q\,r^{\varphi}(x).

Since
|e_{i}-e_{j}|^{2}=2
and there are
q(q-1)/2
roots in this generated subsystem,

\sum_{\alpha\in R_{+}(\varphi)}|\alpha|^{2}=q(q-1).

Therefore

2\sum_{k=1}^{N}(h^{\varphi}_{k}(x))^{2}-r^{\varphi}(x)\sum_{\alpha\in R_{+}(\varphi)}|\alpha|^{2}=q(3-q)r^{\varphi}(x)\leq 0,

because in the present multiple-collision case
q\geq 3
. In the mixed type
D_{N}
case, put

z_{i}=x_{i},\quad i\in J,\qquad z_{N}=-x_{N},\qquad\bar{z}_{I}:=\frac{1}{q}\left(\sum_{i\in J}z_{i}+z_{N}\right).

Then

r^{\varphi}(x)=\sum_{i<j,\ i,j\in J}(x_{i}-x_{j})^{2}+\sum_{i\in J}(x_{i}+x_{N})^{2}=\sum_{i<j,\ i,j\in I(\varphi)}(z_{i}-z_{j})^{2}=q\sum_{i\in I(\varphi)}(z_{i}-\bar{z}_{I})^{2}.

Moreover, for
i\in J
,

h^{\varphi}_{i}(x)=\sum_{j\in J,\,j\neq i}(x_{i}-x_{j})+(x_{i}+x_{N})=q(z_{i}-\bar{z}_{I}),

while

h^{\varphi}_{N}(x)=\sum_{i\in J}(x_{i}+x_{N})=-q(z_{N}-\bar{z}_{I}).

Hence

\sum_{k=1}^{N}(h^{\varphi}_{k}(x))^{2}=q\,r^{\varphi}(x).

The generated subsystem contains

\binom{q-1}{2}+(q-1)=\frac{q(q-1)}{2}

positive roots, all of length squared equal to
2
. Therefore

\sum_{\alpha\in R_{+}(\varphi)}|\alpha|^{2}=q(q-1).

Consequently,

2\sum_{k=1}^{N}(h^{\varphi}_{k}(x))^{2}-r^{\varphi}(x)\sum_{\alpha\in R_{+}(\varphi)}|\alpha|^{2}=q(3-q)r^{\varphi}(x)\leq 0.

Here
q=2
would give only the single root
e_{i}+e_{N}
, so in the present multiple-collision case we again have
q\geq 3
. In the type
B_{N}
case we have

r^{\varphi}(x)=\sum_{i\in I(\varphi)}x_{i}^{2}+\sum_{i<j,\ i,j\in I(\varphi)}\left[(x_{i}-x_{j})^{2}+(x_{i}+x_{j})^{2}\right]=(2q-1)\sum_{i\in I(\varphi)}x_{i}^{2}.

Moreover, for
i\in I(\varphi)
,

h^{\varphi}_{i}(x)=x_{i}+\sum_{j\in I(\varphi),\,j\neq i}\left[(x_{i}-x_{j})+(x_{i}+x_{j})\right]=(2q-1)x_{i}.

Thus

\sum_{k=1}^{N}(h^{\varphi}_{k}(x))^{2}=(2q-1)r^{\varphi}(x).

The generated subsystem contains
q
short roots and
2\binom{q}{2}=q(q-1)
long roots. Consequently,

\sum_{\alpha\in R_{+}(\varphi)}|\alpha|^{2}=q+2q(q-1)=q(2q-1).

Hence

2\sum_{k=1}^{N}(h^{\varphi}_{k}(x))^{2}-r^{\varphi}(x)\sum_{\alpha\in R_{+}(\varphi)}|\alpha|^{2}=(2-q)(2q-1)r^{\varphi}(x)\leq 0,

because here
q\geq 2
in the multiple-collision case. Finally, in the type
D_{N}
case we have

r^{\varphi}(x)=\sum_{i<j,\ i,j\in I(\varphi)}\left[(x_{i}-x_{j})^{2}+(x_{i}+x_{j})^{2}\right]=2(q-1)\sum_{i\in I(\varphi)}x_{i}^{2}.

Moreover, for
i\in I(\varphi)
,

h^{\varphi}_{i}(x)=\sum_{j\in I(\varphi),\,j\neq i}\left[(x_{i}-x_{j})+(x_{i}+x_{j})\right]=2(q-1)x_{i}.

Thus

\sum_{k=1}^{N}(h^{\varphi}_{k}(x))^{2}=2(q-1)r^{\varphi}(x).

The generated subsystem contains
2\binom{q}{2}=q(q-1)
positive roots, all of length squared equal to
2
. Therefore

\sum_{\alpha\in R_{+}(\varphi)}|\alpha|^{2}=2q(q-1),

and

2\sum_{k=1}^{N}(h^{\varphi}_{k}(x))^{2}-r^{\varphi}(x)\sum_{\alpha\in R_{+}(\varphi)}|\alpha|^{2}=-2(q-1)(q-2)r^{\varphi}(x)\leq 0.

In particular, the type
D_{N}
zero-pair case corresponds to
q=2
, where the last expression is equal to zero. This is still sufficient, because the active singular term (
5.12
) is strictly negative near the zero-pair wall by Assumption
(A1)
.

We can now finish the proof, since we get to the conclusion, that the whole expression related to
r^{\varphi}(x)
, on sufficiently small neighborhood of
y
, is non-positive and thus can not explode in finite time. It means that the process does not reach
y
in finite time almost surely, which means that
\tau_{0}^{m}=\tau_{0}^{m+1}
for every
m\geq 2
on
\{\tau_{0}^{m}\leq\tau_{\mathcal{K}}\}\cap\{\tau_{0}^{m}<\zeta^{X}\}
. Since
\tau_{0}^{s+1}=\zeta^{X}
, this ends the proof.

∎

We next prove tha whenever
(A1)
holds, the process
U
, which is the baseline for our construction of a solution
X
does not leave the set
\mathcal{K}
. It is crucial to remove the restriction related to
\tau_{\mathcal{K}}
from our results and consequently provide the general result considering the multi-collisions in the positive repulsion regime.

5.2.
No exit from the invariant chamber image

We begin with showing that
U
stays in
\mathcal{K}
, whenever we start our diffusion from inside of the set. Then, in particular, the positive repulsion assumed in
(A1)
pushed the process away from the multiple collision points, as we have seen in Proposition
2
. This is one of the most important observation used in the following.

Proposition 3
.

Assume
(C1)
,
(C2)
, and
(A1)
. If
U(0)=w(x_{0})
with
x_{0}\in C_{+}
, then

(5.15)

U(t)\in\mathcal{K}=\overline{w[C_{+}]},\qquad 0\leq t<\zeta^{U},

almost surely. Consequently
\tau_{\mathcal{K}}=\zeta^{U}
a.s. and
X(t)=f(U(t))
for all
t<\zeta^{X}=\zeta^{U}
.

Proof.

We write the proof in a form which covers the three classical root systems at once. The first step is to record the algebraic equations defining the closed chamber image.

Let
M_{R}:=|R_{+}|
. In type
A_{N-1}
, let
P_{u}^{A}
be the monic polynomial whose normalized power sums are
u_{1},\ldots,u_{N}
, and let
z_{1}(u),\ldots,z_{N}(u)
be its complex roots. Define
\Phi_{1}^{A},\ldots,\Phi_{M_{R}}^{A}
by

(5.16)

\prod_{1\leq i<j\leq N}\left(\lambda+(z_{i}(u)-z_{j}(u))^{2}\right)=\lambda^{M_{R}}+\Phi_{1}^{A}(u)\lambda^{M_{R}-1}+\cdots+\Phi_{M_{R}}^{A}(u).

In types
B_{N}
and
D_{N}
we use the squared variables
y_{i}=x_{i}^{2}
. For
R=B_{N}
, let
P_{u}^{B}
be the monic polynomial whose normalized power sums are

\frac{1}{k}\sum_{i=1}^{N}y_{i}^{k}=2u_{k},\qquad k=1,\ldots,N.

Let
y_{1}(u),\ldots,y_{N}(u)
be the complex roots of
P_{u}^{B}
and define
\Phi_{1}^{B},\ldots,\Phi_{N^{2}}^{B}
by

(5.17)

\prod_{i=1}^{N}(\lambda+y_{i}(u))\prod_{1\leq i<j\leq N}\left((\lambda+y_{i}(u)+y_{j}(u))^{2}-4y_{i}(u)y_{j}(u)\right)=\lambda^{N^{2}}+\Phi_{1}^{B}(u)\lambda^{N^{2}-1}+\cdots+\Phi_{N^{2}}^{B}(u).

For
R=D_{N}
, let
P_{u}^{D}
be the monic polynomial in the variables
y_{i}=x_{i}^{2}
whose normalized power sums are

\frac{1}{k}\sum_{i=1}^{N}y_{i}^{k}=2u_{k},\qquad k=1,\ldots,N-1,

and whose top elementary symmetric polynomial is

e_{N}(y_{1},\ldots,y_{N})=u_{N}^{2}.

Let
y_{1}(u),\ldots,y_{N}(u)
be the complex roots of
P_{u}^{D}
and define
\Phi_{1}^{D},\ldots,\Phi_{N(N-1)}^{D}
by

(5.18)

\prod_{1\leq i<j\leq N}\left((\lambda+y_{i}(u)+y_{j}(u))^{2}-4y_{i}(u)y_{j}(u)\right)=\lambda^{N(N-1)}+\Phi_{1}^{D}(u)\lambda^{N(N-1)-1}+\cdots+\Phi_{N(N-1)}^{D}(u).

Note that there is no factor
\prod_{i}(\lambda+y_{i})
in type
D_{N}
, because type
D_{N}
has no short roots.

The coefficients in (
5.16
)-(
5.18
) are genuine real polynomials in the invariant coordinates
u
. Indeed, they are symmetric polynomials in the roots of the corresponding polynomial
P_{u}^{R}
, and hence are polynomials in its coefficients, equivalently in the basic invariant coordinates chosen above. Moreover,

(5.19)

\mathcal{K}=\{u:\Phi_{m}^{R}(u)\geq 0,\ m=1,\ldots,M_{R}\}.

To justify this, observe first that if
u=w(x)
with
x\in\overline{C_{+}}
, then the factors in (
5.16
)-(
5.18
) are exactly
\lambda+\langle x,\alpha\rangle^{2}
,
\alpha\in R_{+}
. Thus all coefficients
\Phi_{m}^{R}(u)
are non-negative. Conversely, if all these coefficients are non-negative, the polynomial in
\lambda
on the left-hand side cannot vanish at any positive
\lambda
. In type
A_{N-1}
, a non-real pair
a\pm ib
among the roots would give the positive zero
\lambda=4b^{2}
. In types
B_{N}
and
D_{N}
, a non-real pair
a\pm ib
among the squared roots
y_{i}
gives a positive zero of one of the pair factors

(\lambda+y_{i}+y_{j})^{2}-4y_{i}y_{j}.

In type
B_{N}
, a negative real squared root gives a positive zero of the short root factor
\lambda+y_{i}
. In type
D_{N}
, negative real squared roots are also impossible: since
\prod_{i}y_{i}=u_{N}^{2}\geq 0
, either at least two negative roots occur, or one negative root occurs together with a zero root, and then one of the pair factors again has a positive zero. Hence the squared roots are non-negative real numbers in types
B_{N}
and
D_{N}
, and the roots are real in type
A_{N-1}
. Ordering the roots, and in type
D_{N}
choosing the sign of the last coordinate according to the sign of
u_{N}
, gives a point
x\in\overline{C_{+}}
with
u=w(x)
. This proves (
5.19
).

We now prove that the solution cannot leave this set. Suppose, to the contrary, that
\tau_{\mathcal{K}}<\zeta^{U}
with positive probability. By continuity,
U(\tau_{\mathcal{K}})\in\partial\mathcal{K}
on this event. Put

u_{*}:=U(\tau_{\mathcal{K}}),\qquad x_{*}:=f(u_{*}).

By Proposition
2
, no two non-orthogonal positive roots can vanish at
x_{*}
. Thus the vanishing roots at
x_{*}
are pairwise orthogonal. The only point which requires separate mention is type
D_{N}
: if a zero pair occurs, then both
e_{i}-e_{j}
and
e_{i}+e_{j}
vanish, and these two roots are orthogonal.

We next describe local defining functions for
\mathcal{K}
near
u_{*}
. After shrinking a neighborhood
\mathcal{O}
of
u_{*}
if necessary, one can find real analytic functions
r_{1},\ldots,r_{q}
on
\mathcal{O}
such that

(5.20)

\mathcal{K}\cap\mathcal{O}=\{u\in\mathcal{O}:r_{1}(u)\geq 0,\ldots,r_{q}(u)\geq 0\}.

Before going to the construction given below note that formally we do not start with
u_{*}
, which is random and then consider the random set
\mathcal{O}
, but instead we cover all boundary points in
\partial\mathcal{K}
, which have property that the vanishing roots at corresponding boundary point
x
are pairwise orthogonal by open cover. Since the space considered is separable, we can choose a countable subcover, and then we can construct family of functions
r_{\ell}
for each element of this subcover, as it is described below, and finally work on the set
U(\tau_{\mathcal{K}})\in\mathcal{O}_{i}
for some
i\in\mathbb{N}
. We omit the subscript
i
in the notation to make the proof easier to read. The construction of local defining functions is as follows. In type
A_{N-1}
, each active root corresponds to a separated double root of the local polynomial
P_{u}^{A}
. We factor the corresponding pair as
z^{2}-s_{\ell}(u)z+p_{\ell}(u)
and put

r_{\ell}(u):=s_{\ell}(u)^{2}-4p_{\ell}(u).

On
\mathcal{K}
this gives

r_{\ell}(w(x))=\langle x,\alpha_{\ell}\rangle^{2}.

In type
B_{N}
, there are two possible local factors. For a short-root wall
x_{i}=0
, the corresponding squared root
y_{i}
is a separated simple root of
P_{u}^{B}
and we take

r_{\ell}(u):=y_{i}(u),\qquad r_{\ell}(w(x))=x_{i}^{2}.

For a non-zero pair collision
x_{i}=x_{j}>0
, the two squared roots form a local quadratic factor
z^{2}-s_{\ell}(u)z+p_{\ell}(u)
and we take

r_{\ell}(u):=s_{\ell}(u)^{2}-4p_{\ell}(u),\qquad r_{\ell}(w(x))=(x_{i}^{2}-x_{j}^{2})^{2}.

The root
e_{i}+e_{j}
cannot occur as an isolated simple wall in type
B_{N}
inside
\overline{C_{+}}
; if
x_{i}+x_{j}=0
, then both coordinates are zero and non-orthogonal roots also vanish, a case excluded by the stopped no-multiple-collision result. In type
D_{N}
, a non-zero collision
x_{i}=x_{j}\neq 0
or
x_{i}=-x_{j}\neq 0
is treated in the same squared-variable way, with

r_{\ell}(w(x))=(x_{i}^{2}-x_{j}^{2})^{2}.

There is also the zero-pair case. Then the two small squared roots form a local quadratic factor
z^{2}-s(u)z+p(u)
with
s=x_{i}^{2}+x_{j}^{2}
on
\mathcal{K}
. Since the inactive coordinates stay away from zero, the product coordinate
u_{N}=e_{N}(x)
gives an analytic local function
m(u)
satisfying
m(w(x))=x_{i}x_{j}
. We then use the two local variables

r_{-}(u):=s(u)-2m(u),\qquad r_{+}(u):=s(u)+2m(u),

so that, on
\mathcal{K}
,

r_{-}(w(x))=(x_{i}-x_{j})^{2},\qquad r_{+}(w(x))=(x_{i}+x_{j})^{2}.

Together with the variables coming from the remaining active, mutually orthogonal collisions, these functions give the local description (
5.20
).

Fix one of the local functions
r_{\ell}
and put
R_{\ell}(t)=r_{\ell}(U(t))
. On the stochastic interval on which
U(t)\in\mathcal{O}
, Itô’s formula applied to the smooth function
r_{\ell}(U)
gives

(5.21)

dR_{\ell}(t)=\sum_{i=1}^{N}\theta_{\ell i}(t,U(t))\,dB_{i}(t)+\gamma_{\ell}(t,U(t))\,dt.

We claim that, after shrinking
\mathcal{O}
and localizing to a compact time interval
[0,T]
, there are constants
c,C>0
such that

(5.22)

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{N}\theta_{\ell i}^{2}(t,u)

\displaystyle\leq C|r_{\ell}(u)|,

\displaystyle 0\leq t\leq T,\ u\in\mathcal{O},

(5.23)

\displaystyle\gamma_{\ell}(t,u)

\displaystyle\geq c,

\displaystyle 0\leq t\leq T,\ u\in\mathcal{O},\ r_{\ell}(u)\leq 0.

On the hypersurface
\{r_{\ell}=0\}
, the two local roots corresponding to the active factor coincide. Hence the first-order variation of the local discriminant
r_{\ell}
in every Brownian direction of the invariant-coordinate equation vanishes:

\theta_{\ell i}(t,u)=0,\qquad u\in\mathcal{O},\ r_{\ell}(u)=0,\quad i=1,\ldots,N.

The estimate (
5.22
) is obtained directly on a local branch. Let
z_{\ell}(x)=\langle x,\alpha_{\ell}\rangle
be the active root coordinate. Locally, the corresponding boundary coordinate has the form

r_{\ell}(w(x))=q_{\ell}(x)z_{\ell}(x)^{2},

where
q_{\ell}
is positive and continuous. Applying Itô’s formula on the interior branch shows that the martingale coefficient of
R_{\ell}=r_{\ell}(U)
contains the factor
z_{\ell}(x)
. Hence, on compact time intervals and after shrinking the neighbourhood,

\sum_{i=1}^{N}\theta_{\ell i}^{2}(t,u)\leq Cz_{\ell}(x)^{2}\leq C^{\prime}|r_{\ell}(u)|.

The same argument applies to the walls
x_{i}-x_{j}=0
,
x_{i}+x_{j}=0
, and, in type
B_{N}
, to the short-root wall
x_{i}=0
. This gives (
5.22
). The drift at the wall is

(5.24)

\gamma_{\ell}(t,w(x_{*}))=q_{\ell}(x_{*})\left(\sum_{i=1}^{N}\alpha_{\ell,i}^{2}\sigma_{i}^{2}(t,x_{*})+2|\alpha_{\ell}|^{2}k_{\alpha_{\ell}}(t,x_{*})\right)>0.

The remaining singular-looking terms are harmless: inactive denominators stay bounded away from zero, while active roots different from
\alpha_{\ell}
are orthogonal to
\alpha_{\ell}
. Assumption
(C2)
guarantees the required continuity at long-root walls. By Assumption
(A1)
and continuity, (
5.23
) follows after shrinking
\mathcal{O}
and localizing in time.

We now apply Tanaka’s formula. Let
s<\tau_{\mathcal{K}}
be such that
U(s)\in\mathcal{O}
and stop the process at the first time after
s
when it leaves
\mathcal{O}
, and also at the fixed time
T
. Since
R_{\ell}(s)\geq 0
, Tanaka’s formula for
R_{\ell}^{-}:=\max\{-R_{\ell},0\}
gives on the localized interval

\displaystyle R_{\ell}^{-}(t)

\displaystyle=-\sum_{i=1}^{N}\int_{s}^{t}\mathbf{1}_{\{R_{\ell}(r)<0\}}\theta_{\ell i}(r,U(r))\,dB_{i}(r)

\displaystyle\quad-\int_{s}^{t}\mathbf{1}_{\{R_{\ell}(r)<0\}}\gamma_{\ell}(r,U(r))\,dr+\frac{1}{2}\left(L_{t}^{0}(R_{\ell})-L_{s}^{0}(R_{\ell})\right).

By (
5.22
),

d\langle R_{\ell}\rangle_{t}=\sum_{i=1}^{N}\theta_{\ell i}^{2}(t,U(t))\,dt\leq C|R_{\ell}(t)|\,dt.

Moreover,

\int_{s}^{t}\mathbf{1}_{\{R_{\ell}(r)\neq 0\}}\frac{1}{|R_{\ell}(r)|}\,d\langle R_{\ell}\rangle_{r}\leq C(t-s)<\infty.

By the occupation-time formula, this implies

L_{t}^{0}(R_{\ell})-L_{s}^{0}(R_{\ell})=0.

The stochastic integral is a true martingale after the localization. Taking expectations and using (
5.23
), we obtain

\mathbf{E}R_{\ell}^{-}(t)\leq-c\,\mathbf{E}\int_{s}^{t}\mathbf{1}_{\{R_{\ell}(r)<0\}}\,dr\leq 0.

Thus
R_{\ell}^{-}(t)=0
throughout the localized interval. No local defining function
r_{\ell}
can become negative while the path remains in
\mathcal{O}
.

Returning to the supposed first exit, choose
s<\tau_{\mathcal{K}}
so close to
\tau_{\mathcal{K}}
that
U(s)\in\mathcal{O}
. On
[s,\tau_{\mathcal{K}})
all
r_{\ell}(U(t))
are non-negative. The previous paragraph shows that they remain non-negative as long as the path stays in
\mathcal{O}
. But by (
5.20
), leaving
\mathcal{K}
inside
\mathcal{O}
is exactly the event that at least one of the functions
r_{\ell}
becomes negative. This contradicts the definition of
\tau_{\mathcal{K}}
. Hence
\tau_{\mathcal{K}}=\zeta^{U}
almost surely.
∎

The next step is to generalize the result we have just obtained to all possible starting points including boundary points in
\partial C_{+}
. This can be easily achieved by limiting procedure.

Proposition 4
.

Assume
(C1)
,
(C2)
, and
(A1)
. Then, for every
x_{0}\in\overline{C_{+}}
, there exists a weak solution
U
of the invariant-coordinate equation (
4.4
), started from
U(0)=w(x_{0})
, such that

U(t)\in\mathcal{K}:=\overline{w[C_{+}]},\qquad 0\leq t<\zeta^{U},

almost surely. Consequently, for

X(t):=f(U(t)),\qquad 0\leq t<\zeta^{X},

the process
X
is well-defined and takes values in
\overline{C_{+}}
.

Proof.

If
x_{0}\in C_{+}
, the assertion follows from Proposition
3
. Let now
x_{0}\in\partial C_{+}
. Choose a fixed vector
\rho\in C_{+}
, and put

x_{0}^{(n)}:=x_{0}+\frac{1}{n}\rho,\qquad u_{0}^{(n)}:=w(x_{0}^{(n)}).

Then
x_{0}^{(n)}\in C_{+}
and
u_{0}^{(n)}\to u_{0}:=w(x_{0})
. For every
n
, let
U^{(n)}
be the solution of (
4.4
) started from
u_{0}^{(n)}
. By Proposition
3
,

U^{(n)}(t)\in\mathcal{K},\qquad 0\leq t<\zeta^{(n)},

almost surely. We now use a standard localization and compactness argument. Fix
T,r>0
and define

\zeta_{r}^{(n)}:=\inf\{t\geq 0:|U^{(n)}(t)|\geq r\}.

The coefficients of (
4.4
) are continuous, hence locally bounded. Therefore the laws of the stopped processes

\bigl(U^{(n)}(t\wedge\zeta_{r}^{(n)})\bigr)_{0\leq t\leq T}

are tight in
C([0,T],\mathbb{R}^{N})
. Along a subsequence they converge weakly to a continuous process
U^{r}
. Since
\mathcal{K}
is closed and
U^{(n)}(t\wedge\zeta_{r}^{(n)})\in\mathcal{K}
for all
t\leq T
, we also have

U^{r}(t)\in\mathcal{K},\qquad 0\leq t\leq T,

almost surely. The usual stability of the localized martingale problem under locally uniform convergence of the coefficients shows that
U^{r}
solves the stopped invariant-coordinate equation up to the exit time from the ball of radius
r
. Letting
r\uparrow\infty
and using a diagonal argument gives a weak solution
U
of (
4.4
), started from
u_{0}=w(x_{0})
, up to its explosion lifetime
\zeta^{U}
. Since the stopped limits take values in the closed set
\mathcal{K}
, the unstopped solution satisfies

U(t)\in\mathcal{K},\qquad 0\leq t<\zeta,

almost surely. Finally, because
U(t)\in\mathcal{K}=\overline{w[C_{+}]}
, the inverse map
f:\mathcal{K}\to\overline{C_{+}}
is well-defined, and therefore

X(t):=f(U(t))

is a continuous
\overline{C_{+}}
-valued process.
∎

5.3.
Instant diffraction

We have collected all ingredients to show that positive repulsion
(A1)
not only keeps the process away from the multiple collisions points after the start, but also immediately push the process away from the boundary.

Proposition 5

(Instantaneous diffraction)
.

Assume
(C1)
,
(C2)
, and
(A1)
. Let
U
be the
\mathcal{K}
-valued solution constructed in Proposition
4
, and put
X=f(U)
. Then the instant diffraction of the particles appears, i.e.

\inf\{t>0:X(t)\in C_{+}\}=0\qquad\text{a.s.}

Proof.

Let us consider the basic symmetric polynomials
e_{n}(\mathcal{A})
, where

\mathcal{A}=\left\{\left<x,\alpha\right>^{2}:\alpha\in R_{+}\right\}\/,\qquad n=1,\ldots,M\/,

where
M
is the size of
\mathcal{A}
, i.e.
M=|R_{+}|
. In particular, in the
A_{N-1}
case, we have
M=N(N-1)/2
, with
M=N(N-1)
for
D_{N}
and finally,
M=N^{2}
, when we consider
B_{N}
. In this part, we will slightly break with the standard notation. When we write
e_{n}(\mathcal{A})
, we will not explicitly indicate the point
x
for which we are calculating the value, but in fact
\mathcal{A}
depends on
x
and consequently
e_{n}(\mathcal{A})
also does. Consequently, we will use the same notation to refer to both a function of the variable x and the stochastic process that we get by applying this function to
X=(X_{1},\ldots,X_{N})
. However, it will always be clear from the context which function we are referring to.

Following the approach presented in Remark
4
together with the fact that
U
is a
\mathcal{K}
-valued solution, we get the stochastic description of
e_{n}(\mathcal{A})
in the following form

\displaystyle de_{n}(\mathcal{A})

\displaystyle=2\sum_{k=1}^{N}\sum_{\alpha\in R_{+}}\alpha_{k}\left<X,\alpha\right>e_{n-1}^{\,\,\overline{\alpha}}(\mathcal{A})\sigma_{k}(t,X)dB_{k}+2\sum_{k=1}^{N}\sum_{\alpha\in R_{+}}\alpha_{k}\left<X,\alpha\right>e_{n-1}^{\,\,\overline{\alpha}}(\mathcal{A})b_{k}(t,X)dt

\displaystyle+2\sum_{\alpha\in R_{+}}e_{n-1}^{\,\,\overline{\alpha}}(\mathcal{A})|\alpha|^{2}k_{\alpha}(t,X)\,dt+2\sum_{\beta\in R_{+}}\frac{k_{\beta}(t,X)}{\left<X,\beta\right>}\mathop{\sum_{\alpha\in R_{+}}}_{\alpha\neq\beta,\left<\alpha,\beta\right>\neq 0}\left<\alpha,\beta\right>\left<X,\alpha\right>e_{n-1}^{\,\,\overline{\alpha}}(\mathcal{A})dt

\displaystyle+2\sum_{k=1}^{N}\mathop{\sum_{\alpha\in R_{+}}\sum_{\beta\in R_{+}}}_{\alpha\neq\beta}\alpha_{k}\beta_{k}\left<X,\alpha\right>\left<X,\beta\right>e_{n-2}^{\overline{\alpha},\overline{\beta}}(\mathcal{A})\sigma^{2}_{k}(t,X)dt

\displaystyle+\sum_{k=1}^{N}\sum_{\alpha\in R_{+}}\alpha_{k}^{2}e_{n-1}^{\,\,\overline{\alpha}}(\mathcal{A})\sigma^{2}_{k}(t,X)dt\/.

Using (
3.2
) and the fact that if
\left<\alpha,\beta\right>\neq 0
then
\langle s^{+}_{\beta}(\alpha),\beta\rangle=\pm\left<\alpha,\beta\right>\neq 0
, we can remove the apparent singularity in the following way, where to shorten the notation we write
\gamma=s_{\beta}^{+}(\alpha)
. Then, by using the definition of
s_{\beta}^{+}(\alpha)=\pm(\alpha-2\left<\alpha,\beta\right>\beta/|\beta|^{2})
and noticing that the given below equalities are invariant under the choice of sign in
s_{\beta}^{+}(\alpha)
, we obtain

(5.25)

\left<\alpha,\beta\right>\left<x,\alpha\right>+\left<\beta,\gamma\right>\left<x,\gamma\right>=\frac{2\left<x,\beta\right>}{|\beta|^{2}}

and

(5.26)

\left<\alpha,\beta\right>\left<x,\gamma\right>+\left<\beta,\gamma\right>\left<x,\alpha\right>=\frac{2\left<\alpha,\beta\right>\left<\beta,\gamma\right>\left<x,\beta\right>}{|\beta|^{2}}\/.

This follows also from the fact that for the classical root systems considered here, if
\alpha\neq\beta
and
\langle\alpha,\beta\rangle\neq 0
, then
\langle\alpha,\beta\rangle^{2}=1
. Using those we get

\displaystyle 2\mathop{\sum_{\alpha\in R_{+}}}_{\alpha\neq\beta,\left<\alpha,\beta\right>\neq 0}

\displaystyle\left<\alpha,\beta\right>\left<x,\alpha\right>e_{n-1}^{\,\,\overline{\alpha}}(\mathcal{A})=\sum_{(\alpha,\gamma)\in R_{+}(\beta)}(\left<\alpha,\beta\right>\left<x,\alpha\right>+\left<\gamma,\beta\right>\left<x,\gamma\right>)e_{n-1}^{\overline{\alpha},\overline{\gamma}}(\mathcal{A})

\displaystyle+\sum_{(\alpha,\gamma)\in R_{+}(\beta)}\left<x,\alpha\right>\left<x,\gamma\right>(\left<\alpha,\beta\right>\left<x,\gamma\right>+\left<\gamma,\beta\right>\left<x,\alpha\right>)e_{n-2}^{\overline{\alpha},\overline{\gamma}}(\mathcal{A})

\displaystyle\underset{\eqref{eq:ac:form2}}{\overset{\eqref{eq:ac:form1}}{=}}\frac{2\left<x,\beta\right>}{|\beta|^{2}}\sum_{(\alpha,\gamma)\in R_{+}(\beta)}(e_{n-1}^{\overline{\alpha},\overline{\gamma}}(\mathcal{A})+\left<\alpha,\beta\right>\left<\beta,\gamma\right>\left<x,\alpha\right>\left<x,\gamma\right>e_{n-2}^{\overline{\alpha},\overline{\gamma}}(\mathcal{A})).

As usual, elementary symmetric polynomials of negative degree are understood to be zero. Let us assume that one of the processes
e_{n}(\mathcal{A})
, with
n=1,\ldots,M
, stays to be zero for
t\in[0,T_{n}]
, where
T_{n}
is positive with positive probability. It means that the drift part of
e_{n}(\mathcal{A})=0
on this time interval as well as all the products of
\left<X,\alpha\right>
of length
n
are zero there. In particular
\left<X,\alpha\right>e_{n-1}^{\,\,\overline{\alpha}}(\mathcal{A})=0
,
\alpha_{k}\beta_{k}\left<X,\alpha\right>\left<X,\beta\right>e_{n-2}^{\overline{\alpha},\overline{\beta}}(\mathcal{A})=0
and
\left<X,\alpha\right>\left<X,\gamma\right>e_{n-2}^{\overline{\alpha},\overline{\gamma}}(\mathcal{A})=0
. Using the non-negativity of the remain components of the drift term we arrive at

\sum_{\alpha\in R_{+}}e_{n-1}^{\,\,\overline{\alpha}}(\mathcal{A})|\alpha|^{2}k_{\alpha}(t,X)=0\/,\quad t\in[0,T_{n}]\/.

Now
(A1)
implies that
e_{n-1}^{\,\,\overline{\alpha}}(\mathcal{A})=0
for every
\alpha\in R_{+}
, which is equivalent to
e_{n-1}(\mathcal{A})=0
on
[0,T_{n}]
. Inductively we obtain

e_{M}(\mathcal{A})=\ldots=e_{1}(\mathcal{A})=e_{0}(\mathcal{A})=0

for every
t\in[0,T_{n}]
with positive probability. Since
e_{0}(\mathcal{A})\equiv 1
we get a contradiction, which ends the proof.
∎

5.4.
Properties of
X

Using the results from the previous section we can finally state the general no multiple collision result.

Proposition 6

(No multiple collisions)
.

Assume
(C1)
,
(C2)
, and
(A1)
. Let
U
be the
\mathcal{K}
-valued solution constructed in Proposition
4
, and put
X=f(U)
. Then for every
X(0)=x_{0}\in\overline{C_{+}}
, the process
X
does not visit multiple collision points after the start.

Proof.

If
x_{0}\in C_{+}
, then Proposition
2
excludes visits to
\partial C_{+}^{(m)}
,
m\geq 2
, up to
\tau_{\mathcal{K}}
. By Proposition
3
,
\tau_{\mathcal{K}}=\zeta^{X}
, and hence no multiple collision occurs before the lifetime. Let now
x_{0}\in\overline{C_{+}}
. By Proposition
5
, there exist times
t_{n}\downarrow 0
such that
X(t_{n})\in C_{+}
a.s. Applying the preceding interior-start argument to the shifted process
(X(t_{n}+s))_{s\geq 0}
gives absence of multiple collisions on
[t_{n},\zeta^{X})
for each
n
. Since
t_{n}\downarrow 0
, the assertion follows for every positive time.
∎

Remark 5
.

Notice that the proof of the above Proposition shows a slightly more general fact. Namely, we obtain that when starting from a point
X(0)=x_{0}\in\overline{C_{+}}
, which is not a multiple collision point, the process never visits multiple collision boundary points
y\in\partial C_{+}
for which we have
k_{\alpha}(t,y)>0
.

Finally, we end this section with the proof of the non-explosion result stated in Theorem
5
. The argument is standard, but we include the details for completeness. Note that

Proof of Theorem
5
.

Let
X
be a solution to (
2.4
) or the degenerate interior equation (
2.9
) and define

\displaystyle V(t):=1+|X(t)|^{2},\qquad 0\leq t<\zeta^{X}.

For
\alpha\in R_{+}
, set

\displaystyle\vartheta_{\alpha}(s)

\displaystyle:=\begin{cases}1,&\text{for the equations without the indicator,}\\[5.69054pt]
\mathbf{1}_{\{\langle X(s),\alpha\rangle>0\}},&\text{for the equation with the indicator.}\end{cases}

In particular, in all cases,
0\leq\vartheta_{\alpha}(s)\leq 1
. Applying Itô’s formula gives, for every
t<\zeta^{X}
,

\displaystyle V(t)

\displaystyle=V(0)+M(t)+\int_{0}^{t}\left(2\sum_{i=1}^{N}X_{i}(s)b_{i}(s,X(s))+\sum_{i=1}^{N}\sigma_{i}^{2}(s,X(s))\right)\,ds

(5.27)

\displaystyle\quad+2\sum_{\alpha\in R_{+}}\int_{0}^{t}k_{\alpha}(s,X(s))\vartheta_{\alpha}(s)\,ds,

where

\displaystyle M(t)

\displaystyle:=2\sum_{i=1}^{N}\int_{0}^{t}X_{i}(s)\sigma_{i}(s,X(s))\,dB_{i}(s).

Fix
T>0
and define

\displaystyle\tau_{r}

\displaystyle:=\inf\{0\leq t<\zeta^{X}:\ V(t)\geq r\},\qquad r>1,

with the convention that the infimum of the empty set is
\zeta^{X}
.
By the definition of the explosion lifetime,
\tau_{r}\uparrow\zeta^{X}
as
r\to\infty
. Let

\displaystyle K_{r}:=\{x\in\overline{C_{+}}:\ 1+|x|^{2}\leq r\}.

On the stochastic interval
[0,T\wedge\tau_{r}]
, apart from the trivial case
\tau_{r}=0
, the process remains in the compact set
K_{r}
. Since the coefficients are continuous, the functions
x_{i}\sigma_{i}(t,x)
are bounded on
[0,T]\times K_{r}
. Hence the stopped martingale
M(t\wedge\tau_{r})
is square-integrable on
[0,T]
. Let

\displaystyle A_{m}

\displaystyle:=\{V(0)\leq m\},\qquad m\in\mathbb{N}.

Since
A_{m}\in\mathcal{F}_{0}
and
M(t\wedge\tau_{r})
is a square-integrable martingale, we have

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}\left[\mathbf{1}_{A_{m}}M(t\wedge\tau_{r})\right]

\displaystyle=0,\qquad 0\leq t\leq T.

Moreover, since
k_{\alpha}\geq 0
and
0\leq\vartheta_{\alpha}(s)\leq 1
, Assumption
(G1)
yields, for
0\leq s\leq T
and
s<\zeta^{X}
,

\displaystyle 2\sum_{i=1}^{N}X_{i}(s)b_{i}(s,X(s))+\sum_{i=1}^{N}\sigma_{i}^{2}(s,X(s))+2\sum_{\alpha\in R_{+}}k_{\alpha}(s,X(s))\vartheta_{\alpha}(s)

\displaystyle\qquad\leq 2\left(\sum_{i=1}^{N}X_{i}(s)b_{i}(s,X(s))+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\sigma_{i}^{2}(s,X(s))+\sum_{\alpha\in R_{+}}k_{\alpha}(s,X(s))\right)

(5.28)

\displaystyle\qquad\leq 2C_{T}\bigl(1+|X(s)|^{2}\bigr)=2C_{T}V(s).

Stopping the identity for
V
at
\tau_{r}
, multiplying by
\mathbf{1}_{A_{m}}
, taking expectations, and using
(
5.28
), we obtain, for
0\leq t\leq T
,

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}\left[\mathbf{1}_{A_{m}}V(t\wedge\tau_{r})\right]

\displaystyle\leq\mathbf{E}\left[\mathbf{1}_{A_{m}}V(0)\right]+2C_{T}\int_{0}^{t}\mathbf{E}\left[\mathbf{1}_{A_{m}}V(s\wedge\tau_{r})\right]\,ds

\displaystyle\leq m+2C_{T}\int_{0}^{t}\mathbf{E}\left[\mathbf{1}_{A_{m}}V(s\wedge\tau_{r})\right]\,ds.

Gronwall’s lemma gives

(5.29)

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}\left[\mathbf{1}_{A_{m}}V(t\wedge\tau_{r})\right]

\displaystyle\leq me^{2C_{T}t}\leq me^{2C_{T}T},\qquad 0\leq t\leq T.

On the event
A_{m}\cap\{\tau_{r}\leq t\}
we have
V(t\wedge\tau_{r})=V(\tau_{r})\geq r
. Therefore (
5.29
) implies

(5.30)

\displaystyle r\,\mathbf{P}\bigl(A_{m}\cap\{\tau_{r}\leq t\}\bigr)

\displaystyle\leq me^{2C_{T}T},\qquad 0\leq t\leq T.

Since
\tau_{r}\uparrow\zeta^{X}
as
r\to\infty
, we have
\{\zeta^{X}\leq t\}\subseteq\{\tau_{r}\leq t\}
,
r>0
. Hence, by (
5.30
),

\displaystyle\mathbf{P}\bigl(A_{m}\cap\{\zeta^{X}\leq t\}\bigr)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbf{P}\bigl(A_{m}\cap\{\tau_{r}\leq t\}\bigr)\leq\frac{me^{2C_{T}T}}{r}.

Letting
r\to\infty
gives

(5.31)

\displaystyle\mathbf{P}\bigl(A_{m}\cap\{\zeta^{X}\leq t\}\bigr)

\displaystyle=0,\qquad 0\leq t\leq T.

Since
V(0)<\infty
almost surely,
\mathbf{P}\left(\bigcup_{m=1}^{\infty}A_{m}\right)=1
. Letting
m\to\infty
in (
5.31
), we obtain

(5.32)

\displaystyle\mathbf{P}(\zeta^{X}\leq t)

\displaystyle=0,\qquad 0\leq t\leq T.

Finally, since
T>0
was arbitrary,
\zeta^{X}=\infty
almost surely.
∎

6.
Integrability of the singular drift part

The most delicate part of the proof is related to show that for
x=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{N})
defined in (
4.6
), the singular part of the drift from (
1.2
) is integrable, i.e. for every
i=1,\ldots,N
we have

(6.1)

\int_{0}^{t}\dfrac{k_{\alpha}(s,X(s))}{\left<X_{s},\alpha\right>}ds<\infty\qquad\alpha\in R_{+},\quad t<\zeta^{x}\/,

almost surely. Essentially, the above condition is almost sufficient for
x
to be a solution, as we demonstrate in the following proposition.

Recall that for every
i=1,2,\ldots,N
we introduced
R_{+}^{\,i}=\{\alpha\in R_{+}:\alpha_{i}\neq 0\}
and consider the corresponding sets

\mathcal{A}^{i}=\left\{\left<x,\alpha\right>^{2}:\alpha\in R_{+}^{\,i}\right\}\/,\quad\mathcal{B}^{i}=\left\{\left<x,\alpha\right>:\alpha\in R_{+}^{\,i}\right\}\/,

where we collect all the expressions
\left<x,\alpha\right>^{2}
(or
\left<x,\alpha\right>
) in which
x_{i}
appears. Note that for fixed
N
the number of elements in
R_{+}^{\,i}
is the same for every
i
and let us denote it by
M=\#R_{+}^{\,i}
. In the
A
case we simply have
M=N-1
.

Consider
e_{n}(\mathcal{A}^{i})
,
n=1,\ldots,M
, which control the collisions between
x_{i}
and other particles (or the wall of the Weyl chamber). These are definitely continuous processes. We can also apply the Itô formula, for every starting point in the interior of the Weyl chamber, to get (at least up to the first hitting time of
\partial C_{+}
) that

\displaystyle de_{n}(\mathcal{A}^{i})

\displaystyle=2\sum_{k=1}^{N}\sigma_{k}(t,X)\sum_{\alpha\in R_{+}^{\,i}}\alpha_{k}\left<X,\alpha\right>e_{n-1}^{\,\,\overline{\alpha}}(\mathcal{A}^{i})dB_{k}+2\sum_{k=1}^{N}b_{k}(t,X)\sum_{\alpha\in R_{+}^{\,i}}\alpha_{k}\left<X,\alpha\right>e_{n-1}^{\,\,\overline{\alpha}}(\mathcal{A}^{i})dt

\displaystyle+\sum_{k=1}^{N}\sigma^{2}_{k}(t,X)\sum_{\alpha\in R_{+}^{\,i}}\alpha_{k}^{2}e_{n-1}^{\,\,\overline{\alpha}}(\mathcal{A}^{i})dt+2\sum_{\alpha\in R_{+}^{\,i}}\sum_{\beta\in R_{+}}\dfrac{\left<\alpha,\beta\right>\left<X,\alpha\right>}{\left<X,\beta\right>}e_{n-1}^{\,\,\overline{\alpha}}(\mathcal{A}^{i})k_{\beta}(t,X)

(6.2)

\displaystyle+2\sum_{k=1}^{N}\sigma^{2}_{k}(t,X)\mathop{\sum_{\alpha\in R_{+}^{\,i}}\sum_{\beta\in R_{+}^{\,i}}}_{\alpha\neq\beta}\left<X,\alpha\right>\left<X,\beta\right>\alpha_{k}\beta_{k}e_{n-2}^{\overline{\alpha},\overline{\beta}}(\mathcal{A}^{i})dt\/.

At the first glance the last term above is singular and it is in fact true for every
n=1,\ldots,M-1
. However, the first crucial observation in this construction is that it is not the case for
n=M
, i.e. the function appearing in this expression can be extended to a continuous function on
\overline{C_{+}}
. Indeed, note that for
\alpha,\beta\in R_{+}^{\,i}
such that
\alpha\neq\beta
we have
e_{M-1}^{\,\,\overline{\alpha}}(\mathcal{A}^{i})=\left<x,\beta\right>^{2}e_{M-2}^{\overline{\alpha},\overline{\beta}}(\mathcal{A}^{i})
, which is exceptional in the case
n=M
, since in general we have
e_{M-1}^{\,\,\overline{\alpha}}(\mathcal{A}^{i})=\left<x,\beta\right>^{2}e_{M-2}^{\overline{\alpha},\overline{\beta}}(\mathcal{A}^{i})+e_{M-1}^{\overline{\alpha},\overline{\beta}}(\mathcal{A}^{i})
.

For
\alpha\in R_{+}^{\,i}
and
\beta\notin R_{+}^{\,i}
such that
\left<\alpha,\beta\right>\neq 0
let us denote
\gamma=s^{+}_{\alpha}(\beta)
, where
s^{+}_{\alpha}(\beta)
was defined in Section
3
. Thus

\displaystyle\sum_{\alpha\in R_{+}^{\,i}}\sum_{\beta\in R_{+}}

\displaystyle\dfrac{\left<\alpha,\beta\right>\left<x,\alpha\right>}{\left<x,\beta\right>}e_{M-1}^{\,\,\overline{\alpha}}(\mathcal{A}^{i})k_{\beta}(x)=\mathop{\sum_{\alpha\in R_{+}^{\,i}}\sum_{\beta\in R_{+}^{\,i}}}_{\alpha\neq\beta}\left<\alpha,\beta\right>\left<x,\alpha\right>\left<x,\beta\right>e_{M-2}^{\overline{\alpha},\overline{\beta}}(\mathcal{A}^{i})k_{\beta}(x)

\displaystyle+\sum_{\alpha\in R_{+}^{\,i}}|\alpha|^{2}e_{M-1}^{\,\,\overline{\alpha}}(\mathcal{A}^{i})k_{\alpha}(x)+\sum_{\beta\notin R_{+}^{\,i}}k_{\beta}(x)\sum_{\alpha\in R_{+}^{\,i}}\frac{\left<\alpha,\beta\right>\left<x,\alpha\right>\left<x,\gamma\right>^{2}}{\left<x,\beta\right>}e_{M-2}^{\overline{\alpha},\overline{\gamma}}(\mathcal{A}^{i})\/.

To deal with the last expression observe that
\left<\gamma,\beta\right>=-\left<\alpha,\beta\right>
and consequently,
\gamma(\gamma(\alpha,\beta),\beta)=\alpha
. Since

\displaystyle\left<\alpha,\beta\right>\left<x,\gamma(\alpha,\beta)\right>+\left<\gamma(\alpha,\beta),\beta\right>\left<x,\alpha\right>

\displaystyle=-\frac{2\left<\alpha,\beta\right>\left<x,\beta\right>}{|\beta|^{2}}\/,

we obtain the following simplification

\displaystyle\sum_{\alpha\in R_{+}^{\,i}}\frac{\left<\alpha,\beta\right>\left<x,\alpha\right>\left<x,\gamma\right>^{2}}{\left<x,\beta\right>}e_{M-2}^{\overline{\alpha},\overline{\gamma}}(\mathcal{A}^{i})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\alpha\in R_{+}^{\,i}}\frac{\left<x,\alpha\right>\left<x,\gamma\right>(\left<\alpha,\beta\right>\left<x,\gamma\right>+\left<\gamma,\beta\right>\left<x,\alpha\right>)}{\left<x,\beta\right>}e_{M-2}^{\overline{\alpha},\overline{\gamma}}(\mathcal{A}^{i})

\displaystyle=-\sum_{\alpha\in R_{+}^{\,i}}\frac{\left<\alpha,\beta\right>}{|\beta|^{2}}\left<x,\alpha\right>\left<x,\gamma\right>e_{M-2}^{\overline{\alpha},\overline{\gamma}}(\mathcal{A}^{i})\/.

In the next proposition we in fact show that
e_{M}(\mathcal{A}^{i})
are semimartingales and the above-given description holds without any additional assumption on the starting point and with no restriction on time.

Proposition 7
.

For every
i=1,\ldots,N
the processes
e_{M}(\mathcal{A}^{i})
are semi-martingales described by

(6.3)

de_{M}(\mathcal{A}^{i})=2e_{M}(\mathcal{B}^{i})\sum_{k=1}^{N}\sigma_{k}(t,X)\sum_{\alpha\in R_{+}^{\,i}}\alpha_{k}e_{M-1}^{\overline{\alpha}}(\mathcal{B}^{i})dB_{k}+(V^{(1)}_{M}(X)+e_{M}(\mathcal{B}^{i})V_{M}^{(2)}(X))dt\/,

where

\displaystyle V^{(1)}_{M}(X)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{N}\sigma^{2}_{k}(t,X)\left(\sum_{\alpha\in R_{+}^{\,i}}\alpha_{k}e_{M-1}^{\overline{\alpha}}(\mathcal{B}^{i})\right)^{2}+2\sum_{\alpha\in R_{+}^{\,i}}|\alpha|^{2}e_{M-1}^{\,\,\overline{\alpha}}(\mathcal{A}^{i})k_{\alpha}(t,X)

and

\displaystyle V^{(2)}_{M}(X)

\displaystyle=2\sum_{k=1}^{N}\sigma^{2}_{k}(t,X)\mathop{\sum_{\alpha\in R_{+}^{\,i}}\sum_{\beta\in R_{+}^{\,i}}}_{\alpha\neq\beta}\alpha_{k}\beta_{k}e_{M-2}^{\overline{\alpha},\overline{\beta}}(\mathcal{B}^{i})+2\sum_{k=1}^{N}b_{k}(t,X)\sum_{\alpha\in R_{+}^{\,i}}\alpha_{k}e_{M-1}^{\overline{\alpha}}(\mathcal{B}^{i})

\displaystyle+2\mathop{\sum_{\alpha\in R_{+}^{\,i}}\sum_{\beta\in R_{+}^{\,i}}}_{\alpha\neq\beta}\left<\alpha,\beta\right>e_{M-2}^{\overline{\alpha},\overline{\beta}}(\mathcal{B}^{i})k_{\beta}(x)-2\sum_{\beta\notin R_{+}^{\,i}}\sum_{\alpha\in R_{+}^{\,i}}\frac{\left<\alpha,\sigma_{\beta}(\alpha)\right>}{|\beta|^{2}}e_{M-2}^{\overline{\alpha},\overline{\sigma_{\beta}(\alpha)}}(\mathcal{B}^{i})k_{\beta}(x)\/.

Proof.

We start with the following calculation in
A_{N-1}
case, where we exploit the fact that
e_{M}(\mathcal{B}^{i})
is just characteristic polynomial with roots
\{x_{j}:j\neq i\}
evaluated at point
x_{i}
, which immediately gives

\displaystyle e_{M}(\mathcal{B}^{i})

\displaystyle=\prod_{\alpha\in R_{+}^{\,i}}\left<x,\alpha\right>=(-1)^{i+1}\sum_{k=0}^{N-1}x_{i}^{N-1-k}(-1)^{k}e_{k}^{\overline{x_{i}}}(x)

\displaystyle=(-1)^{i+1}\sum_{k=0}^{N-1}(N-k)\,x_{i}^{N-1-k}(-1)^{k}e_{k}(x)\/.

It is now easy to see that for
R=B_{N}
we have

e_{M}(\mathcal{B}^{i})=x_{i}\sum_{k=0}^{N-1}(N-k)\,(x_{i}^{2})^{N-1-k}(-1)^{k}e_{k}(x^{2})\/,

where
x^{2}=(x_{1}^{2},\ldots,x_{N}^{2})
and removing the first factor
x_{i}
we get the representation for
R=D_{N}
. We can thus conclude that in general we have

e_{M}(\mathcal{B}^{i})=\sum_{k=0}^{N-1}g_{k}^{R}(x_{i})H_{k}^{R}(u_{1},\ldots,u_{N})\/,

where
g_{k}^{R}
is suitable power function and, which is more important,
H_{k}^{R}
is smooth (polynomial in fact) function of
u=(u_{1},\ldots,u_{N})
. This together with Proposition
22
shows that
e_{M}(\mathcal{A}^{i})
is a smooth function of
u=(u_{1},\ldots,u_{N})
whenever we are away from this part of the boundary which corresponds to collisions involving
x_{i}
with its neighbors. Indeed, for
R=A_{N-1}
we have

\displaystyle\dfrac{\partial}{\partial p_{k}}(e_{M}(\mathcal{A}^{i}))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2e_{M}(\mathcal{B}^{i})\dfrac{\partial}{\partial p_{k}}\sum_{l=0}^{N-1}g_{l}^{A}(x_{i})H_{l}^{A}(p_{1},\ldots,p_{N})

which by (
13.2
) is equal to

2\sum_{l=0}^{N-1}(g_{l}^{A})^{\prime}(x_{i})(-1)^{i+k}{e_{N-k+1}^{\overline{x_{i}}}(x)}H_{l}^{A}(p_{1},\ldots,p_{N})+2e_{M}(\mathcal{B}^{i})\sum_{l=0}^{N-1}g_{l}^{A}(x_{i})\dfrac{\partial}{\partial p_{k}}H_{l}^{A}(p_{1},\ldots,p_{N})\/.

Obviously it is a smooth function. Moreover, we can exploit the relation
{e_{l}^{\overline{x_{i}}}(x)}=e_{l}(x)-x_{i}e_{l-1}^{\overline{x_{i}}}(x)
to represents each of
{e_{N-k+1}^{\overline{x_{i}}}(x)}
as a polynomial in
x_{i}
with coefficients being smooth functions of
(p_{1},\ldots,p_{N})
. Another usage of (
13.2
) will show that the second derivative with respect to
p_{k}
and
p_{l}
of
e_{M}(\mathcal{A}^{i})
will be a smooth function whenever
e_{M}(\mathcal{A}^{i})>0
(More precisely on the corresponding image of
\{x:e_{M}(\mathcal{A}^{i})>0\}
by
p=(p_{1},\ldots,p_{N})
). It is because the singular expression
1/e_{M}(\mathcal{B}^{i})
will appear and we do not have the factor
e_{M}(\mathcal{B}^{i})
anymore to remove this singularity as it has happened in the first derivative. Note that in the
B_{N}
case we consider polynomials
(p_{1},\ldots,p_{N})
of
(x_{1}^{2},\ldots,x_{N}^{2})
and
e_{M}(\mathcal{A}^{i})
is just the same function as in
A_{N-1}
case with
(x_{1},\ldots,x_{N})
replaced by
(x_{1}^{2},\ldots,x_{N}^{2})
and multiplied by
x_{i}^{2}
. Consequently, the similar arguments lead to the corresponding claim. Finally, in
D_{N}
case, in
e_{M}(\mathcal{A}^{i})
we just replace particles by its squares and the result follows directly from the consideration in
A_{N-1}
case.

Now let us consider the family of functions
\psi_{\varepsilon}:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
for
\varepsilon>0
defined by
\psi_{\varepsilon}(z)=z\varphi(z/\varepsilon)
, where
\varphi
is a fix smooth function on
\mathbb{R}
with values in
[0,1]
such that
\varphi(z)=0
for
|z|<1
and
\varphi(z)=1
for
|z|\geq 2
. It is then clear that we have the following point wise convergence

(6.4)

\displaystyle\psi_{\varepsilon}(z)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\varepsilon\to 0}}{{\longrightarrow}}

\displaystyle z\/,\quad z\in\mathbb{R}\/,

(6.5)

\displaystyle\psi^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}(z)=\varphi(z/\varepsilon)+\frac{z}{\varepsilon}\varphi^{\prime}(z/\varepsilon)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\varepsilon\to 0}}{{\longrightarrow}}

\displaystyle\mathbf{1}_{\mathbb{R}\setminus\{0\}}(z)\/,\quad z\in\mathbb{R}\/,

(6.6)

\displaystyle\psi^{\prime\prime}_{\varepsilon}(z)=\frac{2}{\varepsilon}\varphi^{\prime}(z/\varepsilon)+\frac{z}{\varepsilon^{2}}\varphi^{\prime\prime}(z/\varepsilon)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\varepsilon\to 0}}{{\longrightarrow}}

\displaystyle 0\/,\quad z\in\mathbb{R}\/,

since we have
\varphi^{\prime}(z/\varepsilon)=\varphi^{\prime\prime}(z/\varepsilon)=0
for every
|z|>2\varepsilon
as well as for
z=0
. Moreover, we have the following uniform estimates

(6.7)

\displaystyle|\psi^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}(z)|\leq||\varphi||_{\infty}+||\varphi^{\prime}||_{\infty}\/,\quad|z\psi^{\prime\prime}(z)|\leq 2||\varphi^{\prime}||_{\infty}+||\varphi^{\prime\prime}||_{\infty}\/,

which hold for every
z\in\mathbb{R}
and
\varepsilon>0
. Using
\psi_{\varepsilon}
we consider the family of diffusions
\psi_{\varepsilon}(e_{M}(\mathcal{A}^{i}))
, which are well-defined semi-martingales for every
t<T
. Indeed, as we have already seen, the function

u\longrightarrow\psi_{\varepsilon}\left(\left(\sum_{k=0}^{N-1}g_{k}^{R}(f_{i}(u))H_{k}^{R}(u_{1},\ldots,u_{N})\right)^{2}\right)

is smooth, because the function
\psi_{\varepsilon}
removes the problem with singularity of the derivatives of
f_{i}(u)
, which appears when
\left<x,\alpha\right>=0
for
\alpha\in R_{+}^{\,i}
or equivalently
e_{M}(\mathcal{A}^{i})=0
. The Itô formula gives that

(6.8)

\displaystyle d\psi_{\varepsilon}(e_{M}(\mathcal{A}^{i}))

\displaystyle=2\psi_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}(e_{M}(\mathcal{A}^{i}))e_{M}(\mathcal{B}^{i})\sum_{k=1}^{N}\sigma_{k}(x)\sum_{\alpha\in R_{+}^{\,i}}\alpha_{k}e_{M-1}^{\overline{\alpha}}(\mathcal{B}^{i})dB_{k}

(6.9)

\displaystyle+\psi_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}(e_{M}(\mathcal{A}^{i}))(V_{M}^{(1)}(X)+V_{M}^{(2)}(X)e_{M}(\mathcal{B}^{i}))dt

(6.10)

\displaystyle+2\psi_{\varepsilon}^{\prime\prime}(e_{M}(\mathcal{A}^{i}))e_{M}(\mathcal{A}^{i})\sum_{k=1}^{N}\sigma_{k}^{2}(x)\left(\sum_{\alpha\in R_{+}^{\,i}}\alpha_{k}e_{M-1}^{\overline{\alpha}}(\mathcal{B}^{i})\right)^{2}dt

Standard localization argument, i.e. considering our processes for
t<T_{K}=\inf\{x:|x_{i}|\leq K,i=1,\ldots,N\}
and then taking
K\to\infty
, which gives
T_{K}\to T
, allows us to consider all the above-given coefficients to by uniformly (for
t<T_{K}
) bounded.
Then using the bounds in (
6.7
) we can easily show that (
6.10
) vanishes when
\varepsilon\to 0
. Since
\psi_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}(z)\to\mathbf{1}_{\mathbb{R}\setminus\{0\}}(z)
and
e_{M}(\mathcal{B}^{i})
vanishes iff
e_{M}(\mathcal{A}^{i})
vanishes we get that

\psi_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}(e_{M}(\mathcal{A}^{i}))e_{M}(\mathcal{B}^{i})\longrightarrow e_{M}(\mathcal{B}^{i})

and consequently the right-hand side of (
6.8
) goes to the

2e_{M}(\mathcal{B}^{i})\sum_{k=1}^{N}\sigma_{k}(x)\sum_{\alpha\in R_{+}^{\,i}}\alpha_{k}e_{M-1}^{\overline{\alpha}}(\mathcal{B}^{i})dB_{k}\/,

which follows from the
L_{2}
property of the Ito integral. Finally,

\psi_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}(e_{M}(\mathcal{A}^{i}))(V_{M}^{(1)}+V_{M}^{(2)}e_{M}(\mathcal{B}^{i}))dt\longrightarrow\mathbf{1}_{\mathbb{R}\setminus\{0\}}(e_{M}(\mathcal{A}^{i}))(V_{M}^{(1)}(X)+V_{M}^{(2)}(X)e_{M}(\mathcal{B}^{i}))dt\/.

Since
\psi_{\varepsilon}(e_{M}(\mathcal{A}^{i}))\to e_{M}(\mathcal{A}^{i})
we have just proved that
e_{M}(\mathcal{A}^{i})
is a semi-martingale for every
t<T
given by (
6.3
) but with additional indicator
\mathbf{1}_{\mathbb{R}\setminus\{0\}}(e_{M}(\mathcal{A}^{i}))
in the drift part. To finish the proof it is enough to show that we can replace this indicator simply by
1
. Indeed, taking the semi-martingale
Y_{t}=e_{M}(\mathcal{A}^{i})_{t}
, since the process is non-negative,
L_{t}^{0-}[Y]_{t}\equiv 0
, where
L_{t}^{a}[Y]
denotes the local time of
Y
. From the other-side, the occupation times formula gives

\displaystyle\int_{[0,\infty)}\mathbf{1}_{\mathbb{R}\setminus\{0\}}(a)\frac{1}{a}L_{t}^{a}[Y]da

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{t}\mathbf{1}_{\mathbb{R}\setminus\{0\}}(Y_{s})\frac{1}{Y_{s}}dY_{s}dY_{s}

\displaystyle=4\int_{0}^{t}\mathbf{1}_{\mathbb{R}\setminus\{0\}}(e_{M}(\mathcal{A}^{i})_{s})\sum_{k=1}^{N}\sigma_{k}^{2}(s,X_{s})\left(\sum_{\alpha\in R_{+}^{\,i}}\alpha_{k}e_{M-1}^{\overline{\alpha}}(\mathcal{B}^{i})_{s}\right)^{2}ds\/,

where the last expression is always finite. It implies that
L_{t}^{0}[Y]=\lim_{a\to 0^{+}}L_{t}^{a}[Y]=0
. This together with the formula (see
[
39
]
Theorem 1.7 page 225)

0=L_{t}^{0}[Y]-L_{t}^{0-}[Y]=\int_{0}^{t}\mathbf{1}_{\{0\}}(Y_{s})dV_{s}

with
V=V_{M}^{(1)}+V_{M}^{(2)}e_{M}(\mathcal{B}^{i})
implies the desired result.
∎

Proposition 8
.

Assume that
X=(X_{1},\ldots,X_{N})
is a diffusion process defined in (
3
) such that
X(0)\in C_{+}
. If the continuity assumption
(C1)
,
(C2)
together with
(A1)
hold, then for every
\alpha\in R_{+}

(6.11)

\int_{0}^{t}\frac{k_{\alpha}(s,X(s))}{\left<X(s),\alpha\right>}ds<\infty\/,\quad

for every
t<\zeta^{X}
.

Proof.

We start by localizing our consideration and define
T_{r}=\inf\{t:|X_{t}|>r\}\wedge\inf\{t>0:e_{M-1}(\mathcal{A}^{i})<1/r\}\wedge r
for given and fixed
r>0
such that
|X(0)|<r
. We will consider
t<T_{r}
. We begin with the following simple computation

\int_{0}^{t}\sum_{\alpha\in R_{+}^{\,i}}\dfrac{k_{\alpha}(s,X(s))|\alpha|^{2}}{\left<X(s),\alpha\right>}ds=\int_{0}^{t}\sum_{\alpha\in R_{+}^{\,i}}|\alpha|^{2}e_{M-1}^{\overline{\alpha}}(\mathcal{B}^{i})\frac{k_{\alpha}(s,X(s))}{e_{M}(\mathcal{B}^{i})}ds\/.

Recall that Proposition
6
implies the existence of the positive
c_{3}=c_{3}(\omega)>0
such that
e_{M-1}(\mathcal{B}^{i})_{t}>c_{3}
for every
t<T_{r}
, which gives that the last integral can be bounded from above by

\displaystyle\frac{1}{c_{3}}\int_{0}^{t}\sum_{\alpha\in R_{+}^{\,i}}|\alpha|^{2}e_{M-1}^{\overline{\alpha}}(\mathcal{B}^{i})e_{M-1}(\mathcal{B}^{i})\frac{k_{\alpha}(s,X(s))}{e_{M}(\mathcal{B}^{i})_{s}}ds\/,

where we also have

\displaystyle\sum_{\alpha\in R_{+}^{\,i}}|\alpha|^{2}e_{M-1}^{\overline{\alpha}}(\mathcal{B}^{i})e_{M-1}(\mathcal{B}^{i})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{\alpha\in R_{+}^{\,i}}|\alpha|^{2}e_{M-1}^{\,\,\overline{\alpha}}(\mathcal{A}^{i})+\mathop{\sum_{\alpha\in R_{+}^{\,i}}\sum_{\beta\in R_{+}^{\,i}}}_{\alpha\neq\beta}|\alpha|^{2}e_{M-1}^{\overline{\alpha}}(\mathcal{B}^{i})e_{M-1}^{\overline{\beta}}(\mathcal{B}^{i})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{\alpha\in R_{+}^{\,i}}|\alpha|^{2}e_{M-1}^{\,\,\overline{\alpha}}(\mathcal{A}^{i})+\mathop{\sum_{\alpha\in R_{+}^{\,i}}\sum_{\beta\in R_{+}^{\,i}}}_{\alpha\neq\beta}|\alpha|^{2}e_{M}(\mathcal{B}^{i})e_{M-2}^{\overline{\alpha},\overline{\beta}}(\mathcal{B}^{i})\/.

Since the factor
e_{M}(\mathcal{B}^{i})
in the last sum cancels the singularity in the integral, we can just reduce our consideration to show the finiteness of the integral

\int_{0}^{t}\sum_{\alpha\in R_{+}^{\,i}}|\alpha|^{2}e_{M-1}^{\,\,\overline{\alpha}}(\mathcal{A}^{i})\frac{k_{\alpha}(s,X(s))}{e_{M}(\mathcal{B}^{i})_{s}}ds\/.

Note that since
e_{M-1}(\mathcal{A}^{i})
stays strictly positive, we will get
\lim_{r\to\infty}T_{r}=\zeta^{X}
. Due to the continuity of the coefficient and the lack of singularities in (
6.3
), we can find a constant
c_{1}=c_{1}(r)>0
such that
|V_{M}^{(2)}(x)|\leq c_{1}(r)
for every
x\in\mathbb{R}^{N}
such that
|x|\leq r
. Moreover, by
(A1)
, the functions
(t,x)\to\sigma_{k}(t,x)
and
(t,x)\to k_{\alpha}(t,x)
are continuous, and the latter is additionally strictly positive on the compact set
\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{N}:|x|\leq r\}\cap\partial C_{+}
. It means that there exist
\varepsilon_{0}=\varepsilon_{0}(r)>0
,
\gamma=\gamma(r)>0
, and
c_{2}=c_{2}(r)>0
such that for every
t<T_{r}
and
x
, for which
\prod_{\alpha\in R_{+}^{i}}\left<x,\alpha\right>^{2}\leq\varepsilon_{0}
, we have that

(6.12)

\sum_{\alpha\in R_{+}^{\,i}}e_{M-1}^{\,\,\overline{\alpha}}(\mathcal{A}^{i})\sum_{k=1}^{N}\alpha_{k}^{2}\sigma^{2}_{k}(t,X)\leq\frac{1}{M\gamma}\sum_{\alpha\in R_{+}^{\,i}}|\alpha|^{2}e_{M-1}^{\,\,\overline{\alpha}}(\mathcal{A}^{i})k_{\alpha}(t,X)

and

(6.13)

c_{2}\sum_{\alpha\in R_{+}^{\,i}}|\alpha|^{2}e_{M-1}^{\,\,\overline{\alpha}}(\mathcal{A}^{i})\leq\sum_{\alpha\in R_{+}^{\,i}}|\alpha|^{2}e_{M-1}^{\,\,\overline{\alpha}}(\mathcal{A}^{i})k_{\alpha}(t,X)

Indeed, if we take
\alpha\in R_{+}
such that
\left<x,\alpha\right>
is sufficiently small
(A1)
gives

(6.14)

\sum_{k=1}^{N}\alpha_{k}^{2}\sigma^{2}_{k}(t,x)\leq\frac{|\alpha|^{2}}{2M\gamma}k_{\alpha}(t,x)\quad\textrm{ and }\quad k_{\alpha}(t,x)\geq c_{2}/2\/,

For those
\alpha\in R_{+}
which are not making
\left<x,\alpha\right>
small, the small factors
\left<x,\beta\right>
appear in
e_{M-1}^{\,\,\overline{\alpha}}(\mathcal{A}^{i})
and those elements of the sums can be consumed by the previous ones again due to the positivity of
k_{\alpha}
. We set
\varepsilon=\varepsilon_{0}/2\wedge c_{2}^{2}/(2r^{2}c_{1}^{2})\wedge 1>0
and define the following recurrent sequence of stopping times starting with
\tau_{0}^{\uparrow}=0
together with

\displaystyle\tau_{k}^{\downarrow}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\inf\{t\geq\tau_{k-1}^{\uparrow}:e_{M}(\mathcal{A}^{i})_{t}\leq\varepsilon\}\/,\quad k=1,2,3,\ldots

\displaystyle\tau_{k}^{\uparrow}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\inf\{t\geq\tau_{k}^{\downarrow}:e_{M}(\mathcal{A}^{i})_{t}\geq 2\varepsilon\}\/,\quad k=1,2,3,\ldots

By definition, we have
0\leq\tau_{1}^{\downarrow}<\tau_{1}^{\uparrow}<\tau_{2}^{\downarrow}<\tau_{2}^{\uparrow}<\ldots
. It is also obvious that there exists a finite number
n=n(\omega)
such that only
\tau_{n}^{\uparrow}<T_{r}\leq\tau_{n+1}^{\downarrow}
. Otherwise, these moments would accumulate over a bounded period of time, which would contradict the continuity of the process. In general, the idea is to notice that in every time interval of the form
[\tau_{k}^{\uparrow},\tau_{k+1}^{\downarrow}]
,
k=0,1,\ldots,n
, the process
e_{M}(\mathcal{A}^{i})
stays above
\varepsilon
(away from zero), and for
[\tau_{k}^{\downarrow},\tau_{k}^{\uparrow}]
with
k=1,\ldots,n
we have
e_{M}(\mathcal{A}^{i})\leq 2\varepsilon
(near zero). As a consequence, we get that

\int_{\tau_{k}^{\uparrow}}^{\tau_{k+1}^{\downarrow}}\sum_{\alpha\in R_{+}^{\,i}}|\alpha|^{2}e_{M-1}^{\,\,\overline{\alpha}}(\mathcal{A}^{i})\frac{k_{\alpha}(s,X(s))}{e_{M}(\mathcal{B}^{i})_{s}}ds\leq\frac{1}{\sqrt{\varepsilon}}\int_{\tau_{k}^{\uparrow}}^{\tau_{k+1}^{\downarrow}}\sum_{\alpha\in R_{+}^{\,i}}|\alpha|^{2}e_{M-1}^{\,\,\overline{\alpha}}(\mathcal{A}^{i})k_{\alpha}(s,X(s))ds<\infty\/,

and it implies that it is enough to show the finiteness of every integral of the form,

(6.15)

\int_{\tau_{k}^{\downarrow}}^{\tau_{k}^{\uparrow}}\sum_{\alpha\in R_{+}^{\,i}}|\alpha|^{2}e_{M-1}^{\,\,\overline{\alpha}}(\mathcal{A}^{i})\frac{k_{\alpha}(s,X(s))}{e_{M}(\mathcal{B}^{i})_{s}}ds\/.

We also recall that the SDE for
e_{M}(\mathcal{A}^{i})
can be rewritten as

de_{M}(\mathcal{A}^{i})=2\sqrt{e_{M}(\mathcal{A}^{i})}\sqrt{a(t)}dW+(V^{(1)}_{M}(X)+e_{M}(\mathcal{B}^{i})V_{M}^{(2)}(X))dt,

where
W
is a Wiener process and

A(t)=\int_{0}^{t}a(s)ds\,\qquad a(t)=\sum_{k=1}^{N}\sigma_{k}^{2}(t,X)\left(\sum_{\alpha\in R_{+}^{\,i}}\alpha_{k}e_{M-1}^{\overline{\alpha}}(\mathcal{B}^{i})\right)^{2}\/.

For
t\in[\tau_{k}^{\downarrow},\tau_{k}^{\uparrow}]
we have by Hölder’s inequality that

\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{N}\sigma^{2}_{k}(t,X)\left(\sum_{\alpha\in R_{+}^{\,i}}\alpha_{k}e_{M-1}^{\overline{\alpha}}(\mathcal{B}^{i})\right)^{2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle M\sum_{\alpha\in R_{+}^{\,i}}e_{M-1}^{\,\,\overline{\alpha}}(\mathcal{A}^{i})\sum_{k=1}^{N}\alpha_{k}^{2}\sigma^{2}_{k}(t,X)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\gamma}\sum_{\alpha\in R_{+}^{\,i}}|\alpha|^{2}e_{M-1}^{\,\,\overline{\alpha}}(\mathcal{A}^{i})k_{\alpha}(t,X)\/.

Moreover, we also have the following

\displaystyle|e_{M}(\mathcal{B}^{i})V_{M}^{(2)}(X)|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\sqrt{2\varepsilon}c_{1}\leq\sqrt{2\varepsilon}c_{1}r\,e_{M-1}(\mathcal{A}^{i})

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle c_{2}\sum_{\alpha\in R_{+}^{\,i}}|\alpha|^{2}e_{M-1}^{\,\,\overline{\alpha}}(\mathcal{A}^{i})\leq\sum_{\alpha\in R_{+}^{\,i}}|\alpha|^{2}e_{M-1}^{\,\,\overline{\alpha}}(\mathcal{A}^{i})k_{\alpha}(t,X)\/.

Combining all together, we arrive at the following estimates of the drift part of
e_{M}(\mathcal{A}^{i})
,

\displaystyle(V^{(1)}_{M}(X)+e_{M}(\mathcal{B}^{i})V_{M}^{(2)}(X))

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle(1+\gamma)\sum_{k=1}^{N}\sigma^{2}_{k}(t,X)\left(\sum_{\alpha\in R_{+}^{\,i}}\alpha_{k}e_{M-1}^{\overline{\alpha}}(\mathcal{B}^{i})\right)^{2}=(1+\gamma)a(t)\/,

which is valid on the near-zero interval
[\tau_{k}^{\downarrow},\tau_{k}^{\uparrow}]
. In particular, by the comparison theorem (see Revuz Yor, Corollary 3.4 p.390 and Theorem 3.7 p.394), it implies that
e_{M}(\mathcal{A}^{i})
is bounded from below by process
Y
, which is a solution to

(6.16)

dY=2\sqrt{Y}\sqrt{a(t)}dW+(1+\gamma)a(t)dt\/,

which is a squared Bessel process with dimension
(1+\gamma)
with time changed by
A(t)
. This is a crucial observation in this proof.

First, we use it to show that for every
t\in[\tau_{k}^{\downarrow},\tau_{k}^{\uparrow}]
such that
e_{M}(\mathcal{A}^{i})_{t}=0
we have

(6.17)

\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{N}\sigma_{k}^{2}(t,X_{t})\sum_{\alpha\in R_{+}^{\,i}}\alpha_{k}^{2}e_{M-1}^{\,\,\overline{\alpha}}(\mathcal{A}^{i})>0\/,\quad a.s.

Indeed, if with positive probability the process
X
hits boundary (
e_{M}(\mathcal{A}^{i})_{t}=0
) in a point where
\sum_{k=1}^{N}\sigma_{k}^{2}(t,X_{t})\sum_{\alpha\in R_{+}^{\,i}}\alpha_{k}^{2}e_{M-1}^{\,\,\overline{\alpha}}(\mathcal{A}^{i})=0
, then using continuity of the coefficients and the fact that
\sum_{\alpha\in R_{+}^{\,i}}|\alpha|^{2}e_{M-1}^{\,\,\overline{\alpha}}(\mathcal{A}^{i})k_{\alpha}(t,X)
is strictly positive (due to positivity of
k_{\alpha}
and the result of Proposition
6
) we can find (with positive probability) the small random time sub-interval of
[\tau_{k}^{\downarrow},\tau_{k}^{\uparrow}]
, where we can make
\gamma
in (
6.14
) as big as we want. In particular, we see that there exists a time interval, where the process
e_{M}(\mathcal{A}^{i})
is bounded from below by the squared Bessel process of dimension
2
with time change, but this process never visits
0
, which gives a contradiction.

Next, we define the random set

Z_{0}=\{t\in[\tau_{k}^{\downarrow},\tau_{k}^{\uparrow}]:e_{M}(\mathcal{A}^{i})_{t}=0\}

It is definitely compact set as it is closed due to continuity of
t\to e_{M}(\mathcal{A}^{i})_{t}
and bounded (finiteness of
\tau_{k}^{\downarrow}
and
\tau_{k}^{\uparrow}
). As we have previously seen, for each
t\in Z_{0}
we have (
6.17
). It means that for every
t^{*}\in Z_{0}
there exists
\delta=\delta(t,\omega)>0
such that for every
t\in(t^{*}-\delta,t^{*}+\delta)
the inequality (
6.17
) still holds and consequently there exists
L^{*}=L^{*}(t^{*},\delta,\omega)>0
such that

(6.18)

\sum_{\alpha\in R_{+}^{\,i}}|\alpha|^{2}e_{M-1}^{\,\,\overline{\alpha}}(\mathcal{A}^{i})k_{\alpha}(t,X)\leq L^{*}\sum_{k=1}^{N}\sigma_{k}^{2}(t,X_{t})\sum_{\alpha\in R_{+}^{\,i}}\alpha_{k}^{2}e_{M-1}^{\,\,\overline{\alpha}}(\mathcal{A}^{i})

for every
t\in(t^{*}-\delta,t^{*}+\delta)
. It provides an open (random) cover of the set
Z_{0}
and compactness of
Z_{0}
implies that we can find finite sub-cover. Then taking
\mathcal{U}
as a sum of all elements from this finite sub-cover we end up with an open set such that
Z_{0}\subset\mathcal{U}
and there exists
L>0
(which is obviously a maximum of all
L^{*}
found for each element of the finite sub-cover) such that (
6.18
) holds with
L^{*}
replaced by
L
for every
t\in\mathcal{U}
.

Writing
1=\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{U}}(s)+\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{U}^{c}}(s)
, we split the near-zero integral
(
6.15
) on the interval

[\tau_{k}^{\downarrow},\tau_{k}^{\uparrow}]
. Namely,

\displaystyle I_{1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{\tau_{k}^{\downarrow}}^{\tau_{k}^{\uparrow}}\sum_{\alpha\in R_{+}^{\,i}}|\alpha|^{2}e_{M-1}^{\,\,\overline{\alpha}}(\mathcal{A}^{i})\frac{k_{\alpha}(s,X_{s})}{e_{M}(\mathcal{B}^{i})_{s}}\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{U}}(s)\,ds

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle L\int_{\tau_{k}^{\downarrow}}^{\tau_{k}^{\uparrow}}\sum_{j=1}^{N}\sigma_{j}^{2}(s,X_{s})\sum_{\alpha\in R_{+}^{\,i}}\alpha_{j}^{2}e_{M-1}^{\,\,\overline{\alpha}}(\mathcal{A}^{i})\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{U}}(s)\frac{ds}{e_{M}(\mathcal{B}^{i})_{s}}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle L\int_{\tau_{k}^{\downarrow}}^{\tau_{k}^{\uparrow}}\sum_{j=1}^{N}\sigma_{j}^{2}(s,X_{s})\sum_{\alpha\in R_{+}^{\,i}}\alpha_{j}^{2}e_{M-1}^{\,\,\overline{\alpha}}(\mathcal{A}^{i})\frac{ds}{e_{M}(\mathcal{B}^{i})_{s}}.

Moreover,

\displaystyle I_{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{\tau_{k}^{\downarrow}}^{\tau_{k}^{\uparrow}}\sum_{\alpha\in R_{+}^{\,i}}|\alpha|^{2}e_{M-1}^{\,\,\overline{\alpha}}(\mathcal{A}^{i})\frac{k_{\alpha}(s,X_{s})}{e_{M}(\mathcal{B}^{i})_{s}}\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{U}^{c}}(s)\,ds.

Note that
\mathcal{U}^{c}\cap[\tau_{k}^{\downarrow},\tau_{k}^{\uparrow}]
is again a compact set on which the continuous function
t\to e_{M}(\mathcal{B}^{i})_{t}
is positive, which means that there exists
c_{4}=c_{4}(\omega)>0
such that
e_{M}(\mathcal{B}^{i})_{t}>c_{4}
on
\mathcal{U}^{c}\cap[\tau_{k}^{\downarrow},\tau_{k}^{\uparrow}]
and consequently
I_{2}
is just finite. Consequently, we can focus our attention on showing the finiteness of

(6.19)

\int_{\tau_{k}^{\downarrow}}^{\tau_{k}^{\uparrow}}{\sum_{j=1}^{N}\sigma_{j}^{2}(s,X)\left(\sum_{\alpha\in R_{+}^{\,i}}\alpha_{j}e_{M-1}^{\overline{\alpha}}(\mathcal{B}^{i})\right)^{2}}\frac{ds}{e_{M}(\mathcal{B}^{i})_{s}}=\int_{\tau_{k}^{\downarrow}}^{\tau_{k}^{\uparrow}}\frac{A^{\prime}(s)ds}{e_{M}(\mathcal{B}^{i})_{s}}\/,

as again we have

\displaystyle\left(\sum_{\alpha\in R_{+}^{\,i}}\alpha_{j}e_{M-1}^{\overline{\alpha}}(\mathcal{B}^{i})\right)^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{\alpha\in R_{+}^{\,i}}\alpha_{j}^{2}e_{M-1}^{\,\,\overline{\alpha}}(\mathcal{A}^{i})+\mathop{\sum_{\alpha\in R_{+}^{\,i}}\sum_{\beta\in R_{+}^{\,i}}}_{\alpha\neq\beta}\alpha_{j}\beta_{j}e_{M}(\mathcal{B}^{i})e_{M-2}^{\overline{\alpha},\overline{\beta}}(\mathcal{B}^{i})

and we see as before a similar cancellation of the singularity for the second double sum.

Finally, we return to the fact that
e_{M}(\mathcal{B}^{i})_{s}\geq\sqrt{Y(s)}
for
s\in[{\tau_{k}^{\downarrow}},{\tau_{k}^{\uparrow}}]
, where
Y
is a squared Bessel process with dimension
1+\gamma>1
, starting from
0
, with time-changed by
A(t)
, i.e. we have
Y(s)=Z\bigl(A(s)-A(\tau_{k}^{\downarrow})\bigr)
, where
Z
is
\textrm{BESQ}^{(1+\gamma)}_{0}
. Using a similar approach as before, one can show that in fact
t\to A(t)
is strictly increasing in the neighborhood of the boundary as we have (
6.17
). However, we can also provide the final claim noticing that since
H=\{s\in[{\tau_{k}^{\downarrow}},{\tau_{k}^{\uparrow}}]:A^{\prime}(s)=a(s)>0\}
is relatively open in
[\tau_{k}^{\downarrow},\tau_{k}^{\uparrow}]
, it can be obtained as countable union of disjoined intervals, i.e.
H=\bigcup_{i=1}^{\infty}(l_{i},r_{i})
and use this random set representation in the following

\displaystyle\int_{\tau_{k}^{\downarrow}}^{\tau_{k}^{\uparrow}}\frac{a(s)ds}{e_{M}(\mathcal{B}^{i})_{s}}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\int_{\tau_{k}^{\downarrow}}^{\tau_{k}^{\uparrow}}\frac{a(s)ds}{\sqrt{Y(s)}}=\int_{\tau_{k}^{\downarrow}}^{\tau_{k}^{\uparrow}}\frac{a(s)}{\sqrt{Y(s)}}\mathbf{1}_{\{a(s)>0\}}ds=\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\int_{l_{i}}^{r_{i}}\frac{a(s)ds}{\sqrt{Z(A(s)-A(\tau_{k}^{\downarrow}))}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\int_{A(l_{i})-A(\tau_{k}^{\downarrow})}^{A(r_{i})-A(\tau_{k}^{\downarrow})}\frac{du}{\sqrt{Z(u)}}\leq\int_{0}^{A(\tau_{k}^{\uparrow})-A(\tau_{k}^{\downarrow})}\frac{du}{\sqrt{Z(u)}}<\infty.

The last integral is finite because
\sqrt{Z}
is a Bessel process of dimension
1+\gamma>1
, and therefore its reciprocal is locally integrable in time.

∎

Remark 6
.

Note that the proof given above shows in particular that whenever
(A1)
holds, then for every
\alpha\in R_{+}
we have for every
t<\zeta^{X}
that

(6.20)

\int_{0}^{t}\dfrac{\sum_{k=1}^{N}\alpha_{k}^{2}\sigma_{k}^{2}(s,X(s))}{\left<X(s),\alpha\right>}ds<\infty\/,\quad a.s.

This fact will be important in the final stage of the solution construction.

7.
Construction of a solution in non-degenerate case

All the propositions provided in the previous sections lead to this point, where we are finally able to prove that our candidate process
X=(X_{1},\ldots,X_{N})
is a semi-martingale, which solve (
1.2
).

Proposition 9
.

Assume that
X=(X_{1},\ldots,X_{N})
is a diffusion process defined in (
3
) such that
X(0)\in\overline{C_{+}}
. If
(A1)
holds, then
X
is a semi-martingale solving (
1.2
).

Proof.

First, we assume that the starting point
X(0)
is in the interior
C_{+}
and focus our attention to the case
A_{N-1}
. Then we define the following sequence of random times starting from
T_{0}=0
. Then for
n\geq 0
, if
T_{n}<\zeta^{X}
, we define

\displaystyle T_{n+1}

\displaystyle=\inf\{t\geq T_{n}:\textrm{there exists two different }\alpha,\beta\in\mathcal{H}_{n}(t)\textrm{ such that }\left<\alpha,\beta\right>\neq 0\}\wedge\zeta^{X}

with the convention that the infimum of the empty set is
\zeta^{X}
. Here

\mathcal{H}_{n}(t)=\{\alpha\in R_{+}:\textrm{there exists }u\in[T_{n},t]\textrm{ such that }\left<X(u),\alpha\right>=0\}.

In other words, after time
T_{n}
we collect all new roots
\alpha
for which
\left<X(u),\alpha\right>
vanishes for some
u\geq T_{n}
and we stop this procedure whenever the constructed set
\mathcal{H}_{n}(t)
contains two non-orthogonal roots. Continuity of
X
and the lack of multiple collisions implies that

T_{0}<T_{1}<\ldots<T_{n}<\ldots\/.

By Proposition
6
, we have
T_{n+1}>T_{n}
on
\{T_{n}<\zeta^{X}\}
. Indeed, if
T_{n+1}=T_{n}
, then two non-orthogonal roots would vanish at the same time
T_{n}
, which is excluded by absence of multiple collisions.

Moreover, the sequence
(T_{n})_{n}
has no accumulation point before
\zeta^{X}
. Suppose, to the contrary, that
T_{n}\uparrow T<\zeta
. By the definition of
T_{n+1}
, for each
n
there exist non-orthogonal roots
\alpha_{n},\beta_{n}\in R_{+}
and times
s_{n},u_{n}\in[T_{n},T_{n+1}]
such that

\langle X(s_{n}),\alpha_{n}\rangle=0,\qquad\langle X(u_{n}),\beta_{n}\rangle=0,\qquad\langle\alpha_{n},\beta_{n}\rangle\neq 0.

Since
R_{+}
is finite, passing to a subsequence if necessary, we may assume that
\alpha_{n}=\alpha
and
\beta_{n}=\beta
for all
n
, with
\langle\alpha,\beta\rangle\neq 0
. Since
s_{n},u_{n}\to T
, continuity of
X
gives
