\langle X(T),\alpha\rangle=\langle X(T),\beta\rangle=0,

which contradicts Proposition
6
. Hence
(T_{n})_{n}
is locally finite on
[0,\zeta^{X})
, and therefore
T_{n}\uparrow\zeta^{X}
.

Let us now fix
n=0,1,2,\ldots
and
t<T_{n+1}
. By construction,
\mathcal{H}_{n}(t)
is a pairwise orthogonal subset of
R_{+}
, and every root which vanishes somewhere on
[T_{n},t]
belongs to
\mathcal{H}_{n}(t)
. In the type
A_{N-1}
case, the roots in
\mathcal{H}_{n}(t)
correspond to disjoint adjacent collision pairs. If
i
is such that for every
\alpha\in\mathcal{H}_{n}(t)

\alpha_{i}=0
, then
X_{i}
does not hit any of its neighbors on the interval
[T_{n},t]
. Therefore, using Proposition
23
(
i
) and Itô’s formula, we obtain

X_{i}(s)=X_{i}(T_{n})+\int_{T_{n}}^{s}\sigma_{i}(s,X)dB_{u}+\int_{T_{n}}^{s}b_{i}(s,X)du+\int_{T_{n}}^{s}\sum_{\alpha\in R_{+}}\dfrac{k_{\alpha}(s,X)\alpha_{i}}{\left<X,\alpha\right>}du\/,

for every
s\in[T_{n},t]
. To deal with the remaining case, i.e. there exists
\alpha\in\mathcal{H}_{n}(t)
such that
\alpha_{i}\neq 0
, then on the interval
[T_{n},t]
, the particles
X_{j}
and
X_{j+1}
can collide only with each other, where
i\in\{j,j+1\}
. Thus, by Proposition
23
(
ii
), the function
(x_{j}+x_{j+1})(p_{1},\ldots,p_{N})
is smooth on this localized branch, and Itô’s formula gives

(7.1)

\displaystyle X_{j}(s)+X_{j+1}(s)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle X_{j}(T_{n})+X_{j+1}(T_{n})+\int_{T_{n}}^{t}\sigma_{j}(s,X)dB_{j}+\int_{T_{n}}^{t}\sigma_{j+1}(s,X)dB_{j+1}

\displaystyle+\int_{T_{n}}^{t}(b_{j}(s,X)+b_{j+1}(s,X))ds

\displaystyle+\int_{T_{n}}^{t}\sum_{\alpha\in R_{+}}\dfrac{k_{\alpha}(s,X)\alpha_{j}}{\left<X,\alpha\right>}ds+\int_{T_{n}}^{t}\sum_{\alpha\in R_{+}}\dfrac{k_{\alpha}(s,X)\alpha_{j+1}}{\left<X,\alpha\right>}ds\/.

To go from SDE for the sum
X_{j}+X_{j+1}
to the SDEs for
X_{j}
and
X_{j+1}
separately we will explore the difference
X_{j}-X_{j+1}
. Here we can not claim the the mapping from the polynomials to the difference is smooth as we definitely might see collisions between
X_{j}
and
X_{j+1}
. To deal with this we use again the smoothing function, which we have used before in Proposition
7
. Recall that we defined
\psi_{\varepsilon}:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
for
\varepsilon>0
by
\psi_{\varepsilon}(z)=z\varphi(z/\varepsilon)
, where
\varphi
is a fix smooth function on
\mathbb{R}
with values in
[0,1]
such that
\varphi(z)=0
for
|z|<1
and
\varphi(z)=1
for
|z|\geq 2
. Proposition
23
shows that
Z_{j}=\psi_{\varepsilon}(X_{j}-X_{j+1})
is a semimartingle and the Itô formula implies the following representation on
[T_{n},T_{n+1})

\displaystyle d\psi_{\varepsilon}(X_{j}-X_{j+1})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\psi^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}(X_{j}-X_{j+1})(dX_{j}-dX_{j+1})

\displaystyle+\dfrac{1}{2}\psi^{\prime\prime}_{\varepsilon}(X_{j}-X_{j+1})(dX_{j}dX_{j}+dX_{j+1}dX_{j+1})\/.

for every
\varepsilon>0
. To deal with the limit as
\varepsilon
goes to
0
, let us focus on the last part, which is given by

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\int_{T_{n}}^{t}(X_{j}-X_{j+1})\psi^{\prime\prime}_{\varepsilon}(X_{j}-X_{j+1})\dfrac{\sigma^{2}_{j}(s,X(s))+\sigma^{2}_{j+1}(s,X(s))}{X_{j}-X_{j+1}}ds\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\varepsilon\to 0}}{{\longrightarrow}}0

by (
6.6
) and the Dominated Convergence Theorem (by (
6.7
) and integrability given in (
6.20
)). Similarly, we proceed with the drift part in
\psi^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}(X_{j}-X_{j+1})(dX_{j}-dX_{j+1})
, where we use the bounds for the first derivative of
\psi_{\varepsilon}
given in (
6.7
) and the integrability of the drift terms given in (
6.11
). Additionally, the integrability given in (
6.11
) implies that the set
\{t:X_{j}(t)=X_{j+1}(t)\}
has Lebesgue measure zero a.s. Consequently,
\psi^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}(X_{j}(t)-X_{j+1}(t))\to 1
as
\varepsilon\to 0
. Finally, the local-martingale part, after localization, can be treated in the same way using the
L_{2}
property of the Ito integral. It allows us to arrive at

(7.2)

\displaystyle X_{j}(t)-X_{j+1}(t)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle X_{j}(T_{n})-X_{j+1}(T_{n})+\int_{T_{n}}^{t}\left(\sigma_{j}(s,X(s))dB_{j}-\sigma_{j+1}(s,X(s))dB_{j+1}\right)

\displaystyle+\int_{T_{n}}^{t}\left(b_{j}(s,X(s))-b_{j+1}(s,X(s))\right)ds

\displaystyle+\int_{T_{n}}^{t}\sum_{\alpha\in R_{+}}\dfrac{k_{\alpha}(s,X)\alpha_{j}}{\left<X,\alpha\right>}ds-\int_{T_{n}}^{t}\sum_{\alpha\in R_{+}}\dfrac{k_{\alpha}(s,X)\alpha_{j+1}}{\left<X,\alpha\right>}ds

Obviously, using (
7.1
) and (
7.2
) we obtain the desired SDEs for
X_{j}
and
X_{j+1}
. Gluing these local representations gives the desired equation in type
A_{N-1}
for an initial point in the interior
C_{+}
.

Let us now briefly describe the changes in the above-given proof, which are necessary to deal with the remaining cases
B_{N}
and
D_{N}
. The argument is local and uses the same approximation procedure as above. For
x\in\overline{C_{+}}
put

\mathcal{A}(x):=\{\alpha\in R_{+}:\langle x,\alpha\rangle=0\}.

Fix a positive time and localize to a sufficiently small random time interval on which no root outside the active set can vanish. By Proposition
6
, all roots in the active set are pairwise orthogonal. Hence, if
\beta
is an active root and
\gamma
is another active root, then the singular term generated by
\gamma
does not contribute to the equation for
\langle X,\beta\rangle
, because
\langle\beta,\gamma\rangle=0
. Therefore the active walls can be treated one by one. All quantities which are smooth functions of the invariant coordinates are handled by Itô’s formula exactly as in the type
A_{N-1}
case. Only the corresponding root distances
Z_{\alpha}(t):=\langle X(t),\alpha\rangle
,
\alpha\in R_{+}
require the smoothing argument with
\psi_{\varepsilon}
.

In type
B_{N}
, the positive roots are
e_{i}
,
e_{i}-e_{j}
and
e_{i}+e_{j}
. First observe that
e_{i}+e_{j}
does not produce any additional problems as
x_{i}+x_{j}=0
forces
x_{i}=x_{j}=0
and hence a non-orthogonal multiple collision, which is excluded by Proposition
6
. Moreover, the roots
\alpha=e_{i}-e_{j}
can be treated exactly the same way as in the
A_{N-1}
case with the smoothing functions
\psi_{\varepsilon}
, the Itô formula application to
\psi_{\varepsilon}(Z_{\alpha})
and passing to the limit
\varepsilon\downarrow 0
. Finally, the only remaining case to deal with is the last coordinate, namely for the root
\alpha=e_{N}
and the wall
X_{N}=0
. The square
Y_{N}:=X_{N}^{2}

is locally a smooth function of the invariant coordinates. On the interior, Itô’s formula gives

\displaystyle dY_{N}(t)

\displaystyle=2X_{N}(t)\sigma_{N}(t,X(t))\,dB_{N}(t)

\displaystyle\quad+\left[\sigma_{N}^{2}(t,X(t))+2X_{N}(t)b_{N}(t,X(t))+2X_{N}(t)\sum_{\delta\in R_{+}}\frac{k_{\delta}(t,X(t))\delta_{N}}{\langle X(t),\delta\rangle}\right]dt.

We then apply Itô’s formula to
h_{\varepsilon}(Y_{N}):=\sqrt{Y_{N}+\varepsilon}
. The martingale part converges in quadratic variation to

\int_{a}^{t}\sigma_{N}(s,X(s))\,dB_{N}(s)
, while the drift terms converge to the desired drift by dominated convergence, using (
6.20
) and (
6.11
). The only additional Itô correction is

\frac{\varepsilon\,\sigma_{N}^{2}(t,X(t))}{2\left(X_{N}^{2}(t)+\varepsilon\right)^{3/2}}\,dt,

which converges to zero by dominated convergence, using again (
6.20
), which reads here as

\int_{0}^{t}\frac{\sigma_{N}^{2}(s,X(s))}{X_{N}(s)}\,ds<\infty.

Finally, in the case
D_{N}
there are no short roots. The walls corresponding to roots
e_{i}-e_{j}
and
e_{i}+e_{j}
are treated by the same smoothing argument. More precisely, for a root
\alpha=e_{i}-e_{j}
we apply the argument to
Z_{\alpha}=X_{i}-X_{j}
,
while
X_{i}+X_{j}
stays locally separated from zero and is therefore a smooth function of the invariant coordinates on the localized branch. Similarly, for a root
\alpha=e_{i}+e_{j}
we apply the argument to
Z_{\alpha}=X_{i}+X_{j}
, while
X_{i}-X_{j}
stays locally separated from zero. There is no remaining zero-pair case to consider. Indeed, if both
e_{i}-e_{j}
and
e_{i}+e_{j}
vanished at a positive time, then
X_{i}=X_{j}=0
, and the two roots would form a multiple cluster. This is excluded by Proposition
6
. Hence, after localization, each active type
D_{N}
wall is treated as a single root wall, exactly as in the preceding smoothing argument.

Since the preceding arguments are local, the resulting semimartingale representations are glued over the same type of localized intervals as in the type
A_{N-1}
proof. Thus, for an initial point in the interior
C_{+}
, the process
X
satisfies (
1.2
) up to its lifetime in all three classical cases
A_{N-1}
,
B_{N}
, and
D_{N}
.

Last part of the proof relates to the general starting point
X(0)\in\overline{C_{+}}
, which, in particular, might be on the boundary of the Weyl chamber and has multiple collisions. Then, by Proposition
5
, there exists a sequence of times
t_{n}\downarrow 0
such that
X(t_{n})\in C_{+}
. For every fixed
n
, applying the preceding interior-start argument to the shifted process gives the expected semi-martingale representation on
[t_{n},t]
. It remains only to justify that the singular integrals remain finite when
t_{n}\downarrow 0
. This follows by the standard one-sided argument used for ordered particle systems. With our convention for the Weyl chamber,
X_{1}
is the largest particle. After putting the singular part on one side and all non-singular terms on the other side, the right-hand side has a finite limit as
t_{n}\downarrow 0
by continuity of
X
, continuity of the stochastic integral, and local integrability of the regular drift. Since the singular terms in the
X_{1}
-equation are non-negative, no cancellation is possible, and each of these integrals is finite on
[0,t]
. We then proceed inductively. Suppose that all singular integrals involving particles with indices smaller than
i
have already been shown to be finite. In the equation for
X_{i}
, we move those already controlled terms to the non-singular side. The remaining singular terms, namely those involving
X_{i}
and particles with larger indices, and in type
B_{N}
also the term corresponding to the wall
X_{i}=0
, again have the same sign. The same one-sided argument therefore proves their finiteness. Repeating this for
i=1,\ldots,N
gives the finiteness of all singular integrals on
[0,t]
. Consequently, taking the limit
t_{n}\downarrow 0
in the shifted semi-martingale representations yields the semi-martingale representation given in the main SDE (
1.2
).
∎

Collecting all together we have completed the proof of Theorem
1
in the following way.

Proof of Theorem
1
.

By Proposition
4
, there exists a
\mathcal{K}
-valued solution
U
of the invariant-coordinate equation started from
w(x_{0})
. Put
X=f(U)
. Proposition
9
shows that
X
is a semimartingale satisfying (
1.2
). For
x_{0}\in C_{+}
, the finiteness of the singular integrals follows from Proposition
8
. For
x_{0}\in\overline{C_{+}}\setminus C_{+}
, it follows from the one-sided limiting argument at the end of Proposition
9
. This proves the theorem.
∎

8.
Construction of a solution in the degenerate case

In this section we prove the existence result in the degenerate boundary regime stated
in Theorem
3
. In contrast to the strictly positive
case, the coefficient
k_{\alpha}
is now allowed to vanish on the wall

\{\langle x,\alpha\rangle=0\}
. The construction is based on approximation by
systems with strictly positive boundary repulsion. Namely, for
n\in\mathbb{N}
we replace
k_{\alpha}
by

(8.1)

k_{\alpha}^{(n)}(t,x):=k_{\alpha}(t,x)+\frac{1}{n},\qquad\alpha\in R_{+}.

For each fixed
n
, the coefficients
k_{\alpha}^{(n)}
satisfy the strict positivity
condition
(A1)
. Hence the corresponding
non-degenerate existence theorem yields a chamber-valued solution
X^{(n)}
.

The main difficulty is to identify the limit of the singular drift terms. Along a
weakly convergent subsequence, the finite-variation terms

\int_{0}^{t}\frac{k_{\alpha}^{(n)}(s,X^{(n)}(s))}{\langle X^{(n)}(s),\alpha\rangle}\,ds

may converge to a measure which, a priori, contains an additional component supported on the set of times
\{t:\langle X(t),\alpha\rangle=0\}
. The purpose of the argument below is to show that, under Assumptions
(D1)
and
(D2)
, such hidden boundary measures are in fact absent.

We first recall the two degenerate assumptions introduced in Section
2
. They replace the strict positivity condition
(A1)
. The first one is the normal dominance condition
(D1)
. For
\alpha\in R_{+}
, set

a_{\alpha}(t,x):=\sum_{j=1}^{N}\alpha_{j}^{2}\,\sigma_{j}^{2}(t,x).

This is the quadratic variation density of the martingale part of
\langle X,\alpha\rangle
. Assumption
(D1)
says that, locally in time and space, for points close to the wall
\{\langle x,\alpha\rangle=0\}
, the repulsion coefficient
k_{\alpha}(t,x)
dominates the normal martingale variation
a_{\alpha}(t,x)
. Thus
k_{\alpha}
may vanish at the wall, but only in a way compatible with the degeneration of the diffusion in the same normal direction. More precisely, for every
T,R>0
there exist constants
\varepsilon=\varepsilon(T,R)>0
and
\gamma=\gamma(T,R)>0
such that, for every
t\in[0,T]
, every
x\in\overline{C_{+}}
with
|x|\leq R
, and every
\alpha\in R_{+}
,

0\leq\langle x,\alpha\rangle\leq\varepsilon\quad\Longrightarrow\quad k_{\alpha}(t,x)\geq\gamma\,a_{\alpha}(t,x).

Since
R_{+}
is finite, the constants may be chosen uniformly over all positive roots
on each compact time-space set. This condition is weaker than strict positivity of

k_{\alpha}
on the wall. Indeed, if
k_{\alpha}
is strictly positive on

\{0\leq t\leq T,\ |x|\leq R,\ \langle x,\alpha\rangle=0\},

then, by continuity and compactness,
k_{\alpha}
remains bounded from below in a
neighborhood of the wall, whereas
a_{\alpha}
is bounded above on the same compact set.
Hence Assumption
(D1)
follows after possibly shrinking the neighborhood.

The second assumption is the face sign condition
(D2)
. Recall the notation introduced in Section
2
in this context. In particular, by our convention, the detector family
\mathfrak{D}(S)

contains the canonical detector

u_{S}:=\sum_{\beta\in S}\beta

and, for
u\in\mathfrak{D}(S)
, the regular drift of the detector
\tau_{u}
is

B_{S,u}(t,x)=\langle u,b(t,x)\rangle+\sum_{\beta\in R_{+}\setminus S}\frac{k_{\beta}(t,x)\langle u,\beta\rangle}{\langle x,\beta\rangle}.

This is the same quantity as in (
2.6
). On localized neighborhoods of
F_{S}
, all denominators with
\beta\in R_{+}\setminus S
are bounded away from zero. Consequently,
B_{S,u}
admits a continuous trace on
F_{S}
. Indeed, while the process is localized near
F_{S}
, applying the linear functional
\tau_{u}
to the SDE formally gives

\displaystyle d\tau_{u}(X(t))

\displaystyle=dM^{u}(t)+B_{S,u}(t,X(t))\,dt+\sum_{\alpha\in S}\frac{k_{\alpha}(t,X(t))\langle u,\alpha\rangle}{\langle X(t),\alpha\rangle}\,dt,

where

M^{u}(t):=\sum_{j=1}^{N}u_{j}\int_{0}^{t}\sigma_{j}(s,X(s))\,dB_{j}(s).

Thus
B_{S,u}
is precisely the regular part of the detector drift, after the singular terms corresponding to the roots vanishing on the face have been separated. Assumption
(D2)
requires that, for every collision face
F_{S}
and every admissible detector
u\in\mathfrak{D}(S)
, the continuous trace of
B_{S,u}
on
F_{S}
satisfies

B_{S,u}(t,x)\geq 0,\qquad t\geq 0\/,x\in F_{S}.

This is an inward-pointing condition for the regular part of the detector drift. Since
\tau_{u}
is nonnegative on the chamber and vanishes on
F_{S}
, the regular drift should not push the detector into negative values at the boundary. The condition is not needed for tightness or for the construction of a generalized weak limit. It is used only in the final local-time argument, where the possible hidden boundary measures are eliminated.

8.1.
Approximation and localized compactness estimates

We continue with the approximating solutions
X^{(n)}
associated with (
8.1
). Thus
X^{(n)}
is a
\overline{C_{+}}
-valued weak solution, started from
x_{0}
, to (
1.2
) with
k_{\alpha}
replaced by
k_{\alpha}^{(n)}
. We denote its lifetime by
\zeta^{(n)}
. At this compactness stage only Assumptions
(C1)
-
(C2)
are used. The degenerate boundary assumptions
(D1)
and
(D2)
enter only later, after a generalized weak limit has been constructed, in the argument excluding boundary measures.

For
\alpha\in R_{+}
and
0\leq t<\zeta^{(n)}
, define the accumulated

\alpha
-singular drift by

(8.2)

A^{(n),\alpha}(t):=\int_{0}^{t}\frac{k_{\alpha}^{(n)}(s,X^{(n)}(s))}{\langle X^{(n)}(s),\alpha\rangle}\,ds.

Since the approximating coefficients
k_{\alpha}^{(n)}
satisfy
Assumption
(A1)
, Theorem
1
ensures that these
increasing processes are well defined and finite on compact subintervals of

[0,\zeta^{(n)})
.

The next proposition collects the localized estimates needed for the limiting
argument: tightness of the stopped paths, uniform control of the singular drift
masses, and compactness of the associated stopped drift measures.

Proposition 10

(Localized compactness estimates)
.

Assume
(C1)
-
(C2)
. Let
X^{(n)}
be the approximating solutions associated with (
8.1
). For every
T,r>0
, define

(8.3)

\chi_{r}^{(n)}:=T\wedge\inf\{t\in[0,\zeta^{(n)}):\ |X^{(n)}(t)|\geq r\},

with the convention
\inf\varnothing=\infty
. Then the following hold.

(i)

The laws of the stopped processes

\bigl(X^{(n)}(t\wedge\chi_{r}^{(n)})\bigr)_{0\leq t\leq T},\qquad n\in\mathbb{N},

form a tight family in
\mathcal{C}([0,T];\overline{C_{+}})
.

(ii)

There exists a constant
C_{r,T}<\infty
, independent of
n
, such
that

\sup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\mathbb{E}\sum_{\alpha\in R_{+}}A^{(n),\alpha}(\chi_{r}^{(n)})\leq C_{r,T}.

In particular, for every
\alpha\in R_{+}
there exists
C_{\alpha,r,T}<\infty
, independent of
n
, such that

\sup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\mathbb{E}\int_{0}^{\chi_{r}^{(n)}}\frac{k_{\alpha}^{(n)}(s,X^{(n)}(s))}{\langle X^{(n)}(s),\alpha\rangle}\,ds\leq C_{\alpha,r,T}.

(iii)

For every
\alpha\in R_{+}
, define the stopped drift measure on
[0,T]
by

(8.4)

\mu_{r,T}^{(n),\alpha}(dt):=\mathbf{1}_{\{t\leq\chi_{r}^{(n)}\}}\frac{k_{\alpha}^{(n)}(t,X^{(n)}(t))}{\langle X^{(n)}(t),\alpha\rangle}\,dt.

Then the joint laws of

\left(X^{(n)}(\cdot\wedge\chi_{r}^{(n)}),\bigl(\mu_{r,T}^{(n),\alpha}\bigr)_{\alpha\in R_{+}}\right),\qquad n\in\mathbb{N},

are tight in

\mathcal{C}([0,T];\overline{C_{+}})\times\prod_{\alpha\in R_{+}}\mathcal{M}_{+}([0,T]),

where
\mathcal{M}_{+}([0,T])
is equipped with the weak topology.

Moreover, for every
n\in\mathbb{N}
,

\chi_{r}^{(n)}\uparrow T\wedge\zeta^{(n)}\qquad\text{as }r\to\infty.

Proof.

Let
w=(w_{1},\ldots,w_{N})
be the basic invariant-coordinate map introduced in Subsection
4.2
. For
t<\zeta^{(n)}
, set

U^{(n)}(t):=w(X^{(n)}(t)).

Then
U^{(n)}
solves the invariant-coordinate equations from Definition
2
, with
k_{\alpha}
replaced by
k_{\alpha}^{(n)}
. We first prove the path tightness in
\mathcal{C}([0,T];\overline{C_{+}})
. Set

Y^{(n)}(t):=U^{(n)}(t\wedge\chi_{r}^{(n)}),\qquad 0\leq t\leq T.

Let us denote by
K_{r_{*}}
the closed ball with center at
0
and radius
r^{*}=r\vee|x_{0}|
. For
0\leq t\leq\chi_{r}^{(n)}
, the process
X^{(n)}(t)
stays in
K_{r_{*}}
. Since the invariant-coordinate equations are non-singular, their coefficients are continuous functions of
(t,X^{(n)}(t))
. Moreover,

0\leq k_{\alpha}^{(n)}(t,x)=k_{\alpha}(t,x)+\frac{1}{n}\leq k_{\alpha}(t,x)+1,\qquad n\geq 1.

Thus, on
[0,T]\times K_{r_{*}}
, the stopped invariant-coordinate coefficients are bounded uniformly in
n
. Consequently, for each
k=1,\ldots,N
, the stopped process
Y_{k}^{(n)}
admits a decomposition

\displaystyle Y_{k}^{(n)}(t)

\displaystyle=Y_{k}^{(n)}(0)+\sum_{i=1}^{N}\int_{0}^{t}a_{i,k}^{(n)}(s)\,dB_{i}^{(n)}(s)+\int_{0}^{t}c_{k}^{(n)}(s)\,ds,

where the progressively measurable stopped coefficients satisfy

|a_{i,k}^{(n)}(s)|+|c_{k}^{(n)}(s)|\leq C_{r,T},\qquad 0\leq s\leq T,

with a constant independent of
n
. In what follows,
C_{r,T}
may change from line to line, as long as it depends only on
r
and
T
.

Let
0\leq s\leq t\leq T
. By the Burkholder-Davis-Gundy inequality,

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}\left|\sum_{i=1}^{N}\int_{s}^{t}\bar{a}_{i,k}^{(n)}(u)\,dB_{i}^{(n)}(u)\right|^{4}

\displaystyle\leq C\,\mathbf{E}\left(\int_{s}^{t}\sum_{i=1}^{N}|\bar{a}_{i,k}^{(n)}(u)|^{2}\,du\right)^{2}\leq C_{r,T}|t-s|^{2}.

For the drift part,

\mathbf{E}\left|\int_{s}^{t}\bar{c}_{k}^{(n)}(u)\,du\right|^{4}\leq C_{r,T}|t-s|^{4}.

Hence

\mathbf{E}|Y_{k}^{(n)}(t)-Y_{k}^{(n)}(s)|^{4}\leq C_{r,T}|t-s|^{2}.

Summing over
k=1,\ldots,N
, and increasing the constant if necessary, we get

\mathbf{E}|Y^{(n)}(t)-Y^{(n)}(s)|^{4}\leq C_{r,T}|t-s|^{2},\qquad 0\leq s\leq t\leq T,

with
C_{r,T}
independent of
n
. Since the initial values
Y^{(n)}(0)=w(x_{0})
are deterministic, Kolmogorov’s tightness criterion implies that the laws of
Y^{(n)}
are tight in
\mathcal{C}([0,T];\mathbb{R}^{N})
. Finally,
X^{(n)}=\tilde{f}(U^{(n)})
, where
\tilde{f}:\mathbb{R}^{N}\to\overline{C_{+}}
is the continuous extension introduced together with the invariant-coordinate map. Therefore

X^{(n)}(t\wedge\chi_{r}^{(n)})=\tilde{f}\bigl(Y^{(n)}(t)\bigr),\qquad 0\leq t\leq T.

The map
\mathcal{C}([0,T];\mathbb{R}^{N})\ni y\longmapsto\tilde{f}\circ y\in\mathcal{C}([0,T];\overline{C_{+}})
is continuous for the uniform topology. Hence the continuous mapping theorem implies the tightness of the laws of

\bigl(X^{(n)}(t\wedge\chi_{r}^{(n)})\bigr)_{0\leq t\leq T},\qquad n\in\mathbb{N},

in
\mathcal{C}([0,T];\overline{C_{+}})
. This proves (i).

We now prove the uniform singular-drift estimate. The idea is to test the approximating equation against a single vector
\rho\in C_{+}
. Since
\rho
lies in the open chamber, every positive root has strictly positive projection on
\rho
. Fix such a
\rho
, and set
c_{\beta}:=\langle\rho,\beta\rangle
,
\beta\in R_{+}
. Then
c_{\beta}>0
for every
\beta\in R_{+}
. Applying the scalar product
\langle\rho,\cdot\rangle
to the approximating SDE gives, for
t<\zeta^{(n)}
,

\displaystyle\langle\rho,X^{(n)}(t)\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle\rho,x_{0}\rangle+\sum_{j=1}^{N}\rho_{j}\int_{0}^{t}\sigma_{j}(s,X^{(n)}(s))\,dB_{j}^{(n)}(s)

\displaystyle\quad+\int_{0}^{t}\langle\rho,b(s,X^{(n)}(s))\rangle\,ds+\sum_{\beta\in R_{+}}c_{\beta}A^{(n),\beta}(t).

We now localize once more in order to justify taking expectations. Fix
m\in\mathbb{N}
and define

\eta_{m}^{(n)}:=\inf\left\{t\in[0,\zeta^{(n)}):\sum_{\beta\in R_{+}}c_{\beta}A^{(n),\beta}(t)\geq m\right\},

We then set
\tau_{r,m}^{(n)}=\chi_{r}^{(n)}\wedge\eta_{m}^{(n)}
. The role of
\eta_{m}^{(n)}
is only to cap the accumulated singular drift and thus make all terms manifestly integrable. Recall that by
K_{r_{*}}
we have denoted the closed centered ball with radius
r_{*}:=r\vee|x_{0}|
. For
0\leq t\leq\chi_{r}^{(n)}
, the process
X^{(n)}(t)
stays in
K_{r_{*}}
. Since
\sigma
is continuous, it is bounded on
[0,T]\times K_{r_{*}}
. Hence the quadratic variation of the martingale part in the semi-martingale decomposition of
\langle\rho,X^{(n)}(t)\rangle
stopped at
\tau_{r,m}^{(n)}
is bounded by a deterministic constant depending only on
r
,
T
, and
\rho
. In particular, the stopped martingale is square-integrable, and therefore

\mathbf{E}\left[\sum_{j=1}^{N}\rho_{j}\int_{0}^{\tau_{r,m}^{(n)}}\sigma_{j}(s,X^{(n)}(s))\,dB_{j}^{(n)}(s)\right]=0.

Evaluating the scalar identity at
\tau_{r,m}^{(n)}
and taking expectations, we obtain

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}\sum_{\beta\in R_{+}}c_{\beta}A^{(n),\beta}({\tau_{r,m}^{(n)}})

\displaystyle=\mathbf{E}\langle\rho,X^{(n)}({\tau_{r,m}^{(n)}})\rangle-\langle\rho,x_{0}\rangle-\mathbf{E}\int_{0}^{\tau_{r,m}^{(n)}}\langle\rho,b(s,X^{(n)}(s))\rangle\,ds.

We now estimate the right-hand side term by term. Since
|X^{(n)}({\tau_{r,m}^{(n)}})|\leq r_{*}
and
b
is continuous, we have

\mathbf{E}\langle\rho,X^{(n)}({\tau_{r,m}^{(n)}})\rangle\leq|\rho|\,r_{*}.

and

\left|\mathbf{E}\int_{0}^{\tau_{r,m}^{(n)}}\langle\rho,b(s,X^{(n)}(s))\rangle\,ds\right|\leq C_{\rho,r,T}.

Combining these bounds yields

\mathbf{E}\sum_{\beta\in R_{+}}c_{\beta}A^{(n),\beta}({\tau_{r,m}^{(n)}})\leq|\rho|\,r_{*}+|\rho|\,|x_{0}|+C_{\rho,r,T}.

We now let
m\to\infty
. Since each
A^{(n),\beta}
is increasing,

A^{(n),\beta}({\tau_{r,m}^{(n)}})\uparrow A^{(n),\beta}({\chi_{r}^{(n)}})\qquad\text{as }m\to\infty,

and monotone convergence gives

\mathbf{E}\sum_{\beta\in R_{+}}c_{\beta}A^{(n),\beta}({\chi_{r}^{(n)}})\leq|\rho|\,r_{*}+|\rho|\,|x_{0}|+C_{\rho,r,T}.

At this point the positivity of the coefficients
c_{\beta}
becomes decisive: for the fixed root
\alpha\in R_{+}
,

\mathbf{E}A^{(n),\alpha}({\chi_{r}^{(n)}})\leq\frac{|\rho|\,r_{*}+|\rho|\,|x_{0}|+C_{\rho,r,T}}{\langle\rho,\alpha\rangle}.

The right-hand side does not depend on
n
. Since

A^{(n),\alpha}({\chi_{r}^{(n)}})=\int_{0}^{\chi_{r}^{(n)}}\frac{k_{\alpha}^{(n)}(s,X^{(n)}(s))}{\langle X^{(n)}(s),\alpha\rangle}\,ds,

this proves the required uniform estimate. Finally, the monotonicity

\chi_{r}^{(n)}\uparrow T\wedge\zeta^{(n)}\qquad\text{as }r\to\infty

follows directly from the definition of the explosion lifetime through exit times from
compact balls.

It remains to prove the compactness of the stopped drift measures. For

\alpha\in R_{+}
, by (ii),

\sup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\mathbf{E}\mu_{r,T}^{(n),\alpha}([0,T])=\sup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\mathbf{E}A^{(n),\alpha}(\chi_{r}^{(n)})\leq C_{\alpha,r,T}.

Hence, by Markov’s inequality, for every
L>0
,

\sup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\mathbb{P}\bigl(\mu_{r,T}^{(n),\alpha}([0,T])>L\bigr)\leq\frac{C_{\alpha,r,T}}{L}.

Since
[0,T]
is compact, the set

\mathcal{K}_{L}:=\{\mu\in\mathcal{M}_{+}([0,T]):\ \mu([0,T])\leq L\}

is compact in the weak topology on finite measures. Therefore the laws of

\mu_{r,T}^{(n),\alpha}
,
n\in\mathbb{N}
, are tight in

\mathcal{M}_{+}([0,T])
. Since
R_{+}
is finite, the laws of

\bigl(\mu_{r,T}^{(n),\alpha}\bigr)_{\alpha\in R_{+}}

are tight in

\prod_{\alpha\in R_{+}}\mathcal{M}_{+}([0,T]).

Combining this measure tightness with the path tightness proved in (i)
gives the tightness of the joint laws of

\left(X^{(n)}(\cdot\wedge\chi_{r}^{(n)}),\bigl(\mu_{r,T}^{(n),\alpha}\bigr)_{\alpha\in R_{+}}\right)

in

\mathcal{C}([0,T];\overline{C_{+}})\times\prod_{\alpha\in R_{+}}\mathcal{M}_{+}([0,T]).

This proves (iii) and completes the proof.
∎

We now pass to the limit. At this point it is convenient to keep track not only of the
stopped paths and the stopped drift measures, but also of the corresponding regular
drift, martingale part, and quadratic variation. This will allow us to identify the
structure of the limiting equation before we analyze the support of the limiting drift
measures.

For fixed
T,r>0
, define

\displaystyle X^{(n),r}(t)

\displaystyle:=X^{(n)}(t\wedge\chi_{r}^{(n)}),

\displaystyle D^{(n),r}(t)

\displaystyle:=\int_{0}^{t\wedge\chi_{r}^{(n)}}b(s,X^{(n)}(s))\,ds,

\displaystyle M^{(n),r}(t)

\displaystyle:=\int_{0}^{t\wedge\chi_{r}^{(n)}}\sigma(s,X^{(n)}(s))\,dB^{(n)}(s),

\displaystyle Q_{i}^{(n),r}(t)

\displaystyle:=\int_{0}^{t\wedge\chi_{r}^{(n)}}\sigma_{i}^{2}(s,X^{(n)}(s))\,ds,\qquad i=1,\ldots,N.

Then, for every
0\leq t\leq T
,

X^{(n),r}(t)=x_{0}+M^{(n),r}(t)+D^{(n),r}(t)+\sum_{\alpha\in R_{+}}\alpha\,\mu_{r,T}^{(n),\alpha}([0,t]).

Proposition 11

(Skorokhod representation and generalized weak limit)
.

Assume
(C1)
-
(C2)
, and let
X^{(n)}
be the approximating
sequence. Then, after passing to a subsequence, there exist a probability space, a
continuous
\overline{C_{+}}
-valued process
X
, a continuous local martingale

M=(M_{1},\ldots,M_{N})
, and, for every
\alpha\in R_{+}
, a continuous increasing
process
A^{\alpha}
on
[0,\zeta)
with
A^{\alpha}(0)=0
, such that, for

\zeta:=\lim_{r\to\infty}\inf\{t\geq 0:\ |X(t)|\geq r\},

we have, for every
t<\zeta
,

(8.5)

X(t)=x_{0}+M(t)+\int_{0}^{t}b(s,X(s))\,ds+\sum_{\alpha\in R_{+}}\alpha\,A^{\alpha}(t),

and

(8.6)

\langle M_{i},M_{j}\rangle(t)=\delta_{ij}\int_{0}^{t}\sigma_{i}^{2}(s,X(s))\,ds,\qquad i,j=1,\ldots,N.

Consequently, possibly after enlarging the probability space, there exists an

N
-dimensional Brownian motion
B
such that

M(t)=\int_{0}^{t}\sigma(s,X(s))\,dB(s),\qquad t<\zeta.

In particular,
X
is a generalized weak limit of the approximating sequence.

Proof.

We begin with a fixed time horizon
T
and a fixed localization radius
r
. The
idea is first to identify all possible limits on the compact interval
[0,T]
, and
only afterwards to remove the localization by a diagonal argument.

On the stopped interval
[0,\chi_{r}^{(n)}]
, the process
X^{(n)}
remains in

K_{r_{*}}=\{x\in\overline{C_{+}}:\ |x|\leq r_{*}\}
,
r_{*}:=r\vee|x_{0}|
. Hence, by continuity of
b
and
\sigma
, there exists a constant

C_{r,T}<\infty
such that

|b(t,x)|+\sum_{i=1}^{N}|\sigma_{i}(t,x)|^{2}\leq C_{r,T},

for all
0\leq t\leq T
and all
x\in K_{r_{*}}
.

As a consequence, the stopped drift processes
D^{(n),r}
are uniformly Lipschitz:

|D^{(n),r}(t)-D^{(n),r}(s)|\leq C_{r,T}|t-s|,\qquad 0\leq s\leq t\leq T.

Similarly, each stopped quadratic variation process
Q_{i}^{(n),r}
satisfies

|Q_{i}^{(n),r}(t)-Q_{i}^{(n),r}(s)|\leq C_{r,T}|t-s|,\qquad 0\leq s\leq t\leq T.

Thus the families
\{D^{(n),r}\}_{n\geq 1}
and
\{Q^{(n),r}\}_{n\geq 1}
are tight
in the corresponding spaces of continuous paths.

For the martingale part, we again use Kolmogorov’s criterion. Let
0\leq s\leq t\leq T
.
By the Burkholder-Davis-Gundy inequality,

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}\left[|M^{(n),r}(t)-M^{(n),r}(s)|^{4}\right]

\displaystyle\leq C\mathbf{E}\left[\left(\int_{s}^{t}\mathbf{1}_{\{u\leq\chi_{r}^{(n)}\}}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\sigma_{i}^{2}(u,X^{(n)}(u))\,du\right)^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\leq C_{r,T}|t-s|^{2},

where
C
is the Burkholder-Davis-Gundy constant for
p=4
, and the value of

C_{r,T}
may change from line to line. Therefore
\{M^{(n),r}\}_{n\geq 1}
is
tight in
\mathcal{C}([0,T];\mathbb{R}^{N})
.

Combining these bounds with the path tightness and measure compactness from Proposition
10
, we obtain tightness of the joint laws of

\left(X^{(n),r},D^{(n),r},M^{(n),r},Q^{(n),r},\bigl(\mu_{r,T}^{(n),\alpha}\bigr)_{\alpha\in R_{+}}\right)

in

\mathcal{C}([0,T];\overline{C_{+}})\times\mathcal{C}([0,T];\mathbb{R}^{N})\times\mathcal{C}([0,T];\mathbb{R}^{N})\times\mathcal{C}([0,T];\mathbb{R}_{+}^{N})\times\prod_{\alpha\in R_{+}}\mathcal{M}_{+}([0,T]).

By Prokhorov’s theorem and the Skorokhod representation theorem, after passing to a
subsequence we may realize these variables on a common probability space so that they
converge almost surely. We denote the corresponding limits by

\left(X^{r},D^{r},M^{r},Q^{r},\bigl(\mu^{\alpha,r}\bigr)_{\alpha\in R_{+}}\right).

Thus
X^{(n),r}\longrightarrow X^{r}
,
D^{(n),r}\longrightarrow D^{r}
,
M^{(n),r}\longrightarrow M^{r}
,
Q^{(n),r}\longrightarrow Q^{r}
and all of those convergence are uniform on
[0,T]
. Moreover, for every
\alpha\in R_{+}
,

\mu_{r,T}^{(n),\alpha}\Longrightarrow\mu^{\alpha,r}

weakly as finite measures on
[0,T]
.

The next point is to recover the martingale structure in the limit. For each
n
,

M^{(n),r}
is a continuous square-integrable martingale, and for every

i,j\in\{1,\ldots,N\}
the process

M_{i}^{(n),r}(t)M_{j}^{(n),r}(t)-\delta_{ij}Q_{i}^{(n),r}(t)

is a martingale. Moreover,

\sup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\mathbf{E}Q_{i}^{(n),r}(T)\leq C_{r,T},

so these martingales are uniformly integrable. Passing to the limit in the usual way
against bounded continuous functionals of the paths up to time
s
, we conclude
that
M^{r}
is a continuous martingale and that

M_{i}^{r}(t)M_{j}^{r}(t)-\delta_{ij}Q_{i}^{r}(t)

is a martingale as well. Hence

\langle M_{i}^{r},M_{j}^{r}\rangle(t)=\delta_{ij}Q_{i}^{r}(t),\qquad 0\leq t\leq T.

We now identify the deterministic finite-variation terms. Set

\chi_{r}:=T\wedge\inf\{t\geq 0:\ |X^{r}(t)|\geq r\}.

Fix
t<\chi_{r}
. By continuity of
X^{r}
, the path stays a positive distance away
from the sphere
\{|x|=r\}
on
[0,t]
. It follows that, for all sufficiently
large
n
, one has
\chi_{r}^{(n)}>t
. On this interval the approximating stopped
processes are not yet affected by localization, and the uniform convergence together
with continuity of
b
and
\sigma
gives

\displaystyle D^{r}(t)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{t}b(s,X^{r}(s))\,ds,

\displaystyle Q_{i}^{r}(t)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{t}\sigma_{i}^{2}(s,X^{r}(s))\,ds,\qquad i=1,\ldots,N.

For every
\alpha\in R_{+}
, let

A^{\alpha,r}(t):=\mu^{\alpha,r}([0,t]),\qquad 0\leq t\leq T.

At every common continuity point
t
of the finite measures

\mu^{\alpha,r}
,
\alpha\in R_{+}
, the identity

X^{(n),r}(t)=x_{0}+M^{(n),r}(t)+D^{(n),r}(t)+\sum_{\alpha\in R_{+}}\alpha\,\mu_{r,T}^{(n),\alpha}([0,t])

passes to the limit and yields

X^{r}(t)=x_{0}+M^{r}(t)+D^{r}(t)+\sum_{\alpha\in R_{+}}\alpha\,A^{\alpha,r}(t).

The set of common continuity points is dense in
[0,T]
. Since
X^{r}
,
M^{r}
,
and
D^{r}
are continuous, the vector finite-variation process

V^{r}(t):=X^{r}(t)-x_{0}-M^{r}(t)-D^{r}(t)

is continuous. Thus
V^{r}
is a continuous version of

\sum_{\alpha\in R_{+}}\alpha\,A^{\alpha,r}
.

At this stage only the vector combination is known to be continuous. To conclude that
each individual process
A^{\alpha,r}
is continuous, we use again a vector

\rho\in C_{+}
. Since
\langle\rho,\alpha\rangle>0
for all
\alpha\in R_{+}
,
every jump of
V^{r}
would satisfy

\langle\rho,\Delta V^{r}(t)\rangle=\sum_{\alpha\in R_{+}}\langle\rho,\alpha\rangle\,\Delta A^{\alpha,r}(t).

The left-hand side is zero because
V^{r}
is continuous, whereas every term on the
right-hand side is nonnegative. Hence

\Delta A^{\alpha,r}(t)=0,\qquad\alpha\in R_{+},\qquad 0\leq t\leq T.

So each
A^{\alpha,r}
is continuous and increasing, and the localized identity
holds for every
t\in[0,T]
.

We now remove the localization. Choose increasing sequences

T_{m}\uparrow\infty,\qquad r_{m}\uparrow\infty,\qquad r_{m}>|x_{0}|.

Applying the preceding construction successively to the pairs
(T_{m},r_{m})
and then
using a diagonal extraction, we obtain localized limits which are consistent on
overlapping time intervals. The consistency comes from the fact that the corresponding
stopped approximating objects agree up to the smaller stopping time, and this
agreement is preserved in the limit. We may therefore patch the localized limits
together and obtain a continuous
\overline{C_{+}}
-valued process
X
, a continuous local
martingale
M
, and, for every
\alpha\in R_{+}
, a continuous increasing process

A^{\alpha}
defined on
[0,\zeta)
, where

\zeta:=\lim_{m\to\infty}\inf\{t\geq 0:\ |X(t)|\geq r_{m}\}=\lim_{r\to\infty}\inf\{t\geq 0:\ |X(t)|\geq r\}.

Since
X(0)=x_{0}
and
X
is continuous, we have
\zeta>0
almost surely.

For every
t<\zeta
, choosing
m
so large that
t<T_{m}
and

|X(s)|<r_{m}
for
0\leq s\leq t
, the localized equation gives

X(t)=x_{0}+M(t)+\int_{0}^{t}b(s,X(s))\,ds+\sum_{\alpha\in R_{+}}\alpha\,A^{\alpha}(t),

and similarly

\langle M_{i},M_{j}\rangle(t)=\delta_{ij}\int_{0}^{t}\sigma_{i}^{2}(s,X(s))\,ds,\qquad i,j=1,\ldots,N.

It remains to rewrite the martingale
M
as a stochastic integral with respect to a
Brownian motion. This is standard. Possibly after enlarging the probability space, one
may construct independent Brownian motions on the sets where the coefficients

\sigma_{i}(s,X(s))
vanish and divide the martingale part by
\sigma_{i}(s,X(s))

elsewhere. Lévy’s characterization then yields an
N
-dimensional Brownian motion

B=(B_{1},\ldots,B_{N})
such that

M(t)=\int_{0}^{t}\sigma(s,X(s))\,dB(s),\qquad t<\zeta.

This proves (
8.5
) and
(
8.6
), and completes the construction of the generalized weak
limit.
∎

8.2.
Identification of the limiting singular drift

We now identify the part of the limiting drift which is already visible in the approximating equations. Away from the wall

\{\langle x,\alpha\rangle=0\}
, the singular coefficient
{k_{\alpha}(t,x)}/{\langle x,\alpha\rangle}
is an ordinary continuous function. Hence no loss of information can occur in the limit on regions where
\langle X,\alpha\rangle
stays strictly positive. Any possible ambiguity in the limiting increasing process
A^{\alpha}
can therefore only be supported on the set of times at which the limiting path lies on the corresponding wall. The next proposition makes this precise. It decomposes
A^{\alpha}
into the absolutely continuous interior singular drift and a possible residual boundary measure. It also records that the localized uniform estimates from Proposition
10
pass to the limit and give local integrability of the interior singular drift.

Proposition 12

(Interior identification and local integrability of the limiting drift)
.

Let

\left(X,M,\bigl(A^{\alpha}\bigr)_{\alpha\in R_{+}}\right)

be a generalized weak limit obtained in Proposition
11
. Then, for every
\alpha\in R_{+}
, there exists a continuous increasing process
\Lambda^{\alpha}
on
[0,\zeta)
, with
\Lambda^{\alpha}(0)=0
, such that, almost surely, for every
t<\zeta
,

(8.7)

A^{\alpha}(t)=\int_{0}^{t}\frac{k_{\alpha}(s,X(s))}{\langle X(s),\alpha\rangle}\mathbf{1}_{\{\langle X(s),\alpha\rangle>0\}}\,ds+\Lambda^{\alpha}(t).

Moreover, the Stieltjes measure
d\Lambda^{\alpha}
is supported on the wall
\{\langle X,\alpha\rangle=0\}
, in the sense that, almost surely, for every
t<\zeta
,

(8.8)

\int_{0}^{t}\mathbf{1}_{\{\langle X(s),\alpha\rangle>0\}}\,d\Lambda^{\alpha}(s)=0.

Equivalently, on
[0,\zeta)
,

(8.9)

dA^{\alpha}(t)=\frac{k_{\alpha}(t,X(t))}{\langle X(t),\alpha\rangle}\mathbf{1}_{\{\langle X(t),\alpha\rangle>0\}}\,dt+d\Lambda^{\alpha}(t).

Furthermore, for every
T,r>0
, define

(8.10)

\chi_{r}:=T\wedge\inf\{t\in[0,\zeta):\ |X(t)|\geq r\},

with the convention
\inf\varnothing=\infty
. Then, for every
\alpha\in R_{+}
,

(8.11)

\mathbf{E}\int_{0}^{\chi_{r}}\frac{k_{\alpha}(s,X(s))}{\langle X(s),\alpha\rangle}\mathbf{1}_{\{\langle X(s),\alpha\rangle>0\}}\,ds\leq C_{\alpha,r,T},

where
C_{\alpha,r,T}
is the constant from Proposition
10
(ii)
. Consequently, almost surely,

(8.12)

\int_{0}^{t}\frac{k_{\alpha}(s,X(s))}{\langle X(s),\alpha\rangle}\mathbf{1}_{\{\langle X(s),\alpha\rangle>0\}}\,ds<\infty

for every
t<\zeta
.

Proof.

We first work on a fixed compact time interval and with a fixed localization radius. The point is to test the limiting measure only in regions where the path stays away both from the wall and from the stopping sphere. On such sets the singular density is regular, and the limit can be identified directly.

Fix
T,r>0
and
\alpha\in R_{+}
. Recall the localized stopped paths and stopped drift measures (
8.4
)

\displaystyle X^{(n),r}(t)

\displaystyle:=X^{(n)}(t\wedge\chi_{r}^{(n)}),

\displaystyle\mu_{r,T}^{(n),\alpha}(dt)

\displaystyle:=\mathbf{1}_{\{t\leq\chi_{r}^{(n)}\}}\frac{k_{\alpha}^{(n)}(t,X^{(n)}(t))}{\langle X^{(n)}(t),\alpha\rangle}\,dt,

together with the stopping time

\chi_{r}^{(n)}:=T\wedge\inf\{t\in[0,\zeta^{(n)}):\ |X^{(n)}(t)|\geq r\}.

Along the Skorokhod subsequence from Proposition
11
, we may assume that

\displaystyle X^{(n),r}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow X^{r}\quad\text{uniformly on }[0,T],

\displaystyle\mu_{r,T}^{(n),\alpha}

\displaystyle\Longrightarrow\mu^{\alpha,r}\quad\text{weakly as finite measures on }[0,T].

Here
\mu^{\alpha,r}
is the localized limiting measure corresponding to the root
\alpha
. We first identify
\mu^{\alpha,r}
away from the wall and away from the stopping sphere. Let
\varphi\in C_{b}([0,T]\times\overline{C_{+}})
be such that, for some
\delta>0
,

\displaystyle\varphi(t,x)

\displaystyle=0,\qquad\text{whenever }\langle x,\alpha\rangle\leq\delta\textrm{ or }|x|\geq r.

Thus
\varphi
only probes those portions of the paths where the denominator
\langle x,\alpha\rangle
stays safely away from zero and where the localization has not yet reached the stopping sphere. Define

F_{n}(t,x):=\varphi(t,x)\frac{k_{\alpha}^{(n)}(t,x)}{\langle x,\alpha\rangle},\quad F(t,x):=\varphi(t,x)\frac{k_{\alpha}(t,x)}{\langle x,\alpha\rangle}\/,

with the obvious convention
F_{n}(t,x)=0
and
F(t,x)=0
, when
\langle x,\alpha\rangle=0
. Since
\varphi
is supported away from the wall, both
F_{n}
and
F
are bounded and continuous on the compact region relevant for the stopped paths. Moreover, since
k_{\alpha}^{(n)}(t,x)\to k_{\alpha}(t,x)
, we have
F_{n}\longrightarrow F
uniformly on compact subsets of
[0,T]\times\overline{C_{+}}
.

For each
n
, the definition of
\mu_{r,T}^{(n),\alpha}
gives

(8.13)

\int_{0}^{T}\varphi\bigl(t,X^{(n),r}(t)\bigr)\,\mu_{r,T}^{(n),\alpha}(dt)=\int_{0}^{T}F_{n}\bigl(t,X^{(n),r}(t)\bigr)\,dt.

Indeed, before the stopping time
\chi_{r}^{(n)}
we have

X^{(n),r}(t)=X^{(n)}(t)
, while after the stopping time the stopped path stays at a
point of norm
r
, where
\varphi
vanishes.

We now pass to the limit in both sides of (
8.13
). Since

X^{(n),r}\to X^{r}
uniformly and
\varphi
is bounded and continuous, we have

\varphi\bigl(\cdot,X^{(n),r}(\cdot)\bigr)\longrightarrow\varphi\bigl(\cdot,X^{r}(\cdot)\bigr)

uniformly on
[0,T]
. Therefore,

\displaystyle\left|\int_{0}^{T}\varphi\bigl(t,X^{(n),r}(t)\bigr)\,\mu_{r,T}^{(n),\alpha}(dt)-\int_{0}^{T}\varphi\bigl(t,X^{r}(t)\bigr)\,\mu^{\alpha,r}(dt)\right|

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\left|\varphi\bigl(t,X^{(n),r}(t)\bigr)-\varphi\bigl(t,X^{r}(t)\bigr)\right|\mu_{r,T}^{(n),\alpha}([0,T])

\displaystyle\quad+\left|\int_{0}^{T}\varphi\bigl(t,X^{r}(t)\bigr)\,\mu_{r,T}^{(n),\alpha}(dt)-\int_{0}^{T}\varphi\bigl(t,X^{r}(t)\bigr)\,\mu^{\alpha,r}(dt)\right|.

The first term tends to zero because the integrands converge uniformly and the total
masses
\mu_{r,T}^{(n),\alpha}([0,T])
remain bounded along the convergent
subsequence; the second tends to zero by the weak convergence of the measures. Hence

(8.14)

\int_{0}^{T}\varphi\bigl(t,X^{(n),r}(t)\bigr)\,\mu_{r,T}^{(n),\alpha}(dt)\longrightarrow\int_{0}^{T}\varphi\bigl(t,X^{r}(t)\bigr)\,\mu^{\alpha,r}(dt).

On the other hand, the uniform convergence of
X^{(n),r}
together with the uniform
convergence of
F_{n}
to
F
on compact sets yields

(8.15)

\int_{0}^{T}F_{n}\bigl(t,X^{(n),r}(t)\bigr)\,dt\longrightarrow\int_{0}^{T}F\bigl(t,X^{r}(t)\bigr)\,dt.

Combining (
8.13
), (
8.14
), and
(
8.15
), we obtain

(8.16)

\int_{0}^{T}\varphi\bigl(t,X^{r}(t)\bigr)\,\mu^{\alpha,r}(dt)=\int_{0}^{T}\varphi\bigl(t,X^{r}(t)\bigr)\frac{k_{\alpha}(t,X^{r}(t))}{\langle X^{r}(t),\alpha\rangle}\mathbf{1}_{\{\langle X^{r}(t),\alpha\rangle>0\}}\,dt.

This identity already shows that the limiting measure has the expected density
wherever the path stays away from the wall and away from the stopping sphere.

We now remove the auxiliary cutoff in the space variable. Define

\chi_{r}:=T\wedge\inf\{t\in[0,\zeta):\ |X(t)|\geq r\}.

and

O_{\alpha,r}:=\{t\in[0,T]:\ t<\chi_{r},\ \langle X^{r}(t),\alpha\rangle>0\}.

The set
O_{\alpha,r}
is open. Let
K\subset O_{\alpha,r}
be compact. By
continuity of
X^{r}
, there exist constants
\delta>0
and
r_{0}<r
such that

\displaystyle\langle X^{r}(t),\alpha\rangle

\displaystyle\geq 2\delta,\qquad|X^{r}(t)|\leq r_{0}\/,\qquad t\in K.

Choose continuous cutoff functions
\theta
and
\eta
satisfying

\displaystyle\theta(y)

\displaystyle=0\quad\text{for }y\leq\delta,

\displaystyle\theta(y)

\displaystyle=1\quad\text{for }y\geq 2\delta,

\displaystyle\eta(x)

\displaystyle=1\quad\text{for }|x|\leq r_{0},

\displaystyle\eta(x)

\displaystyle=0\quad\text{for }|x|\geq r.

Now let
\psi\in C_{c}(O_{\alpha,r})
have support contained in
K
, and define

\varphi(t,x):=\psi(t)\,\theta(\langle x,\alpha\rangle)\,\eta(x).

We extend
\psi
by zero to all of
[0,T]
. By construction,

\varphi\bigl(t,X^{r}(t)\bigr)=\psi(t)\qquad\text{for }t\in\operatorname{supp}\psi.

Substituting this choice of
\varphi
into
(
8.16
), we obtain

\int_{0}^{T}\psi(t)\,\mu^{\alpha,r}(dt)=\int_{0}^{T}\psi(t)\frac{k_{\alpha}(t,X^{r}(t))}{\langle X^{r}(t),\alpha\rangle}\,dt.

Since continuous compactly supported functions determine finite measures on the open
set
O_{\alpha,r}
, it follows that

(8.17)

\mu^{\alpha,r}(dt)=\frac{k_{\alpha}(t,X^{r}(t))}{\langle X^{r}(t),\alpha\rangle}\,dt\qquad\text{on }O_{\alpha,r}.

We now return to the global limit constructed in
Proposition
11
. The localized limits there were chosen
consistently as
T
and
r
vary, so the preceding identity passes through the
diagonal construction and yields, on the random interval
[0,\zeta)
,

(8.18)

dA^{\alpha}(t)=\frac{k_{\alpha}(t,X(t))}{\langle X(t),\alpha\rangle}\,dt\qquad\text{on }\{t<\zeta:\ \langle X(t),\alpha\rangle>0\}.

At this point the decomposition is immediate. Let

Z_{\alpha}:=\{t<\zeta:\ \langle X(t),\alpha\rangle=0\}.

Let
\lambda^{\alpha}
be the measure obtained by restricting
dA^{\alpha}

to
Z_{\alpha}
, that is,

\lambda^{\alpha}(B):=dA^{\alpha}(B\cap Z_{\alpha}),\qquad B\subset[0,\zeta)\ \text{Borel}.

Define

\Lambda^{\alpha}(t):=\lambda^{\alpha}([0,t]).

Then
\Lambda^{\alpha}
is continuous and increasing, and
d\Lambda^{\alpha}
is supported on
Z_{\alpha}
. Moreover,

dA^{\alpha}=\mathbf{1}_{Z_{\alpha}^{c}}\,dA^{\alpha}+d\Lambda^{\alpha}.

By construction, the measure
dA^{\alpha}
coincides with the absolutely continuous measure in (
8.18
) away from the wall, and the remaining part is exactly
d\Lambda^{\alpha}
. This gives (
8.9
). The support property is built into the definition of
d\Lambda^{\alpha}
, and the integrated form (
8.7
) follows by integrating (
8.9
) on
[0,t]
.

We now prove the localized expectation bound. Fix
r,T>0
and
\alpha\in R_{+}
. Recall again the stopped approximating measures (
8.4
). By Proposition
10
(ii)

(8.19)

\sup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\mathbf{E}\mu_{r,T}^{(n),\alpha}([0,T])=\sup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\mathbf{E}A^{(n),\alpha}(\chi_{r}^{(n)})\leq C_{\alpha,r,T}.

We now pass this bound to the limit. Along the Skorokhod subsequence constructed in
Proposition
11
, the measures

\mu_{r,T}^{(n),\alpha}
converge weakly to the localized limiting measure

\mu^{\alpha,r}
on
[0,T]
. Since the constant function
1
is continuous on
the compact interval
[0,T]
, weak convergence yields

\mu_{r,T}^{(n),\alpha}([0,T])\longrightarrow\mu^{\alpha,r}([0,T])

almost surely. Therefore Fatou’s lemma together with
(
8.19
) gives

(8.20)

\mathbf{E}\mu^{\alpha,r}([0,T])\leq\liminf_{n\to\infty}\mathbf{E}\mu_{r,T}^{(n),\alpha}([0,T])\leq C_{\alpha,r,T}.

We next relate this localized limiting measure to the global increasing process

A^{\alpha}
. By the consistency built into the diagonal construction from
Proposition
11
, the localized cumulative process

A^{\alpha,r}(t):=\mu^{\alpha,r}([0,t]),\qquad 0\leq t\leq T,

coincides with
A^{\alpha}(t\wedge\chi_{r})
. In particular,
\mu^{\alpha,r}([0,T])=A^{\alpha}(\chi_{r})
. By the decomposition already proved above, we have

A^{\alpha}(t)=\int_{0}^{t}\frac{k_{\alpha}(s,X(s))}{\langle X(s),\alpha\rangle}\mathbf{1}_{\{\langle X(s),\alpha\rangle>0\}}\,ds+\Lambda^{\alpha}(t),\qquad t<\zeta,

where
\Lambda^{\alpha}
is continuous, increasing, and nonnegative. Evaluating this
identity at
t=\chi_{r}
, we obtain

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\chi_{r}}\frac{k_{\alpha}(s,X(s))}{\langle X(s),\alpha\rangle}\mathbf{1}_{\{\langle X(s),\alpha\rangle>0\}}\,ds

\displaystyle\leq A^{\alpha}(\chi_{r})=\mu^{\alpha,r}([0,T]).

Taking expectations and using (
8.20
) proves
(
8.11
).

It remains to deduce the pathwise local statement. For each integer
m>|x_{0}|
and
each integer
q\geq 1
, the already proved estimate with
r=m
and
T=q
implies

\int_{0}^{q\wedge\tau_{m}}\frac{k_{\alpha}(s,X(s))}{\langle X(s),\alpha\rangle}\mathbf{1}_{\{\langle X(s),\alpha\rangle>0\}}\,ds<\infty

almost surely, where
\tau_{m}:=\inf\{t\in[0,\zeta):\ |X(t)|\geq m\}
. Since the family of pairs
(m,q)
is countable, we may intersect these events and obtain a single event of probability one on which all such localized integrals are finite. Fix now a sample point in this event and let
t<\zeta
. Since
X
is continuous on
[0,t]
, there exists an integer
m>|x_{0}|
such that

\sup_{0\leq s\leq t}|X(s)|<m.

Hence
\tau_{m}>t
. Choosing an integer
q>t
, we get

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}\frac{k_{\alpha}(s,X(s))}{\langle X(s),\alpha\rangle}\mathbf{1}_{\{\langle X(s),\alpha\rangle>0\}}\,ds

\displaystyle\leq\int_{0}^{q\wedge\tau_{m}}\frac{k_{\alpha}(s,X(s))}{\langle X(s),\alpha\rangle}\mathbf{1}_{\{\langle X(s),\alpha\rangle>0\}}\,ds<\infty.

This proves (
8.12
).
∎

8.3.
Face detectors and zero local time

We now introduce a canonical detector attached to an arbitrary collision face. Its purpose is to produce a scalar quantity which is nonnegative on the chamber and vanishes exactly on the chosen face after localization. When applied to the generalized weak equation, this detector yields a one-dimensional identity in which the roots vanishing on the face appear explicitly, while all other roots are absorbed into the regular drift. This will be the basic tool in the next subsection, where we eliminate the possible boundary measures.

Fix
S\in\mathfrak{S}
. Recall that
F_{S}
denotes the corresponding relatively open face and that, for
\eta,r>0
,

\mathcal{U}_{S,\eta,r}:=\{x\in\overline{C_{+}}:\ |x|\leq r,\ \langle x,\beta\rangle\geq\eta\text{ for every }\beta\in R_{+}\setminus S\}.

Thus
\mathcal{U}_{S,\eta,r}
is a localized neighborhood of
F_{S}
in which the roots outside
S
are kept uniformly away from zero. We define the canonical face detector by

u_{S}:=\sum_{\beta\in S}\beta,\qquad\tau_{S}(x):=\langle u_{S},x\rangle=\sum_{\beta\in S}\langle x,\beta\rangle.

By our convention in the definition of the detector family,
u_{S}\in\mathfrak{D}(S)
. In particular,
B_{S}=B_{S,u_{S}}
is one of the regular detector drifts to which Assumption
(D2)
applies. Thus, on
\mathcal{U}_{S,\eta,r}
,

B_{S}(t,x)=\langle u_{S},b(t,x)\rangle+\sum_{\beta\in R_{+}\setminus S}\frac{k_{\beta}(t,x)\langle u_{S},\beta\rangle}{\langle x,\beta\rangle}.

The detector
\tau_{S}
has two useful features. First, it is nonnegative on the whole
chamber and vanishes exactly when all roots in
S
vanish. Second, it sees all roots
in
S
simultaneously, in the sense that every such root appears with a strictly
positive coefficient in the detector equation. We also set

(8.21)

q_{S}(t,x):=\sum_{j=1}^{N}u_{S,j}^{2}\,\sigma_{j}^{2}(t,x).

We collect these elementary geometric facts, together with the localized detector equation needed later, in a single lemma.

Lemma 1

(Canonical detector and its localized equation)
.

Let
S\in\mathfrak{S}
, and let
u_{S}
and
\tau_{S}
be defined as above. Let
\left(X,M,\bigl(A^{\alpha}\bigr)_{\alpha\in R_{+}}\right)
be a generalized weak limit obtained in Proposition
11
, and let the decomposition from Proposition
12
hold. Then the following statements hold.

(i)

For every
x\in\overline{C_{+}}
,

\tau_{S}(x)\geq 0.

Moreover,

\tau_{S}(x)=0\qquad\Longleftrightarrow\qquad\langle x,\beta\rangle=0\quad\text{for every }\beta\in S.

In particular,
\tau_{S}
vanishes on
F_{S}
, and on

\mathcal{U}_{S,\eta,r}
one has

\tau_{S}(x)=0\qquad\Longleftrightarrow\qquad x\in F_{S}.

(ii)

For every
x\in\overline{C_{+}}
and every
\beta\in S
,

0\leq\langle x,\beta\rangle\leq\tau_{S}(x).

(iii)

For every
\beta\in S
,

\langle u_{S},\beta\rangle>0.

Hence
u_{S}
is an admissible detector for the face
F_{S}
, and
\Gamma(S,u_{S})=S
. In particular, the constant
c_{S}:=\min_{\beta\in S}\langle u_{S},\beta\rangle
is strictly positive.

(iv)

We have

q_{S}(t,x)\leq|S|\sum_{\beta\in S}a_{\beta}(t,x),\qquad(t,x)\in[0,\infty)\times\overline{C_{+}}.

(v)

Let
T,r,\eta>0
, and let
\theta
be a stopping time such that
\theta\leq T
,
X(s)\in\mathcal{U}_{S,\eta,r}
,
0\leq s\leq\theta
. Then, for every
0\leq t\leq T
,

\displaystyle\tau_{S}\bigl(X(t\wedge\theta)\bigr)

\displaystyle=\tau_{S}(x_{0})+M_{S}(t\wedge\theta)+\int_{0}^{t\wedge\theta}B_{S}(s,X(s))\,ds

\displaystyle\quad+\sum_{\beta\in S}\langle u_{S},\beta\rangle\int_{0}^{t\wedge\theta}\frac{k_{\beta}(s,X(s))}{\langle X(s),\beta\rangle}\mathbf{1}_{\{\langle X(s),\beta\rangle>0\}}\,ds

(8.22)

\displaystyle\quad+\sum_{\beta\in S}\langle u_{S},\beta\rangle\Lambda^{\beta}(t\wedge\theta),

where
M_{S}(t):=\langle u_{S},M(t)\rangle
.

(vi)

The process
M_{S}
is a continuous local martingale and

(8.23)

\langle M_{S}\rangle(t)=\int_{0}^{t}q_{S}(s,X(s))\,ds.

Consequently,

(8.24)

\langle M_{S}(\cdot\wedge\theta)\rangle(t)=\int_{0}^{t\wedge\theta}q_{S}(s,X(s))\,ds.

Proof.

Since every
\beta\in S\subset R_{+}
is nonnegative on
\overline{C_{+}}
, we have

\tau_{S}(x)=\sum_{\beta\in S}\langle x,\beta\rangle\geq 0,\qquad x\in\overline{C_{+}}.

This proves the first assertion in (i). Moreover, all summands in the last display are nonnegative, so
\tau_{S}(x)=0
holds if and only if every term
\langle x,\beta\rangle
,
\beta\in S
, is zero. Since every point
x\in F_{S}
satisfies
\langle x,\beta\rangle=0
for
\beta\in S
, the detector vanishes on
F_{S}
. On the localized set

\mathcal{U}_{S,\eta,r}
, the roots outside
S
are strictly positive, so
\tau_{S}(x)=0
is equivalent to
x\in F_{S}
. This proves (i). Assertion (ii) is immediate from the definition
\tau_{S}(x)=\sum_{\gamma\in S}\langle x,\gamma\rangle
and every term in this sum is nonnegative on
\overline{C_{+}}
.

For (iii), let
R_{S}:=R\cap\operatorname{span}S
. Since
S=S(y)
for some
y\in\partial C_{+}
, the point
y
is orthogonal to every root in
S
, hence to the whole space
\operatorname{span}S
. Therefore
S=R_{S}\cap R_{+}
so
S
is precisely the set of positive roots of the subsystem
R_{S}
. Let
\Delta_{S}=\{\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{m}\}
be the corresponding simple system, and set

\rho_{S}:=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\gamma\in S}\gamma.

Then
u_{S}=2\rho_{S}
. We first show that
\langle u_{S},\alpha\rangle>0
,
\alpha\in\Delta_{S}
. To see that let us fix
\alpha\in\Delta_{S}
. The reflection
s_{\alpha}
preserves the set
S\setminus\{\alpha\}
, and
s_{\alpha}(\alpha)=-\alpha
. Hence

s_{\alpha}(2\rho_{S})=-\alpha+\sum_{\gamma\in S\setminus\{\alpha\}}s_{\alpha}(\gamma)=-\alpha+\sum_{\gamma\in S\setminus\{\alpha\}}\gamma=2\rho_{S}-2\alpha.

On the other hand,

s_{\alpha}(v)=v-\frac{2\langle v,\alpha\rangle}{\langle\alpha,\alpha\rangle}\alpha,\qquad\frac{2\langle 2\rho_{S},\alpha\rangle}{\langle\alpha,\alpha\rangle}=2.

Therefore

\langle u_{S},\alpha\rangle=\langle 2\rho_{S},\alpha\rangle=\langle\alpha,\alpha\rangle>0.

Now let
\beta\in S
. Since
\beta
is a positive root of the subsystem
R_{S}
, it can be written as

\beta=\sum_{j=1}^{m}c_{j}\alpha_{j},\qquad c_{j}\geq 0,

with at least one coefficient
c_{j}
strictly positive. Consequently,

\langle u_{S},\beta\rangle=\sum_{j=1}^{m}c_{j}\langle u_{S},\alpha_{j}\rangle>0.

This proves (iii). Finally, to prove (iv), write

u_{S,j}=\sum_{\beta\in S}\beta_{j}.

By the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality,

u_{S,j}^{2}=\left(\sum_{\beta\in S}\beta_{j}\right)^{2}\leq|S|\sum_{\beta\in S}\beta_{j}^{2}.

Multiplying by
\sigma_{j}^{2}(t,x)
and summing over
j=1,\ldots,N
, we obtain

\displaystyle q_{S}(t,x)

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=1}^{N}u_{S,j}^{2}\,\sigma_{j}^{2}(t,x)\leq|S|\sum_{j=1}^{N}\sum_{\beta\in S}\beta_{j}^{2}\,\sigma_{j}^{2}(t,x)=|S|\sum_{\beta\in S}a_{\beta}(t,x).

This proves (iv).

To deal with the remaining part note that
\tau_{S}
is linear, applying it to the generalized weak equation for
X
produces no Itô correction. We obtain

\displaystyle\tau_{S}(X(t))

\displaystyle=\tau_{S}(x_{0})+M_{S}(t)+\int_{0}^{t}\langle u_{S},b(s,X(s))\rangle\,ds+\sum_{\beta\in R_{+}}\langle u_{S},\beta\rangle A^{\beta}(t).

Stopping this identity at
\theta
, with the properties stated before, gives

\displaystyle\tau_{S}\bigl(X(t\wedge\theta)\bigr)

\displaystyle=\tau_{S}(x_{0})+M_{S}(t\wedge\theta)+\int_{0}^{t\wedge\theta}\langle u_{S},b(s,X(s))\rangle\,ds+\sum_{\beta\in R_{+}}\langle u_{S},\beta\rangle A^{\beta}(t\wedge\theta).

We now split the roots into those in
S
and those outside
S
. On the interval
[0,\theta]
, every root
\beta\in R_{+}\setminus S
satisfies
\langle X(s),\beta\rangle\geq\eta
. Hence, by Proposition
12
, the boundary measure
d\Lambda^{\beta}
carries no mass on
[0,\theta]
, and the indicator
\mathbf{1}_{\{\langle X(s),\beta\rangle>0\}}
is identically equal to one there. Therefore

A^{\beta}(t\wedge\theta)=\int_{0}^{t\wedge\theta}\frac{k_{\beta}(s,X(s))}{\langle X(s),\beta\rangle}\,ds,\qquad\beta\in R_{+}\setminus S.

Consequently, the contribution of the roots outside
S
is

\int_{0}^{t\wedge\theta}\sum_{\beta\in R_{+}\setminus S}\frac{k_{\beta}(s,X(s))\langle u_{S},\beta\rangle}{\langle X(s),\beta\rangle}\,ds,

which, together with the ordinary drift
\langle u_{S},b(s,X(s))\rangle\,ds
, is exactly
B_{S}(s,X(s))\,ds
. The remaining contribution comes from the roots in
S
. For the roots
\beta\in S
, we use the decomposition from
Proposition
12
. Thus

\displaystyle A^{\beta}(t\wedge\theta)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{t\wedge\theta}\frac{k_{\beta}(s,X(s))}{\langle X(s),\beta\rangle}\mathbf{1}_{\{\langle X(s),\beta\rangle>0\}}\,ds+\Lambda^{\beta}(t\wedge\theta).

Therefore the contribution of the roots in
S
is

\displaystyle\sum_{\beta\in S}\langle u_{S},\beta\rangle\int_{0}^{t\wedge\theta}\frac{k_{\beta}(s,X(s))}{\langle X(s),\beta\rangle}\mathbf{1}_{\{\langle X(s),\beta\rangle>0\}}\,ds+\sum_{\beta\in S}\langle u_{S},\beta\rangle\Lambda^{\beta}(t\wedge\theta).

Combining the contribution of the roots in
R_{+}\setminus S
with the ordinary drift gives the term

\int_{0}^{t\wedge\theta}B_{S}(s,X(s))\,ds.

Combining this with the preceding decomposition of the roots in
S
proves (
(v)
). Finally,

M_{S}(t)=\sum_{j=1}^{N}u_{S,j}M_{j}(t).

Using the diagonal bracket identity from Proposition
11
,

\langle M_{i},M_{j}\rangle(t)=\delta_{ij}\int_{0}^{t}\sigma_{i}^{2}(s,X(s))\,ds,

we obtain

\displaystyle\langle M_{S}\rangle(t)

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=1}^{N}u_{S,j}^{2}\,\langle M_{j}\rangle(t)=\int_{0}^{t}\sum_{j=1}^{N}u_{S,j}^{2}\,\sigma_{j}^{2}(s,X(s))\,ds,

which proves (
8.23
).
∎

The next step is to show that the canonical detector has zero local time at the level zero. This is the analytic input needed for the Tanaka argument in the next subsection. The proof rests on two ingredients: first, the detector bracket is controlled by the singular drifts corresponding to roots in the face; second, individual root walls carry no quadratic-variation mass. This consequence will be proved as the first step in the proof below. Let again

\left(X,M,\bigl(A^{\alpha}\bigr)_{\alpha\in R_{+}}\right)

be the generalized weak limit from Proposition
11
, with the decomposition from Proposition
12
. For later use we also introduce non-canonical detector directions. If
v\in\operatorname{span}S
, set

\tau_{v}(x):=\langle v,x\rangle,\qquad q_{S,v}(t,x):=\sum_{j=1}^{N}v_{j}^{2}\sigma_{j}^{2}(t,x),

and, on localized neighborhoods of
F_{S}
,

B_{S,v}(t,x):=\langle v,b(t,x)\rangle+\sum_{\beta\in R_{+}\setminus S}\frac{k_{\beta}(t,x)\langle v,\beta\rangle}{\langle x,\beta\rangle}.

Thus
q_{S,u_{S}}=q_{S}
and
B_{S,u_{S}}=B_{S}
. Moreover, if
v=u+u_{S}
, with
u\in\mathfrak{D}(S)
, then
B_{S,v}=B_{S,u}+B_{S}
.

Proposition 13

(Vanishing local time of face detectors)
.

Assume
(D1)
. Let
S\in\mathfrak{S}
, and let
T,r,\eta>0
. Let
\theta
be a stopping time such that

\theta\leq T,\qquad\theta<\zeta\quad\text{a.s.},\qquad X(s)\in\mathcal{U}_{S,\eta,r},\quad 0\leq s\leq\theta.

Let

v\in\{u_{S}\}\cup\{u+u_{S}:\ u\in\mathfrak{D}(S)\}.

Then, almost surely,

(8.25)

L_{t\wedge\theta}^{0}\bigl(\tau_{v}(X)\bigr)=0,\qquad 0\leq t\leq T.

In particular, the conclusion holds for the canonical detector
v=u_{S}
.

Proof.

We first record two elementary facts which will be used for the chosen direction
v
. Since
v\in\operatorname{span}S
, there exist constants
c_{\beta}
,
\beta\in S
, such that

v=\sum_{\beta\in S}c_{\beta}\beta.

Hence, by the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality, there exists a finite constant

C_{v}
, depending only on
v
and
S
, such that

(8.26)

q_{S,v}(t,x)\leq C_{v}\sum_{\beta\in S}a_{\beta}(t,x),\qquad(t,x)\in[0,\infty)\times\overline{C_{+}}.

Indeed, writing
v_{j}=\sum_{\beta\in S}c_{\beta}\beta_{j}
, we get

v_{j}^{2}\leq C_{v}\sum_{\beta\in S}\beta_{j}^{2},

and multiplication by
\sigma_{j}^{2}(t,x)
, followed by summation over
j
, gives (
8.26
). Second,
\tau_{v}
is nonnegative on
\overline{C_{+}}
. If
v=u_{S}
, this follows from Lemma
1
. If
v=u+u_{S}
, with
u\in\mathfrak{D}(S)
, then

\tau_{v}=\tau_{u}+\tau_{S},

and both terms are nonnegative on
\overline{C_{+}}
. Moreover, in both cases,

(8.27)

\tau_{v}(x)\geq\tau_{S}(x)\geq\langle x,\beta\rangle,\qquad x\in\overline{C_{+}},\quad\beta\in S.

We next show that, for every
\beta\in R_{+}
,

(8.28)

\int_{0}^{t\wedge\theta}\mathbf{1}_{\{\langle X(s),\beta\rangle=0\}}a_{\beta}(s,X(s))\,ds=0\qquad\text{a.s.}

Set
Z^{\beta}(t):=\langle X(t\wedge\theta),\beta\rangle
. This is a continuous semimartingale whose martingale part has quadratic variation

\int_{0}^{t\wedge\theta}a_{\beta}(s,X(s))\,ds.

The occupation-time formula applied to
Z^{\beta}
gives

\int_{0}^{t\wedge\theta}\mathbf{1}_{\{Z^{\beta}(s)=0\}}a_{\beta}(s,X(s))\,ds=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\mathbf{1}_{\{y=0\}}L_{t}^{y}(Z^{\beta})\,dy=0,

which proves (
8.28
). Let
M_{v}(t):=\langle v,M(t)\rangle
. Using the diagonal bracket identity from Proposition
11
, we have

\langle M_{v}\rangle(t)=\int_{0}^{t}q_{S,v}(s,X(s))\,ds.

Therefore, by (
8.26
),

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t\wedge\theta}\mathbf{1}_{\{\tau_{v}(X(s))>0\}}\frac{d\langle M_{v}\rangle(s)}{\tau_{v}(X(s))}

\displaystyle\leq C_{v}\sum_{\beta\in S}\int_{0}^{t\wedge\theta}\mathbf{1}_{\{\tau_{v}(X(s))>0\}}\frac{a_{\beta}(s,X(s))}{\tau_{v}(X(s))}\,ds.

By (
8.27
), on the set
\{\tau_{v}(X(s))>0,\ \langle X(s),\beta\rangle>0\}
, we have

\frac{1}{\tau_{v}(X(s))}\leq\frac{1}{\langle X(s),\beta\rangle}.

Moreover, by (
8.28
), the measure
a_{\beta}(s,X(s))\,ds
does not charge the set

\{\langle X(s),\beta\rangle=0\}
. Hence

(8.29)

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t\wedge\theta}\mathbf{1}_{\{\tau_{v}(X(s))>0\}}\frac{d\langle M_{v}\rangle(s)}{\tau_{v}(X(s))}

\displaystyle\leq C_{v}\sum_{\beta\in S}\int_{0}^{t\wedge\theta}\frac{a_{\beta}(s,X(s))}{\langle X(s),\beta\rangle}\mathbf{1}_{\{\langle X(s),\beta\rangle>0\}}\,ds.

We claim that the right-hand side is finite almost surely. Since
0\leq s\leq\theta\leq T
and
|X(s)|\leq r
on the localized interval, Assumption
(D1)
gives constants
\varepsilon=\varepsilon(T,r)>0
and
\gamma=\gamma(T,r)>0
such that

0\leq\langle x,\beta\rangle\leq\varepsilon\quad\Longrightarrow\quad a_{\beta}(s,x)\leq\gamma^{-1}k_{\beta}(s,x),

for
0\leq s\leq T
,
|x|\leq r
, and
\beta\in R_{+}
. For fixed
\beta\in S
, split

\int_{0}^{t\wedge\theta}\frac{a_{\beta}(s,X(s))}{\langle X(s),\beta\rangle}\mathbf{1}_{\{\langle X(s),\beta\rangle>0\}}\,ds=I_{\beta}^{\mathrm{small}}+I_{\beta}^{\mathrm{large}},

where
I_{\beta}^{\mathrm{small}}
is the contribution of
0<\langle X(s),\beta\rangle\leq\varepsilon
, and
I_{\beta}^{\mathrm{large}}
is the contribution of
\langle X(s),\beta\rangle>\varepsilon
.

On the small-gap region,

I_{\beta}^{\mathrm{small}}\leq\gamma^{-1}\int_{0}^{t\wedge\theta}\frac{k_{\beta}(s,X(s))}{\langle X(s),\beta\rangle}\mathbf{1}_{\{\langle X(s),\beta\rangle>0\}}\,ds.

Let
\chi_{r+1}:=T\wedge\inf\{u\in[0,\zeta):|X(u)|\geq r+1\}
Since
X(s)\in\mathcal{U}_{S,\eta,r}
for
0\leq s\leq\theta
, we have
t\wedge\theta\leq\chi_{r+1}
. Hence

I_{\beta}^{\mathrm{small}}\leq\gamma^{-1}\int_{0}^{\chi_{r+1}}\frac{k_{\beta}(s,X(s))}{\langle X(s),\beta\rangle}\mathbf{1}_{\{\langle X(s),\beta\rangle>0\}}\,ds<\infty\qquad\text{a.s.}

by Proposition
12
. On the large-gap region, continuity of
\sigma
and compactness give

I_{\beta}^{\mathrm{large}}\leq\frac{T}{\varepsilon}\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}0\leq s\leq T\\
|x|\leq r\end{subarray}}a_{\beta}(s,x)<\infty.

Thus the right-hand side of (
8.29
) is finite almost surely, and consequently

(8.30)

\int_{0}^{t\wedge\theta}\mathbf{1}_{\{\tau_{v}(X(s))>0\}}\frac{d\langle M_{v}\rangle(s)}{\tau_{v}(X(s))}<\infty\qquad\text{a.s.}

Set

Y(s):=\tau_{v}(X(s)),\qquad 0\leq s\leq t\wedge\theta.

Then
Y
is a nonnegative continuous semimartingale and its martingale part
is
M_{v}
. For
n\geq 1
, the occupation-time formula applied to

a\mapsto\mathbf{1}_{\{a>1/n\}}/a
gives

\int_{(0,\infty)}\frac{\mathbf{1}_{\{a>1/n\}}}{a}L_{t\wedge\theta}^{a}(Y)\,da=\int_{0}^{t\wedge\theta}\mathbf{1}_{\{Y(s)>1/n\}}\frac{d\langle M_{v}\rangle(s)}{Y(s)}.

Letting
n\to\infty
and using monotone convergence yields

\int_{(0,\infty)}\frac{L_{t\wedge\theta}^{a}(Y)}{a}\,da=\int_{0}^{t\wedge\theta}\mathbf{1}_{\{Y(s)>0\}}\frac{d\langle M_{v}\rangle(s)}{Y(s)}<\infty\qquad\text{a.s.}

By right-continuity of
a\mapsto L_{t\wedge\theta}^{a}(Y)
at
a=0
, this
forces

L_{t\wedge\theta}^{0}(Y)=0.

Since
Y=\tau_{v}(X)
, this proves (
8.25
)
for fixed
t
. Applying the argument to rational
t\in[0,T]
and using
monotonicity in
t
, we obtain the assertion simultaneously for all

0\leq t\leq T
on one probability-one event.
∎

8.4.
Elimination of hidden boundary measures

We now use the canonical face detectors to eliminate the boundary measures
d\Lambda^{\alpha}
. The local detector argument on a fixed face combines the vanishing local time from Proposition
13
with Tanaka’s formula. It shows that
d\Lambda^{\beta}
gives no mass to the portion of the path lying in that face. Since every boundary point belongs to one of the relatively open faces
F_{S}
, a covering argument then yields the global conclusion.

Proposition 14

(Elimination and zero occupation on one face)
.

Assume
(D1)
and
(D2)
. Let
S\in\mathfrak{S}
, and let

T,r,\eta>0
. Let
\theta
be a stopping time such that

\theta\leq T,\qquad\theta<\zeta\quad\text{a.s.},\qquad X(s)\in\mathcal{U}_{S,\eta,r},\quad 0\leq s\leq\theta.

Then, almost surely, for every
\beta\in S
and every
t\geq 0
,

(8.31)

\int_{0}^{t\wedge\theta}\mathbf{1}_{\{\tau_{S}(X(s))=0\}}\,d\Lambda^{\beta}(s)=0.

If, in addition, Assumption
(D3)
holds, then, almost surely,
for every
t\geq 0
,

(8.32)

\int_{0}^{t\wedge\theta}\mathbf{1}_{\{\tau_{S}(X(s))=0\}}\,ds=0.

Proof.

We first prove (
8.31
). Set

Y(t):=\tau_{S}(X(t\wedge\theta)).

By Lemma
1
,
Y
is a nonnegative continuous
semimartingale. Its martingale part is
M_{S}(\cdot\wedge\theta)
, and its
finite-variation part is

\displaystyle V(t)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{t\wedge\theta}B_{S}(s,X(s))\,ds

\displaystyle\quad+\sum_{\beta\in S}\langle u_{S},\beta\rangle\int_{0}^{t\wedge\theta}\frac{k_{\beta}(s,X(s))}{\langle X(s),\beta\rangle}\mathbf{1}_{\{\langle X(s),\beta\rangle>0\}}\,ds

\displaystyle\quad+\sum_{\beta\in S}\langle u_{S},\beta\rangle\Lambda^{\beta}(t\wedge\theta).

By Proposition
13
, applied with
v=u_{S}
,

L_{t}^{0}(Y)=0,\qquad t\geq 0.

Since
Y\geq 0
, Tanaka’s formula gives

Y(t)=Y(0)+\int_{0}^{t}\mathbf{1}_{\{Y(s)>0\}}\,dY(s)+\frac{1}{2}L_{t}^{0}(Y).

Subtracting this from
Y(t)=Y(0)+\int_{0}^{t}dY(s)
, and using

L_{t}^{0}(Y)=0
, we get

\int_{0}^{t}\mathbf{1}_{\{Y(s)=0\}}\,dY(s)=0.

Writing
Y=Y(0)+N+V
, where
N=M_{S}(\cdot\wedge\theta)
, gives

\int_{0}^{t}\mathbf{1}_{\{Y(s)=0\}}\,dN(s)+\int_{0}^{t}\mathbf{1}_{\{Y(s)=0\}}\,dV(s)=0.

The first term is a continuous local martingale with quadratic variation

\int_{0}^{t}\mathbf{1}_{\{Y(s)=0\}}\,d\langle N\rangle(s).

By the occupation-time formula this quadratic variation is zero. Hence the
martingale term is identically zero, and therefore

\int_{0}^{t}\mathbf{1}_{\{Y(s)=0\}}\,dV(s)=0.

Since
dV
is supported on
[0,\theta]
, we obtain

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{t\wedge\theta}\mathbf{1}_{\{\tau_{S}(X(s))=0\}}B_{S}(s,X(s))\,ds

\displaystyle\quad+\sum_{\beta\in S}\langle u_{S},\beta\rangle\int_{0}^{t\wedge\theta}\mathbf{1}_{\{\tau_{S}(X(s))=0\}}\frac{k_{\beta}(s,X(s))}{\langle X(s),\beta\rangle}\mathbf{1}_{\{\langle X(s),\beta\rangle>0\}}\,ds

(8.33)

\displaystyle\quad+\sum_{\beta\in S}\langle u_{S},\beta\rangle\int_{0}^{t\wedge\theta}\mathbf{1}_{\{\tau_{S}(X(s))=0\}}\,d\Lambda^{\beta}(s).

On the set
\{\tau_{S}(X(s))=0\}
, since the path is localized in

\mathcal{U}_{S,\eta,r}
, Lemma
1
(i)
implies
X(s)\in F_{S}
. Hence

\langle X(s),\beta\rangle=0,\qquad\beta\in S,

and the interior singular terms in
(
8.4
) vanish. Therefore

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{t\wedge\theta}\mathbf{1}_{\{\tau_{S}(X(s))=0\}}B_{S}(s,X(s))\,ds

(8.34)

\displaystyle\quad+\sum_{\beta\in S}\langle u_{S},\beta\rangle\int_{0}^{t\wedge\theta}\mathbf{1}_{\{\tau_{S}(X(s))=0\}}\,d\Lambda^{\beta}(s).

By Assumption
(D2)
,

B_{S}(s,X(s))\geq 0\qquad\text{on }\{\tau_{S}(X(s))=0\},

because this set lies in
F_{S}
. The measures
d\Lambda^{\beta}
are
nonnegative, and

\langle u_{S},\beta\rangle>0,\qquad\beta\in S,

by Lemma
1
(iii). Thus all terms in
(
8.4
) are nonnegative. Since their sum is
zero, each term is zero. This proves (
8.31
).

We now assume additionally
(D3)
and prove
(
8.32
). Define

Q_{S}(t,x):=\max_{u\in\mathfrak{D}(S)}q_{S,u}(t,x),\qquad H_{S}(t,x):=\max_{u\in\mathfrak{D}(S)}B_{S,u}(t,x).

On
F_{S}
, Assumption
(D3)
says that

Q_{S}(t,x)=0\quad\Longrightarrow\quad H_{S}(t,x)>0.

First we show that the face has zero occupation on the set where

Q_{S}>0
. Fix
u\in\mathfrak{D}(S)
. The process

\tau_{u}(X(\cdot\wedge\theta))
is a continuous semimartingale whose
martingale bracket density is
q_{S,u}(s,X(s))
. The occupation-time formula
gives

\int_{0}^{t\wedge\theta}\mathbf{1}_{\{\tau_{u}(X(s))=0\}}q_{S,u}(s,X(s))\,ds=0.

Since
X(s)\in F_{S}
implies
\tau_{u}(X(s))=0
, and since on

\mathcal{U}_{S,\eta,r}
the condition
X(s)\in F_{S}
is equivalent to

\tau_{S}(X(s))=0
, we get

\int_{0}^{t\wedge\theta}\mathbf{1}_{\{\tau_{S}(X(s))=0\}}q_{S,u}(s,X(s))\,ds=0.

Therefore, for every
m\in\mathbb{N}
,

\int_{0}^{t\wedge\theta}\mathbf{1}_{\{\tau_{S}(X(s))=0\}}\mathbf{1}_{\{q_{S,u}(s,X(s))\geq 1/m\}}\,ds=0.

Since
\mathfrak{D}(S)
is finite, it follows that

(8.35)

\int_{0}^{t\wedge\theta}\mathbf{1}_{\{\tau_{S}(X(s))=0\}}\mathbf{1}_{\{Q_{S}(s,X(s))>0\}}\,ds=0.

It remains to treat the fully degenerate part of the face:

\{\tau_{S}(X(s))=0,\ Q_{S}(s,X(s))=0\}.

By Assumption
(D3)
, this set is contained in

\bigcup_{u\in\mathfrak{D}(S)}\{\tau_{S}(X(s))=0,\ B_{S,u}(s,X(s))>0\}.

Since
\mathfrak{D}(S)
is finite, it suffices to prove that for every

u\in\mathfrak{D}(S)
,

(8.36)

\int_{0}^{t\wedge\theta}\mathbf{1}_{\{\tau_{S}(X(s))=0\}}\mathbf{1}_{\{B_{S,u}(s,X(s))>0\}}\,ds=0.

Fix
u\in\mathfrak{D}(S)
, and set

v:=u+u_{S}.

Then

\tau_{v}=\tau_{u}+\tau_{S}.

Since both
\tau_{u}
and
\tau_{S}
are nonnegative on
\overline{C_{+}}
, and since

\tau_{S}(X(s))=0
is equivalent to
X(s)\in F_{S}
on the localized interval,
we have

\tau_{v}(X(s))=0\qquad\Longleftrightarrow\qquad\tau_{S}(X(s))=0,\qquad 0\leq s\leq\theta.

By Proposition
13
, applied to this
v
,

L_{t}^{0}\bigl(\tau_{v}(X(\cdot\wedge\theta))\bigr)=0.

Repeating the Tanaka argument above for

Y_{v}(t):=\tau_{v}(X(t\wedge\theta)),

we obtain

(8.37)

\int_{0}^{t\wedge\theta}\mathbf{1}_{\{\tau_{S}(X(s))=0\}}B_{S,v}(s,X(s))\,ds+\sum_{\beta\in S}\langle v,\beta\rangle\int_{0}^{t\wedge\theta}\mathbf{1}_{\{\tau_{S}(X(s))=0\}}\,d\Lambda^{\beta}(s)=0.

Indeed, on the set
\{\tau_{S}(X(s))=0\}
, the interior singular terms with

\beta\in S
vanish because

\mathbf{1}_{\{\langle X(s),\beta\rangle>0\}}=0
.

Now

B_{S,v}=B_{S,u}+B_{S}.

Both
u
and
u_{S}
belong to
\mathfrak{D}(S)
, and therefore
Assumption
(D2)
gives

B_{S,u}\geq 0,\qquad B_{S}\geq 0,\qquad\text{on }F_{S}.

Thus

B_{S,v}\geq B_{S,u}\geq 0\qquad\text{on }F_{S}.

Also, for
\beta\in S
,

\langle v,\beta\rangle=\langle u,\beta\rangle+\langle u_{S},\beta\rangle>0,

because
\langle u,\beta\rangle\geq 0
by admissibility and

\langle u_{S},\beta\rangle>0
by Lemma
1
.
Therefore all terms in (
8.37
) are nonnegative. Since
their sum is zero,

\int_{0}^{t\wedge\theta}\mathbf{1}_{\{\tau_{S}(X(s))=0\}}B_{S,v}(s,X(s))\,ds=0.

As
B_{S,v}\geq B_{S,u}\geq 0
on the face, we get

\int_{0}^{t\wedge\theta}\mathbf{1}_{\{\tau_{S}(X(s))=0\}}B_{S,u}(s,X(s))\,ds=0.

Consequently, for every
m\in\mathbb{N}
,

\frac{1}{m}\int_{0}^{t\wedge\theta}\mathbf{1}_{\{\tau_{S}(X(s))=0\}}\mathbf{1}_{\{B_{S,u}(s,X(s))\geq 1/m\}}\,ds\leq 0.

Letting
m
range over
\mathbb{N}
, we obtain
(
8.36
).

Combining (
8.35
) with the preceding treatment
of the fully degenerate part yields

\int_{0}^{t\wedge\theta}\mathbf{1}_{\{\tau_{S}(X(s))=0\}}\,ds=0.

This proves (
8.32
) for fixed
t
. Applying the
argument to rational
t
and using monotonicity gives the statement
simultaneously for all
t\geq 0
on one probability-one event.
∎

The same conclusions holds on any deterministic interval
[a,b]\subset[0,\zeta)
on which
X(s)\in\mathcal{U}_{S,\eta,r}
. Indeed, the proof above is local in time and applies verbatim to the stopped shifted semimartingale
s\mapsto X((a+s)\wedge b)
.

We can now eliminate the hidden boundary measures globally and complete the proof of the degenerate existence theorem.

Proof of Theorem
3
.

Let

\left(X,M,\bigl(A^{\alpha}\bigr)_{\alpha\in R_{+}}\right)

be the generalized weak limit constructed in
Proposition
11
, and let

(\Lambda^{\alpha})_{\alpha\in R_{+}}
be the continuous increasing processes
from Proposition
12
. Thus, for every

\alpha\in R_{+}
,

A^{\alpha}(t)=\int_{0}^{t}\frac{k_{\alpha}(s,X(s))}{\langle X(s),\alpha\rangle}\mathbf{1}_{\{\langle X(s),\alpha\rangle>0\}}\,ds+\Lambda^{\alpha}(t),\qquad t<\zeta,

and the Stieltjes measure
d\Lambda^{\alpha}
is supported on the wall

Z_{\alpha}:=\{t\in[0,\zeta):\langle X(t),\alpha\rangle=0\}.

It remains to prove that

\Lambda^{\alpha}(t)=0,\qquad\alpha\in R_{+},\quad 0\leq t<\zeta.

Fix
\alpha\in R_{+}
. For every
S\in\mathfrak{S}
such that

\alpha\in S
, set

E_{S}:=\{t\in[0,\zeta):X(t)\in F_{S}\}.

Then

Z_{\alpha}=\bigcup_{\begin{subarray}{c}S\in\mathfrak{S}\\
\alpha\in S\end{subarray}}E_{S}.

Indeed, if
\langle X(t),\alpha\rangle=0
, then

S(X(t))\in\mathfrak{S}
,
\alpha\in S(X(t))
, and

X(t)\in F_{S(X(t))}
. Since
\mathfrak{S}
is finite, it is enough to show
that

d\Lambda^{\alpha}(E_{S})=0

for every
S\in\mathfrak{S}
with
\alpha\in S
.

Fix such an
S
, and let
t_{0}\in E_{S}
. Then
X(t_{0})\in F_{S}
. Since the
roots in
R_{+}\setminus S
are strictly positive at
X(t_{0})
, continuity of

X
implies that there exist
r,\eta>0
and an open interval

I\subset[0,\zeta)
containing
t_{0}
such that

X(s)\in\mathcal{U}_{S,\eta,r},\qquad s\in I.

Choose rational numbers
a<b
such that

t_{0}\in(a,b)\subset I.

After enlarging
r
and shrinking
\eta
, we may take
r,\eta
rational.
On
[a,b]
, the path stays in
\mathcal{U}_{S,\eta,r}
. By the interval
version of Proposition
14
, applied to the hidden-measure
conclusion (
8.31
),

\int_{a}^{b}\mathbf{1}_{\{\tau_{S}(X(s))=0\}}\,d\Lambda^{\alpha}(s)=0.

Since
X(s)\in\mathcal{U}_{S,\eta,r}
on
[a,b]
,
Lemma
1
(i) gives

\tau_{S}(X(s))=0\qquad\Longleftrightarrow\qquad X(s)\in F_{S}.

Therefore

\int_{a}^{b}\mathbf{1}_{E_{S}}(s)\,d\Lambda^{\alpha}(s)=0.

The countable family of rational intervals
(a,b)
and rational parameters

(r,\eta)
for which
X(s)\in\mathcal{U}_{S,\eta,r}
on
[a,b]
covers

E_{S}
. Hence

d\Lambda^{\alpha}(E_{S})=0.

Since this holds for every
S\in\mathfrak{S}
with
\alpha\in S
, we obtain

d\Lambda^{\alpha}(Z_{\alpha})=0.

But
d\Lambda^{\alpha}
is supported on
Z_{\alpha}
. Hence

d\Lambda^{\alpha}=0\qquad\text{on }[0,\zeta),

and therefore

\Lambda^{\alpha}(t)=0,\qquad 0\leq t<\zeta.

Thus, for every
\alpha\in R_{+}
,

A^{\alpha}(t)=\int_{0}^{t}\frac{k_{\alpha}(s,X(s))}{\langle X(s),\alpha\rangle}\mathbf{1}_{\{\langle X(s),\alpha\rangle>0\}}\,ds,\qquad t<\zeta.

Substituting this identity into the generalized weak equation from
Proposition
11
, we get

X(t)=x_{0}+M(t)+\int_{0}^{t}b(s,X(s))\,ds+\sum_{\alpha\in R_{+}}\alpha\int_{0}^{t}\frac{k_{\alpha}(s,X(s))}{\langle X(s),\alpha\rangle}\mathbf{1}_{\{\langle X(s),\alpha\rangle>0\}}\,ds,\qquad t<\zeta.

By Proposition
11
, possibly after enlarging the
probability space, there exists an
N
-dimensional Brownian motion
B
such
that

M(t)=\int_{0}^{t}\sigma(s,X(s))\,dB(s),\qquad t<\zeta.

Therefore
X
satisfies (
2.9
). Finally,
the singular integrals are finite on compact subintervals of
[0,\zeta)
by
Proposition
12
. This completes the
proof.
∎

Proof of Theorem
4
.

By Theorem
3
, there exist a filtered probability
space, an
N
-dimensional Brownian motion
B
, and a continuous

\overline{C_{+}}
-valued semimartingale
X
, defined up to its lifetime

\zeta:=\zeta^{X}>0
, such that
X(0)=x_{0}
and

X(t)=x_{0}+\int_{0}^{t}\sigma(s,X(s))\,dB(s)+\int_{0}^{t}b(s,X(s))\,ds+\sum_{\alpha\in R_{+}}\alpha\int_{0}^{t}\frac{k_{\alpha}(s,X(s))}{\langle X(s),\alpha\rangle}\mathbf{1}_{\{\langle X(s),\alpha\rangle>0\}}\,ds

for every
t<\zeta
. Moreover, all singular integrals in this indicator
formulation are finite on compact subintervals of
[0,\zeta)
.

It remains to prove that
X
spends zero Lebesgue time on every root wall.
First we prove the corresponding statement for each relatively open face

F_{S}
. Fix
S\in\mathfrak{S}
. We claim that

(8.38)

\int_{0}^{t}\mathbf{1}_{\{X(s)\in F_{S}\}}\,ds=0,\qquad t<\zeta,\qquad\text{a.s.}

Let
t_{0}<\zeta
be such that
X(t_{0})\in F_{S}
. Since the roots in

R_{+}\setminus S
are strictly positive at
X(t_{0})
, continuity of
X

implies that there exist
r,\eta>0
and an open interval

I\subset[0,\zeta)
containing
t_{0}
such that

X(s)\in\mathcal{U}_{S,\eta,r},\qquad s\in I.

Choose rational numbers
a<b
such that

t_{0}\in(a,b)\subset I.

After enlarging
r
and shrinking
\eta
, we may assume that
r,\eta
are
rational. On
[a,b]
, the path stays in
\mathcal{U}_{S,\eta,r}
. By the
interval version of Proposition
14
, applied to the
zero-occupation conclusion (
8.32
),

\int_{a}^{b}\mathbf{1}_{\{\tau_{S}(X(s))=0\}}\,ds=0.

Since
X(s)\in\mathcal{U}_{S,\eta,r}
on
[a,b]
,
Lemma
1
(i) gives

\tau_{S}(X(s))=0\qquad\Longleftrightarrow\qquad X(s)\in F_{S}.

Thus

\int_{a}^{b}\mathbf{1}_{\{X(s)\in F_{S}\}}\,ds=0.

The countable family of such rational intervals covers

\{s<\zeta:X(s)\in F_{S}\}.

Therefore (
8.38
) holds.

Now fix
\alpha\in R_{+}
. The root wall inside the closed chamber is the finite
union of relatively open faces:

\{x\in\overline{C_{+}}:\langle x,\alpha\rangle=0\}=\bigcup_{\begin{subarray}{c}S\in\mathfrak{S}\\
\alpha\in S\end{subarray}}F_{S}.

Using (
8.38
) and the finiteness of

\mathfrak{S}
, we obtain

(8.39)

\int_{0}^{t}\mathbf{1}_{\{\langle X(s),\alpha\rangle=0\}}\,ds=0,\qquad\alpha\in R_{+},\quad t<\zeta,\qquad\text{a.s.}

Hence
X
is non-sticky in the sense of (
2.22
).

It remains only to remove the indicators from the singular drift. By
(
8.39
), for every
\alpha\in R_{+}
and
every
t<\zeta
,

\int_{0}^{t}\frac{k_{\alpha}(s,X(s))}{\langle X(s),\alpha\rangle}\,ds=\int_{0}^{t}\frac{k_{\alpha}(s,X(s))}{\langle X(s),\alpha\rangle}\mathbf{1}_{\{\langle X(s),\alpha\rangle>0\}}\,ds,

where the value of the quotient on the wall is immaterial, because the wall is
visited only on a Lebesgue-null set of times. The right-hand side is finite on
compact subintervals of
[0,\zeta)
by
Theorem
3
. Therefore the singular integrals in
the equation without indicators are well defined and locally finite.

Substituting the preceding identity into the indicator equation gives

X(t)=x_{0}+\int_{0}^{t}\sigma(s,X(s))\,dB(s)+\int_{0}^{t}b(s,X(s))\,ds+\sum_{\alpha\in R_{+}}\alpha\int_{0}^{t}\frac{k_{\alpha}(s,X(s))}{\langle X(s),\alpha\rangle}\,ds,\qquad t<\zeta.

Thus
X
satisfies (
2.11
). The
zero-occupation property (
8.39
) is exactly
the asserted non-sticking boundary behavior. This completes the proof.
∎

9.
Uniqueness

This section proves Theorems
6
and
7
. Throughout the section, non-sticky solutions are understood in the sense of (
2.22
). We use the convention

\displaystyle\operatorname{sgn}(u)

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}1,&u>0,\\
0,&u=0,\\
-1,&u<0.\end{cases}

For
\alpha\in R_{+}
and
x\in\overline{C_{+}}
, define

\displaystyle q_{\alpha}(t,x)

\displaystyle:=\begin{cases}\dfrac{k_{\alpha}(t,x)}{\langle x,\alpha\rangle},&\langle x,\alpha\rangle>0,\\
\\[5.16663pt]
0,&\langle x,\alpha\rangle=0.\end{cases}

We then define the singular force on
\overline{C_{+}}
by

\displaystyle G(t,x)

\displaystyle:=\sum_{\alpha\in R_{+}}\alpha\,q_{\alpha}(t,x).

For non-sticky solutions this agrees, as a Lebesgue-time integrand, with the singular force in (
1.2
).

Proof of Theorem
6
.

Let
(X,B)
and
(\widetilde{X},B)
be two non-sticky solutions as in the statement,
driven by the same Brownian motion and with the same initial condition. Put
