Z(t):=X(t)-\widetilde{X}(t)
. Fix
T,r>0
and define

\displaystyle\tau_{r}

\displaystyle:=T\wedge\zeta^{X}\wedge\zeta^{\widetilde{X}}\wedge\inf\left\{t\geq 0:\ |X(t)|\vee|\widetilde{X}(t)|\geq r\right\}.

We also localize the finite-variation terms. Set

\displaystyle A^{r}(t)

\displaystyle:=\int_{0}^{t\wedge\tau_{r}}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\bigl(|b_{i}(s,X(s))|+|b_{i}(s,\widetilde{X}(s))|\bigr)\,ds

\displaystyle\quad+\sum_{\alpha\in R_{+}}\int_{0}^{t\wedge\tau_{r}}\bigl(|q_{\alpha}(s,X(s))|+|q_{\alpha}(s,\widetilde{X}(s))|\bigr)\,ds,

and, for
m\in\mathbb{N}
,
\tau_{r,m}=\tau_{r}\wedge\inf\left\{t\geq 0:\ A^{r}(t)\geq m\right\}
. The singular integrals are finite on compact subintervals of the lifetimes, and therefore
\tau_{r,m}\uparrow\tau_{r}
, as
m\to\infty
. If some singular integral is interpreted improperly at the initial time, the arguments below are first applied on
[\varepsilon,t\wedge\tau_{r,m}]
and then
\varepsilon\downarrow 0
.

We prove the estimate separately in the two uniqueness regimes.

Step 1: the Yamada-Watanabe uniqueness setting.

Assume that alternative (a) in Assumption
(U1)
and Assumption
(U2)
hold. On
[0,\tau_{r}]
, for every
i=1,\ldots,N
,

\displaystyle d\langle Z_{i}\rangle_{t}

\displaystyle=|\sigma_{i}(t,X(t))-\sigma_{i}(t,\widetilde{X}(t))|^{2}\,dt\leq\rho_{T,r}(|Z_{i}(t)|)\,dt.

By the one-dimensional Yamada-Watanabe local-time criterion,

(9.1)

\displaystyle L_{t\wedge\tau_{r,m}}^{0}(Z_{i})

\displaystyle=0,\qquad 0\leq t\leq T,\qquad i=1,\ldots,N.

Applying Tanaka’s formula to
Z_{i}
, summing over
i
, using (
9.1
), and taking expectations, we obtain

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}\sum_{i=1}^{N}|Z_{i}(t\wedge\tau_{r,m})|

\displaystyle=\mathbf{E}\int_{0}^{t\wedge\tau_{r,m}}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\operatorname{sgn}(Z_{i}(s))\bigl(b_{i}(s,X(s))-b_{i}(s,\widetilde{X}(s))\bigr)\,ds

(9.2)

\displaystyle\quad+\mathbf{E}\int_{0}^{t\wedge\tau_{r,m}}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\operatorname{sgn}(Z_{i}(s))\bigl(G_{i}(s,X(s))-G_{i}(s,\widetilde{X}(s))\bigr)\,ds.

The stochastic integral has expectation zero because the martingale integrand is bounded after stopping at
\tau_{r}
. By Assumption
(U2)
, the first integral in (
9.2
) is bounded above by

(9.3)

\displaystyle L_{T,r}\int_{0}^{t}\mathbf{E}\sum_{i=1}^{N}|Z_{i}(s\wedge\tau_{r,m})|\,ds.

We next show that the singular contribution is non-positive. Since both processes
satisfy (
2.22
) and
R_{+}
is finite, for Lebesgue-a.e.
s
before

\tau_{r,m}
both
X(s)
and
\widetilde{X}(s)
belong to
C_{+}
. At such times the
indicators in the singular terms are equal to one, and we may write the singular
drift as

G(s,x)=\sum_{\alpha\in R_{+}}\alpha\,\frac{k_{\alpha}(s,x)}{\langle x,\alpha\rangle}.

Therefore, by Assumption
(U3)
, applied with

x=X(s)
and
y=\widetilde{X}(s)
,

(9.4)

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{N}\operatorname{sgn}(Z_{i}(s))\bigl(G_{i}(s,X(s))-G_{i}(s,\widetilde{X}(s))\bigr)\leq 0.

Thus the second integral in (
9.2
) is non-positive. Combining this with (
9.3
), we obtain

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}\sum_{i=1}^{N}|Z_{i}(t\wedge\tau_{r,m})|

\displaystyle\leq L_{T,r}\int_{0}^{t}\mathbf{E}\sum_{i=1}^{N}|Z_{i}(s\wedge\tau_{r,m})|\,ds.

Gronwall’s lemma gives

(9.5)

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}\sum_{i=1}^{N}|Z_{i}(t\wedge\tau_{r,m})|

\displaystyle=0,\qquad 0\leq t\leq T.

Step 2: the Lipschitz uniqueness setting.

Assume now that alternative (b) in Assumption
(U1)
holds, together with Assumptions
(U2’)
and
(U3’)
. Itô’s formula gives

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}|Z(t\wedge\tau_{r,m})|^{2}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{E}\int_{0}^{t\wedge\tau_{r,m}}\sum_{i=1}^{N}|\sigma_{i}(s,X(s))-\sigma_{i}(s,\widetilde{X}(s))|^{2}\,ds

\displaystyle\quad+2\mathbf{E}\int_{0}^{t\wedge\tau_{r,m}}\left<Z(s),b(s,X(s))-b(s,\widetilde{X}(s))\right>\,ds

\displaystyle\quad+2\mathbf{E}\int_{0}^{t\wedge\tau_{r,m}}\left<Z(s),G(s,X(s))-G(s,\widetilde{X}(s))\right>\,ds.

The stochastic integral has expectation zero because the martingale integrand is bounded after stopping at
\tau_{r}
. For Lebesgue-a.e.
s
before
\tau_{r,m}
, both
X(s)
and
\widetilde{X}(s)
belong to
C_{+}
, because both solutions satisfy (
2.22
) and
R_{+}
is finite. Hence Assumption
(U3’)
gives

\left<Z(s),G(s,X(s))-G(s,\widetilde{X}(s))\right>\leq 0.

The local Lipschitz condition on
\sigma
and Assumption
(U2’)
therefore imply that, for a finite constant
C_{T,r}
,

(9.6)

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}|Z(t\wedge\tau_{r,m})|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq C_{T,r}\int_{0}^{t}\mathbf{E}|Z(s\wedge\tau_{r,m})|^{2}\,ds.

By Gronwall’s lemma,

(9.7)

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}|Z(t\wedge\tau_{r,m})|^{2}

\displaystyle=0,\qquad 0\leq t\leq T.

In either uniqueness setting, (
9.5
) or (
9.7
)
implies that

(9.8)

\displaystyle X(t\wedge\tau_{r,m})

\displaystyle=\widetilde{X}(t\wedge\tau_{r,m})\qquad\text{a.s.}

for every fixed
t\in[0,T]
. Intersecting the corresponding full-probability
events over rational
t\in[0,T]
and using continuity of the paths, the equality
holds simultaneously for all
t\in[0,T]
.

Letting
m\to\infty
gives equality up to
\tau_{r}
. Letting
r\to\infty
gives

(9.9)

\displaystyle X(t)

\displaystyle=\widetilde{X}(t),\qquad 0\leq t<T\wedge\zeta^{X}\wedge\zeta^{\widetilde{X}},\qquad\text{a.s.}

Since
T>0
is arbitrary, (
2.23
) follows.

If the lifetimes are explosion lifetimes, equality of the paths before the minimum
lifetime implies equality of all compact exit times, and therefore

\zeta^{X}=\zeta^{\widetilde{X}}
a.s. If a common deterministic lifetime is
prescribed, the equality of lifetimes is immediate.
∎

Remark 7
.

Theorem
6
does not assert that a solution exists. It is an implication: whenever two non-sticky solutions of (
2.9
) exist on the same filtered probability space, are driven by the same Brownian motion, and have the same initial condition, they coincide up to their common lifetime. Since non-sticky solutions satisfy (
2.22
), they are equivalently solutions of (
1.2
), with the singular force interpreted through its interior values. The theorem is therefore not a uniqueness statement for the broader indicator equation without the non-sticky restriction.

Remark 8

(Relation with condition (A1) from
[
22
]
)
.

Let us spell out what Assumption
(U3)
gives in type
A_{N-1}
. For
\alpha=e_{i}-e_{j}
, with
i<j
, the corresponding scalar force is

\displaystyle\frac{k_{e_{i}-e_{j}}(t,x)}{x_{i}-x_{j}},

where, in our chamber convention,
x_{i}>x_{j}
. Suppose that this coefficient depends only on the two coordinates involved, namely

\displaystyle k_{e_{i}-e_{j}}(t,x)

\displaystyle=H_{ij}(t,x_{i},x_{j}).

Then Assumption
(U3)
says that the force

\displaystyle\frac{H_{ij}(t,u,v)}{u-v},\qquad u>v,

is non-increasing as the gap
u-v
increases. Equivalently, in the chamber convention used here, for
w<x<y<z
, one obtains

(9.10)

\displaystyle H_{ij}(t,z,w)(y-x)

\displaystyle\leq H_{ij}(t,y,x)(z-w).

Indeed, the pair
(z,w)
has the larger gap
z-w
, while the pair
(y,x)
has the smaller gap
y-x
. If one rewrites the same statement in the increasing-order convention used in the ordered-particle formulation of
[
22
]
, then (
9.10
) becomes

\displaystyle H_{ij}(t,w,z)(y-x)

\displaystyle\leq H_{ij}(t,x,y)(z-w),\qquad w<x<y<z,

which is precisely condition (A1) from that setting.

Proof of Theorem
7
.

Under existence alternative
(S1)
, weak existence of a solution of (
1.2
) follows from Theorem
1
. Moreover, the singular integrals are finite on compact subintervals of the lifetime. Since

k_{\alpha}
is strictly positive on the corresponding wall, this finiteness implies the zero-occupation property (
2.22
). Thus the weak solution belongs to the non-sticky class.

Under existence alternative
(S2)
, weak existence of a solution of (
1.2
) in the non-sticky class follows from Theorem
4
. In that case the indicator formulation is first used to construct a weak solution, and the non-sticking conclusion established in Theorem
4
removes the indicator from the singular drift.

Pathwise uniqueness in the non-sticky class follows from Theorem
6
. It remains to pass from weak existence and pathwise uniqueness to strong existence. We use the Yamada-Watanabe principle in localized form. For
r>0
define the exit time

\displaystyle\tau_{r}^{X}

\displaystyle:=\inf\left\{t\geq 0:\ |X(t)|\geq r\right\}.

Weak existence and pathwise uniqueness hold for the equation stopped at
\tau_{r}^{X}
. Hence, by the Yamada-Watanabe principle, the stopped equation admits a strong solution and has uniqueness in law. These stopped strong solutions are consistent as
r
varies. Since, for the explosion lifetime,

\displaystyle\zeta^{X}

\displaystyle=\lim_{r\to\infty}\tau_{r}^{X},

letting
r\to\infty
gives a strong solution up to its lifetime, adapted to the completed filtration generated by the initial condition and the Brownian motion. The same localization argument gives uniqueness in law for weak solutions of (
1.2
) in the non-sticky class with initial law
\nu
.
∎

10.
Mean-field convergence theorem

We use the notation of Section
2.6
. We additionally define, for the short-root contribution in type
B
, the following

\displaystyle H_{f}(t,x)

\displaystyle:=\begin{cases}k(t,x)\dfrac{f^{\prime}(x)}{x},&x>0,\\[5.16663pt]
k(t,0)f^{\prime\prime}(0),&x=0.\end{cases}

In types
A_{N-1}
and
D_{N}
we put
H_{f}\equiv 0
. Moreover, in the
A_{N-1}
and
B_{N}
cases we work directly with the empirical measure
\mu_{t}^{(N)}
defined there.
In the
D_{N}
case, it is convenient in the proof to introduce the auxiliary empirical
measure

\displaystyle\widehat{\mu}_{t}^{(N)}

\displaystyle:=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\delta_{|X_{i}^{(N)}(t)|}.

This auxiliary measure differs from the empirical measure used in the statement only
by a negligible term. Indeed, for every bounded test function
f
,

\displaystyle\sup_{0\leq t\leq T}\left|\left\langle\widehat{\mu}_{t}^{(N)},f\right\rangle-0\left\langle\mu_{t}^{(N)},f\right\rangle\right|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2\|f\|_{\infty}}{N}.

Moreover, since
|X_{N}^{(N)}(t)|\leq X_{N-1}^{(N)}(t)
in the type
D_{N}
chamber, the
moment assumption for
\mu_{0}^{(N)}
implies the corresponding moment bound for

\widehat{\mu}_{0}^{(N)}
. Hence tightness and convergence for

\widehat{\mu}^{(N)}
imply the same conclusions for
\mu^{(N)}
. Thus, throughout the proof we may write

\displaystyle Y_{i}^{(N)}(t)

\displaystyle:=\begin{cases}X_{i}^{(N)}(t),&\mathfrak{r}=A,B,\\
|X_{i}^{(N)}(t)|,&\mathfrak{r}=D,\end{cases}

and then, in all three cases,

\displaystyle\mu_{t}^{(N)}=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\delta_{Y_{i}^{(N)}(t)}.

For deterministic chamber points
x
, we similarly write
y_{i}=x_{i}
in types
A
and

B
, and
y_{i}=|x_{i}|
in type
D
.

In root-wise notation, the classical mean-field scaling is

\displaystyle k_{e_{i}-e_{j}}^{(N)}(t,x)

\displaystyle:=\frac{1}{N}k(t,y_{i},y_{j}),\qquad 1\leq i<j\leq N,

\displaystyle k_{e_{i}+e_{j}}^{(N)}(t,x)

\displaystyle:=\frac{1}{N}k(t,y_{i},y_{j}),\qquad 1\leq i<j\leq N,\qquad\mathfrak{r}=B,D,

\displaystyle k_{e_{i}}^{(N)}(t,x)

\displaystyle:=k(t,y_{i}),\qquad i=1,\ldots,N,\qquad\mathfrak{r}=B.

where
y_{i}=x_{i}
in types
A
and
B
, and
y_{i}=|x_{i}|
in type
D
.

Proof of Theorem
8
.

Fix
T>0
. We first prove the uniform moment estimate. Let

p\in\{2,4,8\}
and put

(10.1)

\displaystyle m_{p}^{(N)}(t)

\displaystyle:=\left\langle\mu_{t}^{(N)},|x|^{p}\right\rangle.

Applying Itô’s formula to
|x|^{p}
in type
A_{N-1}
, to
x^{p}
in type
B_{N}
, and to

|x|^{p}
in type
D_{N}
, localizing the singular drift if necessary, and then using the
symmetry of the pair kernel
k
, gives

(10.2)

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}m_{p}^{(N)}(t)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbf{E}m_{p}^{(N)}(0)+C_{p,T}\int_{0}^{t}\left(1+\mathbf{E}m_{p}^{(N)}(s)\right)\,ds.

Indeed, the one-particle drift and martingale terms are controlled by the growth
assumptions in
(MF2)
. For the pair terms one uses the bounds

(10.3)

\displaystyle\left|\frac{x^{p-1}-y^{p-1}}{x-y}\right|(1+|x|)(1+|y|)

\displaystyle\leq C_{p}(1+|x|^{p}+|y|^{p}),

(10.4)

\displaystyle\left|\frac{x^{p-1}+y^{p-1}}{x+y}\right|(1+x)(1+y)

\displaystyle\leq C_{p}(1+x^{p}+y^{p}),\qquad x,y\geq 0,

and for the short-root term in type
B_{N}
one uses

(10.5)

\displaystyle x^{p-2}k(t,x)

\displaystyle\leq C_{p,T}(1+x^{p}),\qquad x\geq 0.

Letting the localization level tend to infinity and applying Gronwall’s lemma yields

(10.6)

\displaystyle\sup_{N\geq 1}\sup_{0\leq t\leq T}\mathbf{E}\left\langle\mu_{t}^{(N)},|x|^{p}\right\rangle

\displaystyle<\infty,\qquad p=2,4,8.

We now derive the empirical-measure decomposition. Fix

f\in D
. Itô’s formula, together with the symmetrization of
the pair roots, gives

\displaystyle\left\langle\mu_{t}^{(N)},f\right\rangle

\displaystyle=\left\langle\mu_{0}^{(N)},f\right\rangle+M_{f}^{(N)}(t)+A_{\sigma,f}^{(N)}(t)+\int_{0}^{t}\left\langle\mu_{s}^{(N)},b(s,\cdot)f^{\prime}\right\rangle\,ds

\displaystyle\quad+\eta_{\mathfrak{r}}^{0}\int_{0}^{t}\left\langle\mu_{s}^{(N)},H_{f}(s,\cdot)\right\rangle\,ds

(10.7)

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{t}\int_{E^{2}}D_{f}(x,y)k(s,x,y)\,\mu_{s}^{(N)}(dx)\mu_{s}^{(N)}(dy)\,ds+R_{f}^{(N)}(t),

where

(10.8)

\displaystyle M_{f}^{(N)}(t)

\displaystyle:=\frac{1}{N\sqrt{N}}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\int_{0}^{t}f^{\prime}\bigl(Y_{i}^{(N)}(s)\bigr)\sigma\bigl(s,Y_{i}^{(N)}(s)\bigr)\,d\widetilde{B}_{i}(s),

(10.9)

\displaystyle A_{\sigma,f}^{(N)}(t)

\displaystyle:=\frac{1}{2N}\int_{0}^{t}\left\langle\mu_{s}^{(N)},\sigma^{2}(s,\cdot)f^{\prime\prime}\right\rangle\,ds.

Here
\widetilde{B}_{i}=B_{i}
in types
A_{N-1}
and
B_{N}
; in type
D_{N}
the possible sign coming
from the map
x\mapsto|x|
is absorbed into the Brownian motion.

The term
R_{f}^{(N)}
contains the diagonal correction

(10.10)

\displaystyle R_{f,\mathrm{diag}}^{(N)}(t)

\displaystyle:=-\frac{1}{2N}\int_{0}^{t}\left\langle\mu_{s}^{(N)},D_{f}(x,x)k(s,x,x)\right\rangle\,ds.

This correction appears because the finite particle sums run over
i\neq j
, whereas
the product measure
\mu_{s}^{(N)}(dx)\mu_{s}^{(N)}(dy)
contains the diagonal. In type

D_{N}
,
R_{f}^{(N)}
may also contain the harmless one-coordinate correction produced by
passing from the possibly signed chamber coordinate to its absolute value in the
one-particle drift. This additional term is of order
N^{-1}
and is estimated in
the same way as the diagonal correction.

By the growth assumptions in
(MF2)
and the moment bound
(
10.6
),

(10.11)

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}\left[\sup_{0\leq t\leq T}|M_{f}^{(N)}(t)|^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C_{f,T}}{N^{2}}\int_{0}^{T}\mathbf{E}\left\langle\mu_{s}^{(N)},1+|x|^{2}\right\rangle\,ds\longrightarrow 0,

(10.12)

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}\left[\sup_{0\leq t\leq T}|A_{\sigma,f}^{(N)}(t)|\right]

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C_{f,T}}{N}\int_{0}^{T}\mathbf{E}\left\langle\mu_{s}^{(N)},1+|x|^{2}\right\rangle\,ds\longrightarrow 0,

(10.13)

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}\left[\sup_{0\leq t\leq T}|R_{f}^{(N)}(t)|\right]

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C_{f,T}}{N}\int_{0}^{T}\mathbf{E}\left\langle\mu_{s}^{(N)},1+|x|^{2}\right\rangle\,ds\longrightarrow 0.

The same decomposition, the Burkholder-Davis-Gundy inequality, Hölder’s
inequality, and the eighth-moment estimate in (
10.6
) imply the
increment estimate

(10.14)

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}\left|\left\langle\mu_{t}^{(N)},f\right\rangle-\left\langle\mu_{s}^{(N)},f\right\rangle\right|^{4}

\displaystyle\leq C_{f,T}|t-s|^{2},\qquad 0\leq s\leq t\leq T.

Together with (
10.6
), this gives compact containment and tightness
of the laws of
(\mu_{t}^{(N)})_{0\leq t\leq T}
in

C([0,T],\mathcal{P}(E))
. For instance, one may use a countable
convergence-determining family contained in
\mathcal{D}_{\mathfrak{r}}
and apply the
standard tightness criterion for measure-valued continuous processes.

It remains to identify subsequential limits. Let

(\mu^{(N_{m})})_{m\geq 1}
be a weakly convergent subsequence. By Skorokhod’s
representation theorem we may assume that

(10.15)

\displaystyle\mu^{(N_{m})}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\mu\qquad\text{a.s. in }C([0,T],\mathcal{P}(E)).

The initial term converges by
(MF3)
. The martingale term, the finite
diffusion term, and the remainder vanish by
(
10.11
)-(
10.13
). Thus no
diffusion term survives in the limiting equation.

The ordinary drift term passes to the limit by continuity of
b
, truncation on
compact sets, and the uniform moment bound (
10.6
). The wall term
is handled in the same way. Indeed, for
f\in\mathcal{D}_{B}
,

(10.16)

\displaystyle|H_{f}(t,x)|

\displaystyle\leq C_{f,T}(1+x),\qquad x\geq 0,\quad 0\leq t\leq T.

For the pair interaction, note first that
D_{f}^{\mathfrak{r}}
is bounded on

E_{\mathfrak{r}}^{2}
. The bound for
D_{f}^{-}
follows from the mean-value theorem, and
for
D_{f}^{+}
we use
f^{\prime}(0)=0
:

(10.17)

\displaystyle|D_{f}^{+}(x,y)|

\displaystyle\leq\|f^{\prime\prime}\|_{\infty},\qquad x,y\geq 0.

Consequently, by the growth assumption on
k
,

(10.18)

\displaystyle\left|D_{f}(x,y)k(s,x,y)\right|

\displaystyle\leq C_{f,T}(1+|x|)(1+|y|),\qquad 0\leq s\leq T.

The second moments are uniformly bounded by (
10.6
). Hence
truncation and weak convergence imply convergence of the interaction terms.

Passing to the limit in (
10.7
) gives
(
2.28
). Therefore every subsequential weak limit solves the
limiting equation. If (
2.28
) has a unique solution in

C([0,T],\mathcal{P}(E))
, then all subsequential limits coincide, and
therefore the full sequence converges weakly to that deterministic solution. Since
the limit is deterministic, this convergence is equivalent to convergence in
probability.
∎

11.
Examples

11.1.
Dyson-Cépa-Lépingle systems with logarithmic root potentials

We begin with the non-degenerate logarithmic repulsion case. This is the root-system analogue of the electrostatic particle systems of Cépa-Lépingle
[
8
]
. Let
R
be one of the classical root systems considered in this paper, let
R_{+}
be the corresponding set of positive roots, and let
\overline{C_{+}}
be the closed Weyl chamber.

Let

m_{\alpha}:[0,\infty)\longrightarrow(0,\infty),\qquad\alpha\in R_{+},

be continuous functions. We do not require the multiplicities
m_{\alpha}
to be constant on Weyl-orbits, although this is the usual radial Dunkl specialization. Define, for
x\in C_{+}
,

h_{t}(x):=\prod_{\alpha\in R_{+}}\langle x,\alpha\rangle^{m_{\alpha}(t)}.

Then

\nabla\log h_{t}(x)=\sum_{\alpha\in R_{+}}m_{\alpha}(t)\frac{\alpha}{\langle x,\alpha\rangle}.

We consider the chamber-valued equation

(11.1)

dX(t)=\sigma(t,X(t))\,dB(t)+b(t,X(t))\,dt+\nabla\log h_{t}(X(t))\,dt,\qquad X(t)\in\overline{C_{+}},

where
\sigma
is diagonal, with entries
\sigma_{1},\ldots,\sigma_{N}
. Equivalently, (
11.1
) is the root-system SDE
(
1.2
) with

k_{\alpha}(t,x)=m_{\alpha}(t),\qquad\alpha\in R_{+}.

Thus the singular coefficients are strictly positive on every root wall.

Proposition 15

(Existence and uniqueness for logarithmic root-barrier systems)
.

Assume
(C1)
and
(C2)
. Then, for every initial condition

x_{0}\in\overline{C_{+}}
, equation (
11.1
) has a weak non-sticky solution in
\overline{C_{+}}
, up to its lifetime. If, in addition, the growth condition
(G1)
holds, then the solution is global.

Assume moreover that one of the following uniqueness regimes holds:

(i)

the Yamada-Watanabe alternative
(a)
in
Assumption
(U1)
holds, together with
Assumption
(U2)
;

(ii)

the locally Lipschitz alternative
(b)
in
Assumption
(U1)
holds, together with
Assumption
(U2’)
.

Then (
11.1
) has pathwise uniqueness in the non-sticky class. Consequently, by the Yamada-Watanabe principle, it has a strong solution, unique in the non-sticky class, up to its lifetime. Under
(G1)
, the strong solution is global.

Proof.

Since
k_{\alpha}(t,x)=m_{\alpha}(t)>0
, Assumption
(A1)
holds. The
weak existence statement and the integrability of the singular drifts therefore
follow from Theorem
1
. Moreover, since the corresponding
singular coefficient is strictly positive on each wall, the finiteness of the
singular integrals implies the zero-occupation property

\int_{0}^{t}\mathbf{1}_{\{\langle X(s),\alpha\rangle=0\}}\,ds=0,\qquad\alpha\in R_{+},\qquad t<\zeta^{X},\qquad\text{a.s.}

Thus the solution is non-sticky. If Assumption
(G1)
holds, global
existence follows from Theorem
5
.

It remains to check the uniqueness assumptions. Let

G(t,x):=\nabla\log h_{t}(x)=\sum_{\alpha\in R_{+}}m_{\alpha}(t)\frac{\alpha}{\langle x,\alpha\rangle},\qquad x\in C_{+}.

We first verify Assumption
(U3)
. Let
x,y\in C_{+}
and set

d_{i}:=x_{i}-y_{i},\qquad s_{i}:=\operatorname{sgn}(d_{i}),\qquad i=1,\ldots,N.

For a positive root
\alpha
, the corresponding contribution to the left-hand side
of (
2.19
) is

m_{\alpha}(t)\langle s,\alpha\rangle\left(\frac{1}{\langle x,\alpha\rangle}-\frac{1}{\langle y,\alpha\rangle}\right)=-m_{\alpha}(t)\frac{\langle s,\alpha\rangle\langle d,\alpha\rangle}{\langle x,\alpha\rangle\langle y,\alpha\rangle}.

The denominators are positive since
x,y\in C_{+}
, and
m_{\alpha}(t)>0
.

For the classical root systems considered here, the positive roots have the forms

e_{i}-e_{j}
,
e_{i}+e_{j}
,
e_{i}
, or
2e_{i}
, depending on the type. For these roots,

\langle s,\alpha\rangle\langle d,\alpha\rangle\geq 0.

Indeed,

(s_{i}-s_{j})(d_{i}-d_{j})\geq 0

for
\alpha=e_{i}-e_{j}
, by monotonicity of the sign function,

(s_{i}+s_{j})(d_{i}+d_{j})\geq 0

for
\alpha=e_{i}+e_{j}
, and

s_{i}d_{i}\geq 0

for
\alpha=e_{i}
or
2e_{i}
. Hence each root contribution is nonpositive. Summing
over
\alpha\in R_{+}
gives

\sum_{i=1}^{N}\operatorname{sgn}(x_{i}-y_{i})\bigl(G_{i}(t,x)-G_{i}(t,y)\bigr)\leq 0.

Thus Assumption
(U3)
holds. Therefore the first uniqueness regime in
the statement gives pathwise uniqueness by
Theorem
6
.

For the second regime, note that
\log h_{t}
is concave on
C_{+}
, because it is a
positive linear combination of the concave functions

x\mapsto\log\langle x,\alpha\rangle
. Therefore its gradient is dissipative:

\bigl\langle x-y,\nabla\log h_{t}(x)-\nabla\log h_{t}(y)\bigr\rangle\leq 0,\qquad x,y\in C_{+}.

Equivalently, Assumption
(U3’)
holds for the singular force.
The second uniqueness regime then follows again from
Theorem
6
. Strong existence and uniqueness
follow from Theorem
7
.
∎

Remark 9

(More general logarithmic potentials)
.

The preceding proposition can be extended beyond the pure root-barrier product.
Suppose that, for each
t
, a positive differentiable function
h_{t}
on
C_{+}

satisfies

(11.2)

\nabla\log h_{t}(x)=\sum_{\alpha\in R_{+}}\alpha\,\frac{k_{\alpha}(t,x)}{\langle x,\alpha\rangle},\qquad x\in C_{+},

where each
k_{\alpha}
extends continuously to
[0,\infty)\times\overline{C_{+}}
, is
nonnegative on
[0,\infty)\times\overline{C_{+}}
, and is strictly positive on the wall

\{\langle x,\alpha\rangle=0\}
. Then, under the same standing assumptions on

\sigma
and
b
, the existence conclusion of
Proposition
15
remains valid.

If, in addition,
\log h_{t}
is concave for each
t
, then the singular force

\nabla\log h_{t}
satisfies Assumption
(U3’)
. Hence, under
the locally Lipschitz diffusion alternative (b) in
Assumption
(U1)
and the Euclidean one-sided Lipschitz condition
Assumption
(U2’)
, the same pathwise uniqueness and strong
existence conclusion holds.

For the coordinatewise Yamada-Watanabe regime, one needs instead the

\ell^{1}
-dissipativity condition Assumption
(U3)
. This condition holds
for the pure classical root-barrier product treated in
Proposition
15
, as shown above. For a more
general logarithmic potential satisfying
(
11.2
), concavity alone gives the
Euclidean dissipativity condition, but does not by itself imply the

\ell^{1}
-dissipativity condition.

In type
A_{N-1}
, we have

R_{+}=\{e_{i}-e_{j}:\ 1\leq i<j\leq N\},\qquad\overline{C_{+}}=\{x_{1}\geq\cdots\geq x_{N}\}.

If
m_{e_{i}-e_{j}}(t)\equiv\beta/2
, then

h_{t}(x)=\prod_{1\leq i<j\leq N}(x_{i}-x_{j})^{\beta/2},

and (
11.1
) becomes

dX_{i}(t)=\sigma_{i}(t,X(t))\,dB_{i}(t)+b_{i}(t,X(t))\,dt+\frac{\beta}{2}\sum_{j\neq i}\frac{dt}{X_{i}(t)-X_{j}(t)},\qquad\beta>0.

Thus the Dyson-Cépa-Lépingle electrostatic repulsion is recovered, but with the diagonal martingale distortion allowed by Assumption
(C2)
. If
\sigma_{i}(t,x)=\sigma(t,x_{i})
and
b_{i}(t,x)=b(t,x_{i})
, then the Yamada-Watanabe regime applies under the usual one-dimensional modulus condition on
\sigma
and the one-sided Lipschitz condition on
b
, because the logarithmic Dyson force satisfies Assumption
(U3)
. If the coefficients depend on the full configuration and are locally Lipschitz, the Euclidean regime applies instead, using the concavity of
\log h_{t}
.

11.2.
Generalized particle systems with weak repulsion

We next consider type
A_{N-1}
pair-repulsion systems of the form studied by Graczyk and Małecki
[
22
]
, under the boundary-variance compatibility required by the present root-system construction. The point of the present formulation is that we do not impose the additional assumptions which force particles never to collide. Weak repulsion may allow collisions, but the singular drift is still integrable and, under the non-sticking condition below, the solutions spend zero Lebesgue time on collision hyperplanes.

Let
R_{+}=\{e_{i}-e_{j}:\ 1\leq i<j\leq N\}
and
\overline{C_{+}}=\{x_{1}\geq x_{2}\geq\cdots\geq x_{N}\}
. For
1\leq i\leq N
, let
\sigma_{i},b_{i}:[0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
be continuous. For
1\leq i<j\leq N
, let
H_{ij}:[0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}^{2}\to[0,\infty)
be continuous. We set

H_{ji}(t,y,x):=H_{ij}(t,x,y),\qquad i<j,

and define the type
A_{N-1}
root coefficients by

(11.3)

k_{e_{i}-e_{j}}(t,x):=H_{ij}(t,x_{i},x_{j}),\qquad 1\leq i<j\leq N.

The corresponding ordered-particle equation is

(11.4)

dX_{i}(t)=\sigma_{i}(t,X_{i}(t))\,dB_{i}(t)+b_{i}(t,X_{i}(t))\,dt+\sum_{j\neq i}\frac{H_{ij}(t,X_{i}(t),X_{j}(t))}{X_{i}(t)-X_{j}(t)}\,dt.

When collisions are possible, the degenerate construction first gives the indicator version

(11.5)

dX_{i}(t)=\sigma_{i}(t,X_{i}(t))\,dB_{i}(t)+b_{i}(t,X_{i}(t))\,dt+\sum_{j\neq i}\frac{H_{ij}(t,X_{i}(t),X_{j}(t))}{X_{i}(t)-X_{j}(t)}\mathbf{1}_{\{X_{i}(t)\neq X_{j}(t)\}}\,dt.

We impose the following compatibility condition on the diffusion coefficients:

(11.6)

\sigma_{i}^{2}(t,z)=\sigma_{j}^{2}(t,z),\qquad t\geq 0,\quad z\in\mathbb{R},\quad 1\leq i,j\leq N.

Remark 10

(Diffusion compatibility at collision walls)
.

Condition (
11.6
) is not a non-collision assumption. It is the type
A_{N-1}
form of Assumption
(C2)
. When two particles collide, the two rank positions become indistinguishable on the chamber boundary. The invariant-polynomial construction therefore requires the martingale variances attached to these two positions to agree on the collision wall. Equivalently, the diffusion coefficient must not distinguish between two particles which occupy the same spatial position. This condition is automatic in the exchangeable case

\sigma_{i}(t,x)=\sigma(t,x),\qquad i=1,\ldots,N,

and more generally whenever

\sigma_{i}^{2}(t,z)=\sigma_{j}^{2}(t,z)\qquad\text{for all }i,j,t,z.

It does exclude rank-dependent models for which two particles have different variances at the same collision point. Such models belong to the broader Graczyk-Małecki framework when their assumptions preventing collisions are imposed, but they are not covered by the present root-system theorem from arbitrary boundary initial data.

The normal dominance condition becomes the following pairwise estimate. For every
T,R>0
there exist constants
\varepsilon=\varepsilon(T,R)>0
and
\gamma=\gamma(T,R)>0
such that, for all
1\leq i<j\leq N
,

(11.7)

|x-y|\leq\varepsilon,\quad|x|\vee|y|\leq R,\quad 0\leq t\leq T\quad\Longrightarrow\quad H_{ij}(t,x,y)\geq\gamma\bigl(\sigma_{i}^{2}(t,x)+\sigma_{j}^{2}(t,y)\bigr).

Here
\gamma
is only required to be strictly positive. No threshold such as
\gamma\geq 1
is imposed. Indeed, for the root
\alpha=e_{i}-e_{j}
,

\sum_{\ell=1}^{N}\alpha_{\ell}^{2}\sigma_{\ell}^{2}(t,x)=\sigma_{i}^{2}(t,x_{i})+\sigma_{j}^{2}(t,x_{j}),

so (
11.7
) is exactly Assumption
(D1)
near the collision wall
x_{i}=x_{j}
.

It remains to formulate the face conditions. Let

I=\{a,\ldots,a+k-1\},\qquad k\geq 2,

be a maximal collision block on a relatively open face, and let
x_{p}=z
,
p\in I
. For a cut
r=1,\ldots,k-1
, put

I_{L}:=\{a,\ldots,a+r-1\},\qquad I_{R}:=\{a+r,\ldots,a+k-1\},

and use the detector

u_{I,r}:=\frac{1}{r}\sum_{p\in I_{L}}e_{p}-\frac{1}{k-r}\sum_{p\in I_{R}}e_{p}.

The quadratic-variation density of this detector is

q_{I,r}(t,x):=\sum_{p\in I_{L}}\frac{\sigma_{p}^{2}(t,z)}{r^{2}}+\sum_{p\in I_{R}}\frac{\sigma_{p}^{2}(t,z)}{(k-r)^{2}}.

The corresponding regular detector drift on the face is

\displaystyle B_{I,r}(t,x)={}

\displaystyle\frac{1}{r}\sum_{p\in I_{L}}b_{p}(t,z)-\frac{1}{k-r}\sum_{p\in I_{R}}b_{p}(t,z)+\sum_{\ell<a}\frac{\displaystyle\frac{1}{k-r}\sum_{p\in I_{R}}H_{\ell p}(t,x_{\ell},z)-\frac{1}{r}\sum_{p\in I_{L}}H_{\ell p}(t,x_{\ell},z)}{x_{\ell}-z}

\displaystyle+\sum_{q>a+k-1}\frac{\displaystyle\frac{1}{r}\sum_{p\in I_{L}}H_{pq}(t,z,x_{q})-\frac{1}{k-r}\sum_{p\in I_{R}}H_{pq}(t,z,x_{q})}{z-x_{q}}.

The denominators are strictly positive on the relatively open face, because the block
I
is maximal.

We assume the following inward condition:

(11.8)

B_{I,r}(t,x)\geq 0

for every collision block
I
, every cut
1\leq r\leq|I|-1
, every
t\geq 0
, and every point
x
of the corresponding face. This is precisely Assumption
(D2)
, written in block coordinates, when the detector family contains the cut detectors
u_{I,r}
.

Finally, to obtain the equation without indicators, we impose the block escape condition at fully degenerate collision points:

(11.9)

\max_{I,r}q_{I,r}(t,x)=0\quad\Longrightarrow\quad\max_{I,r}B_{I,r}(t,x)>0

on every collision face. The maxima are taken over all collision blocks of the face and all their cuts. This is the concrete type
A_{N-1}
form of Assumption
(D3)
. It says that if all detector martingale variations vanish at a degenerate collision point, then some deterministic detector drift must push the process away from the face.

Proposition 16

(Weak and strong solutions for Generalized particle systems)
.

Assume (
11.6
), (
11.7
), and (
11.8
). Then, for every initial condition
x_{0}\in\overline{C_{+}}
, there exists a weak solution of the indicator equation (
11.5
), up to its lifetime. Moreover, all singular integrals are finite on compact subintervals of the lifetime.

If, in addition, the block escape condition (
11.9
) holds, then there exists a weak solution of the genuine equation (
11.4
), and this solution is non-sticky.

Assume moreover that one of the two uniqueness regimes in Theorem
6
holds. Then the solution is pathwise unique in the non-sticky class. Consequently, by Theorem
7
, the equation has a strong solution, unique in the non-sticky class, up to its lifetime. If the radial growth condition
(G1)
holds, then the solution is global.

Proof.

The coefficient definitions (
11.3
) put (
11.5
) into the type
A_{N-1}
root-system form. The compatibility condition (
11.6
) gives Assumption
(C2)
, and (
11.7
) gives Assumption
(D1)
. The block condition (
11.8
) is exactly Assumption
(D2)
for the cut detector family. Therefore Theorem
3
gives a weak solution of the indicator equation.

If (
11.9
) holds, then Assumption
(D3)
is satisfied. Theorem
4
therefore removes the indicator and gives a non-sticky weak solution of (
11.4
). Pathwise uniqueness and strong existence follow from Theorems
6
and
7
. The final assertion follows from Theorem
5
.
∎

Remark 11

(Comparison with the non-collision assumptions)
.

The estimate (
11.7
) is much weaker than the usual conditions used to exclude collisions. It only says that, close to the diagonal, the repulsion coefficient dominates the normal martingale variation by some positive constant. Thus beta-Dyson type systems with small beta are included. Collisions may occur, but the singular drift remains integrable and the collision hyperplanes have zero Lebesgue occupation time once the non-sticking condition is verified.

Remark 12

(The exchangeable case)
.

A particularly important special case is

\sigma_{i}(t,x)=\sigma(t,x),\qquad b_{i}(t,x)=b(t,x),\qquad H_{ij}(t,x,y)=H(t,x,y),

where
H(t,x,y)=H(t,y,x)
. Then the compatibility condition
(
11.6
) is automatic, and the face sign condition
(
11.8
) holds with equality:

B_{I,r}(t,x)=0

for every maximal block and every cut. Hence
Theorem
3
gives a weak solution of the indicator
equation under the dominance estimate

H(t,x,y)\geq\gamma\bigl(\sigma^{2}(t,x)+\sigma^{2}(t,y)\bigr)

near the diagonal, for any
\gamma>0
.

The block escape condition is more delicate. For a block at level
z
,

q_{I,r}(t,x)=\sigma^{2}(t,z)\left(\frac{1}{r}+\frac{1}{k-r}\right),

whereas
B_{I,r}(t,x)=0
by symmetry. Thus the degenerate non-sticking condition is
automatic at collision levels with
\sigma(t,z)>0
, but it fails at symmetric
collision levels with
\sigma(t,z)=0
unless an additional asymmetric inward drift
is present.

If
H(t,z,z)>0
on the relevant diagonal, then this wall belongs to the strictly
positive repulsion regime and one should apply Theorem
1
rather
than the degenerate indicator theorem. The genuinely delicate case is

\sigma(t,z)=0,\qquad H(t,z,z)=0.

At such a fully degenerate collision point the detector quadratic variation
vanishes, the singular repulsion coefficient vanishes, and the symmetric regular
detector drift is zero. The indicator equation may therefore admit sticky block
solutions.

Remark 13

(Uniqueness conditions in pair coordinates)
.

In the Yamada-Watanabe regime, the rootwise monotonicity assumption

(U3)
becomes the following condition: for every pair
i<j
,

\bigl((x_{i}-x_{j})-(y_{i}-y_{j})\bigr)\left(\frac{H_{ij}(t,x_{i},x_{j})}{x_{i}-x_{j}}-\frac{H_{ij}(t,y_{i},y_{j})}{y_{i}-y_{j}}\right)\leq 0

whenever
x_{i}>x_{j}
and
y_{i}>y_{j}
. In the locally Lipschitz regime, this rootwise
condition may be replaced by the global dissipativity assumption

(U3’)
for the full singular force.

Proposition 17

(Mean-field limit for exchangeable pair-repulsion systems)
.

Assume that

\sigma_{i}(t,x)=\sigma(t,x),\qquad b_{i}(t,x)=b(t,x),\qquad H_{ij}(t,x,y)=H(t,x,y)=H(t,y,x),

where
\sigma,b
and
H
are continuous. For each
N
, consider

(11.10)

dX_{i}^{(N)}(t)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}\sigma\bigl(t,X_{i}^{(N)}(t)\bigr)\,dB_{i}(t)+b\bigl(t,X_{i}^{(N)}(t)\bigr)\,dt+\frac{1}{N}\sum_{j\neq i}\frac{H\bigl(t,X_{i}^{(N)}(t),X_{j}^{(N)}(t)\bigr)}{X_{i}^{(N)}(t)-X_{j}^{(N)}(t)}\,dt.

Suppose that the finite systems satisfy the existence and integrability assumptions
of Theorem
8
; for example, this follows from
Proposition
16
whenever the corresponding dominance, face sign,
and non-sticking conditions hold. Assume also the coefficient growth and initial
moment hypotheses
(MF2)
-
(MF3)
. Then the empirical
measure processes

\mu_{t}^{(N)}=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\delta_{X_{i}^{(N)}(t)}

are tight in
C([0,T],\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}))
for every
T>0
. Every subsequential limit

\mu=(\mu_{t})_{0\leq t\leq T}
satisfies, for every
f\in C_{b}^{2}(\mathbb{R})
,

(11.11)

\displaystyle\langle\mu_{t},f\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle\mu_{0},f\rangle+\int_{0}^{t}\langle\mu_{s},b(s,\cdot)f^{\prime}\rangle\,ds+\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}D_{f}^{-}(x,y)H(s,x,y)\,\mu_{s}(dx)\mu_{s}(dy)\,ds.

If the limiting equation (
11.11
) has a unique solution
in
C([0,T],\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}))
, then the whole sequence
\mu^{(N)}
converges to that
solution.

Proof.

This is the type
A_{N-1}
specialization of Theorem
8
, with

k(t,x,y)=H(t,x,y).

The factor
1/2
appears because the unordered pair interaction is symmetrized in
the empirical-measure equation.
∎

Remark 14

(Relation with random matrix flow limits)
.

Proposition
17
is in the same spirit as the
empirical-measure limits for general random matrix flows studied by
Małecki-Pérez
[
33
]
. The present statement is
formulated directly at the particle level and allows a time-dependent pair kernel

H(t,x,y)
, not necessarily coming from a matrix flow. It also applies to weakly
repulsive finite systems constructed by the degenerate theorem, provided the
non-sticking condition is verified. For Wishart-type high-dimensional limits, see
also Song-Yao-Yuan
[
43
]
.

11.3.
Squared Bessel and Wishart particle systems

We now discuss squared Bessel particle systems and beta-Wishart systems. This is the main family of examples in which the repulsion coefficient vanishes at a boundary point together with the normal martingale variation. The degenerate theory is therefore needed, and the formulation with indicators is the natural starting point.

The nonnegative
B_{N}
formulation

Let

\overline{C_{+}}=\{x_{1}\geq x_{2}\geq\cdots\geq x_{N}\geq 0\}.

Fix parameters

\beta>0,\qquad\delta\in\mathbb{R},\qquad\gamma\in\mathbb{R}.

The beta-Wishart particle system, written in our decreasing order convention, is

(11.12)

d\lambda_{i}(t)=2\sqrt{\lambda_{i}(t)}\,dB_{i}(t)+\left(\delta-2\gamma\lambda_{i}(t)+\beta\sum_{j\neq i}\frac{\lambda_{i}(t)+\lambda_{j}(t)}{\lambda_{i}(t)-\lambda_{j}(t)}\right)dt,\qquad i=1,\ldots,N.

For
\gamma=0
this is the multivariate squared Bessel particle system. For
\gamma>0
it is the mean-reverting Laguerre, or beta-Wishart, version. The sign convention for
\gamma
is chosen so that positive
\gamma
gives mean reversion.

Although (
11.12
) contains only the singular differences
\lambda_{i}-\lambda_{j}
, it is useful to embed it into the root system
B_{N}
,

R_{+}=\{e_{i}:\ 1\leq i\leq N\}\cup\{e_{i}-e_{j},\ e_{i}+e_{j}:\ 1\leq i<j\leq N\}.

The roots
e_{i}
and
e_{i}+e_{j}
are auxiliary: they rewrite continuous drift contributions in a form suitable for the degenerate root-system theorem. Choose constants

\theta_{0}>0,\qquad\theta_{+}>0,

and define, for
x\in\overline{C_{+}}_{B}
,

(11.13)

\displaystyle\sigma_{i}(t,x)

\displaystyle:={2\sqrt{x_{i}}},

(11.14)

\displaystyle k_{e_{i}-e_{j}}(t,x)

\displaystyle:=\beta(x_{i}+x_{j}),\qquad 1\leq i<j\leq N,

(11.15)

\displaystyle k_{e_{i}+e_{j}}(t,x)

\displaystyle:=\theta_{+}(x_{i}+x_{j}),\qquad 1\leq i<j\leq N,

(11.16)

\displaystyle k_{e_{i}}(t,x)

\displaystyle:=\theta_{0}x_{i},\qquad 1\leq i\leq N,

(11.17)

\displaystyle b_{i}(t,x)

\displaystyle:=\delta-2\gamma x_{i}-\theta_{0}-(N-1)\theta_{+}.

At interior points of
\overline{C_{+}}_{B}
, the corresponding
B_{N}
root-system equation is exactly (
11.12
). Indeed,

\frac{k_{e_{i}}(t,x)}{x_{i}}=\theta_{0},\qquad\frac{k_{e_{i}+e_{j}}(t,x)}{x_{i}+x_{j}}=\theta_{+},

and these continuous contributions cancel the constants subtracted in (
11.17
).

Let us verify the degenerate assumptions. Assumption
(C2)
holds. For roots
e_{i}-e_{j}
,

\sigma_{i}^{2}(t,x)-\sigma_{j}^{2}(t,x)=4(x_{i}-x_{j}),

which vanishes on the collision wall
x_{i}=x_{j}
. For roots
e_{i}+e_{j}
, the wall
x_{i}+x_{j}=0
can occur in
\overline{C_{+}}_{B}
only when
x_{i}=x_{j}=0
, and therefore
\sigma_{i}^{2}(t,x)-\sigma_{j}^{2}(t,x)
again vanishes there. The normal dominance condition holds globally, since

(11.18)

\displaystyle\sum_{\ell=1}^{N}(e_{i}-e_{j})_{\ell}^{2}\sigma_{\ell}^{2}(t,x)

\displaystyle=4x_{i}+4x_{j},

(11.19)

\displaystyle\sum_{\ell=1}^{N}(e_{i}+e_{j})_{\ell}^{2}\sigma_{\ell}^{2}(t,x)

\displaystyle=4x_{i}+4x_{j},

(11.20)

\displaystyle\sum_{\ell=1}^{N}(e_{i})_{\ell}^{2}\sigma_{\ell}^{2}(t,x)

\displaystyle=4x_{i}.

Thus

k_{\alpha}(t,x)\geq c_{*}\sum_{\ell=1}^{N}\alpha_{\ell}^{2}\sigma_{\ell}^{2}(t,x),\qquad\alpha\in R_{+},\qquad c_{*}=\frac{1}{4}\min\{\beta,\theta_{+},\theta_{0}\}>0.

Positive collision blocks away from zero are locally symmetric in the colliding coordinates. For cut detectors inside such a block the regular detector drift vanishes by symmetry, and the detector quadratic variation is strictly positive because the common collision level is positive. Hence these faces do not cause sticking.

The only delicate faces are zero blocks. Let

J_{r}:=\{N-r+1,\ldots,N\},\qquad 1\leq r\leq N,

be a zero block, so that
x_{j}=0
for
j\in J_{r}
and, if
r<N
,
x_{N-r}>0
. For the block-center detector

\bar{u}_{J_{r}}:=\frac{1}{r}\sum_{j\in J_{r}}e_{j},

the detector quadratic variation is

(11.21)

q_{\bar{u}_{J_{r}}}(t,x)=\sum_{j\in J_{r}}\frac{1}{r^{2}}\sigma_{j}^{2}(t,x)=0

on the zero-block face. The regular detector drift is

(11.22)

B_{J_{r}}(t,x):=B_{S,\bar{u}_{J_{r}}}(t,x)=\delta-\theta_{0}-(N-r)\beta-(r-1)\theta_{+}.

Indeed, the ordinary drift contribution equals
\delta-\theta_{0}-(N-1)\theta_{+}
, while each outside positive coordinate contributes
\theta_{+}-\beta
. The cut detectors inside the zero block have zero regular detector drift by symmetry. Thus the block-center detector determines whether the zero block is pushed away from the boundary.

Set

(11.23)

C_{r}:=\delta-\theta_{0}-(N-r)\beta-(r-1)\theta_{+},\qquad r=1,\ldots,N.

The face sign condition at zero blocks is
C_{r}\geq 0
for
r=1,\ldots,N
, while the non-sticking condition is verified if

(11.24)

C_{r}>0,\qquad r=1,\ldots,N.

A simple sufficient choice is

(11.25)

0<\theta_{+}\leq\beta,\qquad 0<\theta_{0}<\delta-\beta(N-1).

Consequently, the condition

(11.26)

\delta>\beta(N-1)

allows us to choose
\theta_{0},\theta_{+}
so that all inequalities in (
11.24
) hold.

We also verify the uniqueness assumptions. The diffusion coefficient satisfies the Yamada-Watanabe condition, because

|2\sqrt{u}-2\sqrt{v}|^{2}\leq 4|u-v|,\qquad u,v\geq 0.

The regular drift satisfies Assumption
(U2)
, since

\sum_{i=1}^{N}\operatorname{sgn}(x_{i}-y_{i})\bigl(b_{i}(t,x)-b_{i}(t,y)\bigr)=-2\gamma\sum_{i=1}^{N}|x_{i}-y_{i}|\leq 2|\gamma|\sum_{i=1}^{N}|x_{i}-y_{i}|.

It remains to check Assumption
(U3)
. The auxiliary
e_{i}
and
e_{i}+e_{j}
terms give only constants in the interior force and therefore disappear in
G(t,x)-G(t,y)
. For the Wishart pair force define

\Psi(u,v):=\frac{u+v}{u-v},\qquad u>v\geq 0.

Then
\Psi
is non-increasing in
u
and non-decreasing in
v
. Hence, for
d_{i}=x_{i}-y_{i}
and
s_{i}=\operatorname{sgn}(d_{i})
, the contribution of a pair
i<j
to the
\ell^{1}
singular-force estimate is

\beta(s_{i}-s_{j})\bigl(\Psi(x_{i},x_{j})-\Psi(y_{i},y_{j})\bigr)\leq 0.

Summing over pairs gives Assumption
(U3)
.

Proposition 18

(Beta-Wishart particles in type
B_{N}
)
.

Let
\beta>0
,
\gamma\in\mathbb{R}
, and assume

\delta>\beta(N-1).

Then, for every initial condition
\lambda(0)\in\overline{C_{+}}_{B}
, the system (
11.12
) has a global strong solution. The solution is non-sticky, namely

\int_{0}^{t}\mathbf{1}_{\{\lambda_{i}(s)=\lambda_{j}(s)\}}\,ds=0,\qquad 1\leq i<j\leq N,\qquad t<\zeta^{\lambda},\qquad\text{a.s.},

and

\int_{0}^{t}\mathbf{1}_{\{\lambda_{i}(s)=0\}}\,ds=0,\qquad 1\leq i\leq N,\qquad t<\zeta^{\lambda},\qquad\text{a.s.}

Moreover, pathwise uniqueness and uniqueness in law hold in the class of non-sticky solutions.

Proof.

Choose
\theta_{0},\theta_{+}
satisfying (
11.25
). The coefficient family (
11.13
)-(
11.17
) satisfies Assumptions
(C1)
,
(C2)
,
(D1)
,
(D2)
, and
(D3)
, by the computations above. Theorem
4
gives a weak non-sticky solution of the genuine equation (
11.12
). The Yamada-Watanabe uniqueness regime, with Assumptions
(U1)
(a),
(U2)
, and
(U3)
, holds by the preceding paragraph. Therefore Theorems
6
and
7
give strong existence and uniqueness in the non-sticky class. Finally, the radial growth condition
(G1)
is immediate from the linear growth of
b
, the estimate
\sigma_{i}^{2}(t,x)=4x_{i}
, and the linear growth of the coefficients
k_{\alpha}
on
\overline{C_{+}}_{B}
; hence Theorem
5
gives global existence.
∎

Remark 15

(Relation with Jourdain-Kahn)
.

In the increasing-order convention used by Jourdain-Kahn
[
26
]
, their parameter
\alpha
corresponds to our
\delta
. They prove strong existence and pathwise uniqueness up to the first multiple collision at zero and give sharp conditions for such collisions to be avoided. In particular, for a zero block of size
r
, the relevant threshold is

r\bigl(\delta-(N-r)\beta\bigr)\geq 2.

For
r=2
this is equivalent to

\delta-\beta(N-1)\geq 1-\beta.

Thus, when
0<\beta<1
, the interval

0<\delta-\beta(N-1)<1-\beta

is a regime in which a multiple zero collision may occur. Proposition
18
nevertheless gives a global continuation through such contacts, and it is pathwise unique in the non-sticky class. This is the sense in which the present theorem improves the continuation result available from the collision-avoidance approach. The comparison should not be overstated: Jourdain-Kahn give a sharper analysis of which collisions occur or are avoided, while the present root-system argument gives a robust non-sticky continuation once the boundary is reached.

Remark 16

(Auxiliary coefficients)
.

The constants
\theta_{0}
and
\theta_{+}
are auxiliary. They do not appear in the final SDE (
11.12
). They are introduced only to embed the equation into the degenerate
B_{N}
root-system framework and to verify the boundary conditions at zero blocks. Different choices satisfying (
11.24
) lead to the same particle system (
11.12
).

A type
A_{N-1}
absolute-value analogue

There is also a natural type
A_{N-1}
analogue in which the state space is the whole ordered line,

\overline{C_{+}}=\{x_{1}\geq x_{2}\geq\cdots\geq x_{N}\},

and the square-root coefficient is interpreted with an absolute value. Consider

(11.27)

dX_{i}(t)=2\sqrt{|X_{i}(t)|}\,dB_{i}(t)+\bigl(\delta-2\gamma X_{i}(t)\bigr)dt+\beta\sum_{j\neq i}\frac{|X_{i}(t)|+|X_{j}(t)|}{X_{i}(t)-X_{j}(t)}\mathbf{1}_{\{X_{i}(t)\neq X_{j}(t)\}}\,dt.

This is not the nonnegative beta-Wishart system. It is a type
A_{N-1}
ordered-particle model with squared-Bessel type noise and an interaction kernel that degenerates only when colliding particles meet at zero.

In root-system notation, (
11.27
) corresponds to

R_{+}=\{e_{i}-e_{j}:\ 1\leq i<j\leq N\},\qquad\sigma_{i}(t,x)=2\sqrt{|x_{i}|},\qquad b_{i}(t,x)=\delta-2\gamma x_{i},

and

(11.28)

k_{e_{i}-e_{j}}(t,x)=\beta\bigl(|x_{i}|+|x_{j}|\bigr),\qquad 1\leq i<j\leq N.

The compatibility condition Assumption
(C2)
holds because, on the wall
x_{i}=x_{j}
, one has

\sigma_{i}^{2}(t,x)=4|x_{i}|=4|x_{j}|=\sigma_{j}^{2}(t,x).

The normal dominance condition holds globally:

k_{e_{i}-e_{j}}(t,x)=\frac{\beta}{4}\bigl(\sigma_{i}^{2}(t,x)+\sigma_{j}^{2}(t,x)\bigr).

The face sign condition holds with equality. Indeed, on a collision block all colliding coordinates have the same value
z
, hence the regular drift
\delta-2\gamma z
is the same for all particles in the block, and the interactions with particles outside the block are symmetric. Therefore every cut detector has regular detector drift equal to zero.

The uniqueness assumptions are also satisfied in the Yamada-Watanabe regime. The diffusion coefficient obeys

|2\sqrt{|u|}-2\sqrt{|v|}|^{2}\leq 4|u-v|,\qquad u,v\in\mathbb{R},

and the regular drift satisfies Assumption
(U2)
. For the singular force, set

\Psi(u,v)=\frac{|u|+|v|}{u-v},\qquad u>v.

This function is non-increasing in
u
and non-decreasing in
v
on the region
u>v
. Hence the pairwise sign argument used above gives Assumption
(U3)
.

Proposition 19

(Type
A_{N-1}
absolute-value squared Bessel particles)
.

Let
\beta>0
and
\delta,\gamma\in\mathbb{R}
. For every initial condition
X(0)\in\overline{C_{+}}_{A}
, the indicator equation (
11.27
) has a global weak solution in
\overline{C_{+}}_{A}
, and all singular drift integrals are finite on compact time intervals. Moreover, pathwise uniqueness holds among non-sticky solutions, that is, among solutions satisfying

\int_{0}^{t}\mathbf{1}_{\{X_{i}(s)=X_{j}(s)\}}\,ds=0,\qquad 1\leq i<j\leq N,\qquad t<\zeta^{X},\qquad\text{a.s.}

Proof.

The verification above gives Assumptions
(C1)
,
(C2)
,
(D1)
, and
(D2)
. Therefore Theorem
3
gives a weak solution of the indicator equation. The radial growth condition
(G1)
follows from the estimates

\sigma_{i}^{2}(t,x)=4|x_{i}|,\qquad|b_{i}(t,x)|\leq C(1+|x_{i}|),\qquad k_{e_{i}-e_{j}}(t,x)\leq\beta(|x_{i}|+|x_{j}|),

so Theorem
5
gives global existence. The Yamada-Watanabe uniqueness assumptions
(U1)
(a),
(U2)
, and
(U3)
have also been checked above. Theorem
6
therefore gives pathwise uniqueness in the non-sticky class.
∎

Remark 17

(Zero blocks and possible sticky behavior)
.

For the type
A_{N-1}
absolute-value model, the fully degenerate collision faces occur when a collision block sits at level zero. At such a face all cut-detector quadratic variations vanish, and by symmetry the regular detector drifts also vanish. Thus Assumption
(D3)
is not obtained from the present general criterion, and Proposition
19
deliberately states existence for the indicator equation only, together with uniqueness once the class is restricted to non-sticky solutions.

A detailed study of which zero blocks are sticky, how non-sticky continuations can be selected, and how the indicator can be removed would require additional arguments. This model should be approachable by methods similar in spirit to the analysis of squared Bessel particle systems in Graczyk-Małecki
[
23
]
.

11.4.
Noncolliding Brownian bridges with time-dependent drift

One advantage of allowing the coefficients to depend on time is that bridge-type
particle systems can be treated directly at the level of SDEs. In the work of
Izumi-Katori
[
25
]
, such processes are introduced through
transition probability densities. Here we record a simple representative example:
the type
A_{N-1}
noncolliding Brownian bridge with terminal collapse at the origin.

Let

\overline{C_{+}}=\{x_{1}\geq x_{2}\geq\cdots\geq x_{N}\}

and fix
T>0
. We consider, for
0\leq t<T
, the system

(11.29)

dX_{i}(t)=dB_{i}(t)+\sum_{j\neq i}\frac{dt}{X_{i}(t)-X_{j}(t)}-\frac{X_{i}(t)}{T-t}\,dt,\qquad i=1,\ldots,N.

The last term is the Brownian bridge drift. The singular drift is the usual
logarithmic repulsion in type
A_{N-1}
.

Proposition 20

(Type
A_{N-1}
noncolliding Brownian bridge)
.

For every
x_{0}\in\overline{C_{+}}
, equation (
11.29
) has a strong
solution on
[0,T)
. This solution is pathwise unique in the non-sticky class.
In particular, the singular drift integrals are finite on compact subintervals of

[0,T)
.

Proof.

We write (
11.29
) in the type
A_{N-1}
root-system form with

R_{+}=\{e_{i}-e_{j}:\ 1\leq i<j\leq N\},\qquad\sigma_{i}(t,x)=1,\qquad k_{e_{i}-e_{j}}(t,x)=1,

and

b_{i}(t,x)=-\frac{x_{i}}{T-t}.

Fix
T_{0}<T
. On
[0,T_{0}]
the coefficients are continuous, and
Assumption
(A1)
holds because
k_{\alpha}\equiv 1
for every positive
root. Hence Theorem
1
gives weak existence and finiteness of the
singular drift integrals on compact time intervals. In particular, the solution is non-sticky.

The uniqueness assumptions are also immediate on
[0,T_{0}]
. The diffusion
coefficient is constant, the regular drift is locally Lipschitz in
x
, and the
singular force is

G(x)=\sum_{1\leq i<j\leq N}\frac{e_{i}-e_{j}}{x_{i}-x_{j}}=\nabla\log h_{A}(x),\qquad h_{A}(x)=\prod_{1\leq i<j\leq N}(x_{i}-x_{j}).

Since
\log h_{A}
is concave on the chamber interior, Assumption
(U3’)

holds. Therefore Theorems
6
and

7
give strong existence and pathwise uniqueness
in the non-sticky class on
[0,T_{0}]
.

Finally,

\sum_{i=1}^{N}x_{i}b_{i}(t,x)=-\frac{|x|^{2}}{T-t}\leq 0

on
[0,T_{0}]
, and the remaining terms in Assumption
(G1)
are
constant. Hence Theorem
5
excludes explosion before
T_{0}
.
Since
T_{0}<T
is arbitrary, the solution is defined on the whole interval
[0,T)
.
∎

Remark 18

(Other Katori bridge systems)
.

The same SDE-first viewpoint applies to several other finite-duration
noncolliding systems considered by Izumi-Katori
[
25
]
. For
example, in type
B_{N}
one obtains bridge versions of noncolliding
three-dimensional Bessel processes by adding the time-dependent bridge drift to
the usual type
B_{N}
logarithmic repulsion:

\displaystyle dY_{i}(t)

\displaystyle=dB_{i}(t)+\frac{dt}{Y_{i}(t)}+\sum_{j\neq i}\left(\frac{1}{Y_{i}(t)-Y_{j}(t)}+\frac{1}{Y_{i}(t)+Y_{j}(t)}\right)dt-\frac{Y_{i}(t)}{T-t}\,dt,

(11.30)

\displaystyle\hskip 227.62204pti=1,\ldots,N.

This is again covered by the strictly positive repulsion regime on every compact
subinterval of
[0,T)
. Thus our framework allows one to start from the SDE and
derive existence and uniqueness directly, instead of first constructing the process
from transition densities.

Remark 19

(Affine Katori bridges)
.

Katori also studies bridge systems associated with affine root systems and
Macdonald denominators
[
29
]
. These models should not
all be described as ordinary interval models with the same boundary behavior. The
affine type
A_{N-1}
model is periodic and lives on a circle. The remaining
reduced affine types live on an interval
[0,\pi r]
: the types
B_{N}
,

C_{N}^{\vee}
, and
BC_{N}
have absorbing boundary at
0
and reflecting boundary at

\pi r
; the types
B_{N}^{\vee}
and
C_{N}
have absorbing boundary at both endpoints;
and the type
D_{N}
has reflecting boundary at both endpoints.

The absorbing endpoints correspond to singular logarithmic drifts which prevent
hitting of the endpoint, while reflecting endpoints require local-time reflection
terms. Therefore these affine models are not literally examples of the present
finite conical Weyl-chamber theorem. Nevertheless, our techniques should apply to
the corresponding interior SDEs up to the first hitting time of an affine boundary
wall. A full treatment of the affine Katori systems would require a mild extension
of the present arguments from finite Weyl chambers to affine alcoves, and in the
reflecting cases also the inclusion of reflection terms at the endpoint walls.

12.

The author would like to express his gratitude to Piotr Graczyk and Makoto Katori for many important and fruitful discussions on particle systems, which eventually led to significant improvements of the paper. The author is also very grateful to Prof. Michael Voit for pointing out the missing arguments in the construction strategy presented in
[
22
]
.

13.
Appendix

Proposition 21
.

For fixed
k=1,\ldots,N+1
and
x=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{N})
let

V_{N}^{k}(x)=\det\begin{bmatrix}1&x_{1}&\ldots&x_{1}^{k-1}&x_{1}^{k+1}&\ldots&x_{1}^{N}\\
\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
1&x_{N}&\ldots&x_{N}^{k-1}&x_{N}^{k+1}&\ldots&x_{N}^{N}\end{bmatrix}\/.

Then

(13.1)

V_{N}^{k}(x)=\left(\prod_{1\leq i<j\leq N}(x_{j}-x_{i})\right)e_{N-k+1}(x)\/.

Proof.

Note that the matrix appearing in the definition of
V_{N}^{k}(x)
is the Vandermonde matrix, where we removed the
k
-th column and add the last column of the form
[x_{1}^{N},\ldots,x_{N}^{N}]^{T}
. Obviously
V_{N}^{N+1}(x)
is just the Vandermonde determinant and we simply have

V_{N}^{N+1}(x)=\left(\prod_{1\leq i<j\leq N}(x_{j}-x_{i})\right)=\left(\prod_{1\leq i<j\leq N}(x_{j}-x_{i})\right)e_{0}(x)

and (
13.1
) holds in this case. From the other side we also simply get that

V_{N}^{1}(x)=x_{1}\cdot\ldots\cdot x_{N}\left(\prod_{1\leq i<j\leq N}(x_{j}-x_{i})\right)=\left(\prod_{1\leq i<j\leq N}(x_{j}-x_{i})\right)e_{N}(x)\/,

which also agrees with (
13.1
). Finally, for
k=1,\ldots,N+1
, by simple manipulations on the rows of the matrix, we can get

V_{N}^{k}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{N})=\prod_{i=1}^{N-1}(x_{N}-x_{i})\left[V_{N-1}^{k-1}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{N-1})+x_{N}V_{N-1}^{k}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{N-1})\right]\/.

Thus the induction with respect to
N
based on the above-given formula and the relation
e_{k}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{N})=e_{k}^{\overline{x_{N}}}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{N})+x_{N}e_{k-1}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{N-1})
, leads to (
13.1
).
∎

Proposition 22
.

Consider
p=(p_{1},\ldots,p_{N}):C_{+}^{A_{N-1}}\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}^{N}
defined as

p_{k}(x)=\frac{1}{k}\sum_{i=1}^{N}x_{i}^{k}\/,\quad k=1,\ldots,N\/.

Then the inverse function
x(p)=(x_{1}(p),\ldots,x_{N}(p)):p[C_{+}^{A_{N-1}}]\longrightarrow C_{+}^{A_{N-1}}
is smooth and

(13.2)

\dfrac{\partial x_{i}}{\partial p_{k}}=(-1)^{k+1}\dfrac{e_{N-k}^{\overline{x_{i}}}(x)}{e_{N-1}(\mathcal{B}_{i})}

Proof.

For every
x\in C_{+}^{A_{N-1}}
and
i,j=1,\ldots,N
we simply have

\dfrac{\partial x_{i}}{\partial x_{j}}=\sum_{k=1}^{N}\dfrac{\partial x_{i}}{\partial p_{k}}\dfrac{\partial p_{k}}{\partial x_{j}}=\sum_{k=1}^{N}x_{j}^{k-1}\dfrac{\partial x_{i}}{\partial p_{k}}=\delta_{i,j}\/,

where
\delta_{i,j}=1
for
i=j
and
\delta_{i,j}=0
otherwise. Consequently, for fixed
i
we obtain system of linear equations with variables
(\frac{\partial x_{i}}{\partial p_{1}},\ldots,\frac{\partial x_{i}}{\partial p_{k}})
and the corresponding coefficient matrix is just the Vandermonde matrix
[x_{j}^{k-1}]_{j,k=1,\ldots,N}
. Since the Vandermonde determinant is non-zero on
C_{+}^{A_{N-1}}
, we obtain by the Cramer’s rule that

\dfrac{\partial x_{i}}{\partial p_{k}}=(-1)^{i+k}\dfrac{V^{i,k}_{N-1}(x)}{e_{N(N-1)/2}(\mathcal{B})}\/,

where
V^{i,k}_{N-1}(x)
is the determinant of
(N-1)\times(N-1)
-matrix obtain from the Vandermonde matrix by removing the
i
-th row and
k
-th column. Using (
13.1
) from Proposition
21
ends the proof of (
13.2
).
∎

In the next proposition we show that, at boundary points where the relevant root (or isolated pair of roots) is separated from its neighbors, the corresponding inverse coordinate (respectively the pair-sum) depends smoothly on the power sums. As a consequence, the first and higher derivatives with respect to
(p_{1},\dots,p_{N})
admit continuous extensions at such points. In the proposition below we remove the normalization
1/k
in the definition of the power sums
p_{k}
to make the proof easier to read. Obviously, this has no effect on the presented result, i.e. the claim is exactly the same for the power sums with normalization
1/k
. Moreover, it is also true for any other ordering of the variables
x_{1},\ldots,x_{N}
and corresponding
F
function.

Proposition 23
.

Let
\Omega=\{x=(x_{1},\dots,x_{N})\in\mathbb{R}^{N}:\ x_{1}<\cdots<x_{N}\}
and define the mapping
F:\overline{\Omega}\to\mathbb{R}^{N}
by

F(x)=(p_{1},\dots,p_{N}),\qquad p_{k}=\sum_{j=1}^{N}x_{j}^{k},\quad k=1,\dots,N.

Fix
x^{*}\in\overline{\Omega}
and set
p^{*}=F(x^{*})
.

(i)

Assume that for some
i\in\{1,\dots,N\}
one has

x^{*}_{i-1}<x^{*}_{i}<x^{*}_{i+1},

with the obvious interpretation when
i=1
or
i=N
. Then there exists a neighborhood
U\subset\mathbb{R}^{N}
of
p^{*}
and a unique function

\varphi:U\to\mathbb{R}

of class
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}
such that for all
p\in U\cap F(\overline{\Omega})
we have
\varphi(p)=x_{i}(p)
, where

x(p)=(x_{1}(p)\leq\cdots\leq x_{N}(p))
denotes the ordered root-vector inverse of
F
on
F(\overline{\Omega})
.
In particular, the coordinate
p\mapsto x_{i}(p)
is
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}
at
p^{*}
.

(ii)

Assume that for some
i\in\{1,\dots,N-1\}
one has the
isolation condition

(13.3)

x^{*}_{i-1}<x^{*}_{i}\leq x^{*}_{i+1}<x^{*}_{i+2},

with the obvious endpoint modifications when
i=1
or
i=N-1
. Define

S(p):=x_{i}(p)+x_{i+1}(p).

Then there exists a neighborhood
U\subset\mathbb{R}^{N}
of
p^{*}
and a unique function

\psi:U\to\mathbb{R}

of class
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}
such that

\psi(p)=S(p)\qquad\text{for all }p\in U\cap F(\overline{\Omega}).

In particular, the sum
p\mapsto x_{i}(p)+x_{i+1}(p)
is
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}
at
p^{*}
.

Proof.

For
p=(p_{1},\dots,p_{N})
, denote by
e_{0}(p):=1
and
e_{1}(p),\dots,e_{N}(p)
the elementary symmetric polynomials, expressed via Newton identities

k\,e_{k}(p)=\sum_{j=1}^{k}(-1)^{j-1}e_{k-j}(p)\,p_{j},\qquad k=1,\dots,N.

Each
e_{k}
is a polynomial in
(p_{1},\dots,p_{k})
and, in particular,
p\mapsto e_{k}(p)
is
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}
. Define the monic polynomial

(13.4)

q_{p}(t):=t^{N}-e_{1}(p)t^{N-1}+e_{2}(p)t^{N-2}-\cdots+(-1)^{N}e_{N}(p).

If
p=F(x)
for some
x\in\overline{\Omega}
, then the multiset of roots of
q_{p}
(counted with multiplicity) is precisely
\{x_{1},\dots,x_{N}\}
.

We begin with the proof of the first claim. We have
q_{p^{*}}(x_{i}^{*})=0
. Since
x^{*}\in\overline{\Omega}
is non-decreasing and
x^{*}_{i-1}<x^{*}_{i}<x^{*}_{i+1}
, the value
x_{i}^{*}
is distinct
from every other coordinate
x_{m}^{*}
for
m\neq i
. Hence

q^{\prime}_{p^{*}}(x_{i}^{*})=\prod_{m\neq i}(x_{i}^{*}-x_{m}^{*})\neq 0,

so
t=x_{i}^{*}
is a simple root of
q_{p^{*}}
. Consider
\Phi(p,t):=q_{p}(t)
. This map is
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}
in
(p,t)
and satisfies

\Phi(p^{*},x_{i}^{*})=0,\qquad\partial_{t}\Phi(p^{*},x_{i}^{*})=q^{\prime}_{p^{*}}(x_{i}^{*})\neq 0.

By the implicit function theorem, there exist neighborhoods
U
of
p^{*}
and
I
of
x_{i}^{*}
, and a unique
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}
function

\varphi:U\to I
such that

q_{p}(\varphi(p))=0\quad\text{for all }p\in U,\qquad\varphi(p^{*})=x_{i}^{*}.

Because
x_{i}^{*}
is strictly separated from
x^{*}_{i-1}
and
x^{*}_{i+1}
, we can choose
\varepsilon>0
and (if necessary) shrink
U
so that for every

p\in U\cap F(\overline{\Omega})
, the polynomial
q_{p}
has exactly one real root in the interval

\bigl(x^{*}_{i-1}+\varepsilon,\ x^{*}_{i+1}-\varepsilon\bigr)
, and this root is
\varphi(p)
. But the ordered root vector
x(p)
has
x_{i}(p)
as the unique root
in that interval, hence
\varphi(p)=x_{i}(p)
for all
p\in U\cap F(\overline{\Omega})
.

To deal with the second part let

S^{*}:=x_{i}^{*}+x_{i+1}^{*},\qquad P^{*}:=x_{i}^{*}x_{i+1}^{*},

and define the monic quadratic polynomial

Q^{*}(t):=t^{2}-S^{*}t+P^{*}=(t-x_{i}^{*})(t-x_{i+1}^{*}).

Let
R^{*}(t)
be the monic polynomial of degree
N-2
defined by the factorization

q_{p^{*}}(t)=Q^{*}(t)\,R^{*}(t).

The isolation condition (
13.3
) implies that the roots of
Q^{*}
are separated from all other roots of
q_{p^{*}}
, hence
Q^{*}
and
R^{*}
have
no common root. In particular,

(13.5)

\gcd(Q^{*},R^{*})=1.

Define
u\in\mathbb{R}^{N}
by

u=(S,P,r_{1},\dots,r_{N-2}),

where
S,P\in\mathbb{R}
and

R(t):=t^{N-2}+r_{1}t^{N-3}+\cdots+r_{N-2}

is monic. Introduce the map
H:\mathbb{R}^{N}\times\mathbb{R}^{N}\to\mathbb{R}^{N}
by

H(u,p):=\mathrm{coeff}\Big((t^{2}-St+P)R(t)-q_{p}(t)\Big),

where
\mathrm{coeff}
denotes the coefficient vector (e.g. from
t^{N-1}
down to
t^{0}
). Then
H
is
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}
in
(u,p)
, and for

u^{*}:=(S^{*},P^{*},r_{1}^{*},\dots,r_{N-2}^{*})

(the coefficients of
Q^{*}
and
R^{*}
) we have
H(u^{*},p^{*})=0
.

We claim that the Jacobian
\partial_{u}H(u^{*},p^{*})
is invertible. Indeed, suppose
\delta u=(\delta S,\delta P,\delta r_{1},\dots,\delta r_{N-2})
satisfies

\partial_{u}H(u^{*},p^{*})\,\delta u=0
. Differentiating the product
(t^{2}-St+P)R(t)
in the
u
-variables gives the polynomial identity

(13.6)

\delta Q(t)\,R^{*}(t)+Q^{*}(t)\,\delta R(t)\equiv 0,

where

\delta Q(t):=-\delta S\,t+\delta P,\qquad\delta R(t):=\delta r_{1}t^{N-3}+\cdots+\delta r_{N-2}.

Evaluating (
13.6
) at any root
\alpha
of
Q^{*}
yields
\delta Q(\alpha)R^{*}(\alpha)=0
. By (
13.5
), one has

R^{*}(\alpha)\neq 0
for each root
\alpha
of
Q^{*}
, hence
\delta Q(\alpha)=0
at both roots of
Q^{*}
. If
x_{i}^{*}<x_{i+1}^{*}
, these are two distinct points,
so
\delta Q\equiv 0
(since
\deg\delta Q\leq 1
). If
x_{i}^{*}=x_{i+1}^{*}=a
, then evaluating (
13.6
) at
t=a
gives
\delta Q(a)=0
, and
differentiating (
13.6
) in
t
and evaluating at
t=a
gives
\delta Q^{\prime}(a)=0
(since
Q^{*}(a)=Q^{*\prime}(a)=0
for a double root),
hence again
\delta Q\equiv 0
. In either case,
\delta S=\delta P=0
, and then (
13.6
) implies
Q^{*}(t)\delta R(t)\equiv 0
, so

\delta R\equiv 0
. Therefore
\delta u=0
, proving
\ker(\partial_{u}H(u^{*},p^{*}))=\{0\}
and hence
\partial_{u}H(u^{*},p^{*})
is invertible.

By the implicit function theorem, there exist a neighborhood
U\subset\mathbb{R}^{N}
of
p^{*}
and a unique
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}
map

u(p)=(S(p),P(p),r_{1}(p),\dots,r_{N-2}(p))

defined on
U
such that
u(p^{*})=u^{*}
and

(t^{2}-S(p)t+P(p))\,R_{p}(t)=q_{p}(t)\qquad\text{for all }p\in U,

where
R_{p}(t):=t^{N-2}+r_{1}(p)t^{N-3}+\cdots+r_{N-2}(p)
. In particular,
S(p)
is
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}
on
U
.

Finally, by (
13.3
) and continuity of roots with respect to coefficients, after possibly shrinking
U
the polynomial
q_{p}
has exactly two roots
(counted with multiplicity) in a small neighborhood of
\{x_{i}^{*},x_{i+1}^{*}\}
, and these two roots are precisely the roots of the quadratic factor

Q_{p}(t):=t^{2}-S(p)t+P(p)
. For
p\in U\cap F(\overline{\Omega})
, these are exactly the ordered roots
x_{i}(p)
and
x_{i+1}(p)
, hence

S(p)=x_{i}(p)+x_{i+1}(p)\qquad\text{for all }p\in U\cap F(\overline{\Omega}).

Setting
\psi:=S
concludes the proof of (
ii
).
∎

Proposition 24
.

Let
(\alpha,\beta)\in P_{+}
and
\gamma=s^{+}_{\alpha}(\beta)
. Then
s^{+}_{\beta}(\alpha)=\gamma
and
s^{+}_{\alpha}(\gamma)=\beta
,
s^{+}_{\beta}(\gamma)=\alpha
. Consequently, if
\delta\in\{\alpha,\beta,\gamma\}
, then
\{s^{+}_{\delta}(\alpha),s^{+}_{\delta}(\beta),s^{+}_{\delta}(\gamma)\}=\{\alpha,\beta,\gamma\}
.

Proof.

Since
(\alpha,\beta)\in P_{+}
gives
\left<\alpha,\beta\right>=\pm 1
and
|\alpha|^{2}=|\beta|^{2}=2
we have

s^{+}_{\beta}(\alpha)=\pm\left(\alpha-\frac{2\left<\alpha,\beta\right>\beta}{|\beta|^{2}}\right)=\pm\left<\alpha,\beta\right>\left(\left<\alpha,\beta\right>\alpha-\beta\right)=\mp\left<\alpha,\beta\right>\left(\beta-\frac{2\left<\alpha,\beta\right>\alpha}{|\alpha|^{2}}\right)=s^{+}_{\alpha}(\beta).

We also have

\displaystyle\left<\alpha,\gamma\right>

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\langle\alpha,s^{+}_{\alpha}(\beta)\rangle=\pm\left(\left<\alpha,\beta\right>-\frac{2\left<\alpha,\beta\right>|\alpha|^{2}}{|\alpha|^{2}}\right)=\mp\left<\alpha,\beta\right>,

\displaystyle\left<\beta,\gamma\right>

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\pm\left(|\beta|^{2}-\frac{2\left<\alpha,\beta\right>^{2}}{|\alpha|^{2}}\right)=\pm 1

and consequently

\displaystyle s^{+}_{\alpha}(\gamma)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\pm\left(\gamma-\frac{2\left<\alpha,\gamma\right>\alpha}{|\alpha|^{2}}\right)=\pm\left(\beta-\frac{2\left<\alpha,\beta\right>\alpha}{|\alpha|^{2}}+\frac{2\left<\alpha,\beta\right>\alpha}{|\alpha|^{2}}\right)=\beta,

\displaystyle s^{+}_{\beta}(\gamma)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\pm\left(\gamma-\frac{2\left<\beta,\gamma\right>\beta}{|\beta|^{2}}\right)=\pm\left(\beta-\frac{2\left<\alpha,\beta\right>\alpha}{|\alpha|^{2}}-\beta\right)=\alpha.

∎

Proposition 25
.

Let
(\alpha,\beta)\in P_{+}
and
\delta\in R_{+}
such that
|\delta|^{2}=2
. Then

(a)

If
\left<\alpha,\delta\right>=\left<\beta,\delta\right>=0
, then
\langle s^{+}_{\alpha}(\beta),\delta\rangle=0
.

(b)

If
|\left<\alpha,\delta\right>\left<\beta,\delta\right>|=1
, then
\langle s^{+}_{\alpha}(\beta),\delta\rangle=0
.

(c)

If
|\left<\alpha,\delta\right>\left<\beta,\delta\right>|=2
, then
|\langle s^{+}_{\alpha}(\beta),\delta\rangle|=1
.

Proof.

The proof follows from the definition of
s^{+}_{\alpha}(\beta)
, which together with the assumptions that
|\alpha|=|\beta|=|\delta|=\sqrt{2}
give

\langle s^{+}_{\alpha}(\beta),\delta\rangle=\pm(\left<\beta,\delta\right>-\frac{2\left<\alpha,\beta\right>\left<\alpha,\delta\right>}{|\alpha|^{2}})=\pm(\left<\beta,\delta\right>-\left<\alpha,\beta\right>\left<\alpha,\delta\right>)\/.

The first part follows directly. To justify the second claim, note that since all
\left<\beta,\delta\right>
,
\left<\alpha,\beta\right>
,
\left<\alpha,\delta\right>
are equal to
\pm 1
, then
\alpha,\beta,\delta\in R_{+}^{i}
for some
i=1,\ldots,N
and

\left<\beta,\delta\right>-\left<\alpha,\beta\right>\left<\alpha,\delta\right>=\beta_{i}\delta_{i}-\alpha_{i}^{2}\beta_{i}\delta_{i}=0.

Finally,
|\left<\alpha,\delta\right>\left<\beta,\delta\right>|=2
means that
\delta=\alpha
or
\delta=\beta
. In both cases, we obtain

\displaystyle|\langle s^{+}_{\alpha}(\beta),\delta\rangle|

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle|\langle s^{+}_{\alpha}(\beta),\alpha\rangle|=\left|\left<\alpha,\beta\right>-\frac{2\left<\alpha,\beta\right>|\alpha|^{2}}{|\alpha|^{2}}\right|=|\left<\alpha,\beta\right>|=1\/,\quad\textrm{ for }\delta=\alpha,

\displaystyle|\langle s^{+}_{\alpha}(\beta),\delta\rangle|

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle|\langle s^{+}_{\alpha}(\beta),\beta\rangle|=\left||\beta|^{2}-\frac{2\left<\alpha,\beta\right>^{2}}{|\alpha|^{2}}\right|=2-1=1\/,\quad\textrm{ for }\delta=\beta.

∎
